# Blood and Ice

Working title. Series: The Keeper's Blood.

# Chapter 1: Prologue

The sea lies flat for three days.

No wind. No chop. No white on the water. Only heat, haze, and a silence so complete we all begin to speak softly without knowing why.

On the third day, the lookout sees a ship.

She comes out of the glare bow-first, sails hanging dead from the yards, hull black against the bright water. No wake trails her. No hand moves at her rail. She drifts as if the sea has forgotten to finish sinking her.

I am sixteen, which is young enough to be sent where older men prefer not to go. I stand at the rail beside the mate and watch the stranger slide nearer.

I have been aboard the Mercy Bell for seven months and still own almost nothing: a spare shirt patched at both elbows, a bone comb with three teeth missing, and a copper button my sister pressed into my hand before I sailed. I keep the button tied inside my sleeve where dice and debt cannot find it. When the dead ship appears, my fingers go to that hidden lump without asking me.

"Salvage," Vell says.

He says it like prayer.

The word moves through the crew. Men straighten. Fear loosens under the smell of profit. A dead ship can mean rot, sickness, pirates, or a curse. It can also mean cargo with no owner left to argue.

Rusk kisses two fingers and touches the little charm sewn to his cap. Tam grins too widely and says a dead captain spends no wages. No one laughs, but several men look toward Vell, and Vell looks at the dead ship as if it has already made him richer.

I try to imagine what my sister would say if she stood beside me. She was twelve when I left and clever enough to see lies before they reached the mouth. She would say, Coll, nothing comes free across water. Then she would steal a sweet from the galley and ask whether curses paid in silver.

The thought almost makes me smile.

Vell chooses the boat himself. He takes Rusk the bosun, Tam with the steady hands, and me because someone has to bail.

We row across water smooth enough to hold both ships like mirrors. Heat sits on my neck. Sweat runs down my back. I have crossed these warm latitudes twice and have never once been cold on them.

I am cold before we reach the hull.

It comes in a breath over the water. At first it feels like shade. Then cellar damp. Then winter.

I look down and see my own breath smoke.

No one speaks for three strokes of the oars.

Rusk spits over the side. "Cold tide."

It is stupid. Everyone knows it. Everyone is grateful he has said anything.

We make the chains and climb.

The deck is neat.

That is the first wrong sign. I have seen wrecks. A wreck tells its story openly: snapped rope, broken spars, blood dried where men have crawled, cargo spilled like guts. This ship tells nothing. Lines are coiled. A cup stands upright on a hatch cover, with a skin of frozen tea inside it. Frost clings to the brass near the wheel.

The captain's door stands open. Inside, a chair has been pushed back from a small table. A quill lies beside an uncorked inkpot, the ink black and frozen solid. On the table sit three coins stacked with a careful thumb's-width between each one, as if the captain has been counting them when the cold reaches him and means to come back after a breath.

I hate that thought.

No. I hate it.

Panic leaves mess. Storm leaves mess. Men leave mess when death enters a room.

This ship has been arranged by patience.

The second wrong sign is the galley.

The stove is cold, but not long cold. A pan sits on the iron with onions inside, white and hard as little stones. Four bowls wait beside it. A spoon lies on the floor below one of the benches. Someone dropped it and never bent to pick it up.

Tam lifts one bowl and makes a choked sound.

Inside, a child's milk teeth are set in a ring.

No child sits at the table. No small bones lie near the stove. Only the teeth, cleaned and placed with care, as if saved for counting.

Vell takes the bowl from Tam and sets it down with a hand that is not steady.

"Cargo," he says.

No one answers.

The third wrong sign is the smell.

There should be rot. In this heat, dead men swell and burst and turn the air sweet enough to taste.

There is no rot.

Only cold. Clean, dry, impossible cold.

We find the crew amidships, huddled in the lee of the longboat.

For one merciful second I think they are sleeping. Then Vell turns one over with his boot.

The man comes up light.

Too light.

He has a man's face. Stubble on the chin. A burst vein in one eye. A scar through the left brow. But the skin has drawn tight as gray paper, and beneath it there is nothing. No weight. No blood. No meat. A husk in the shape of a sailor.

Tam begins to pray under his breath.

I touch my sister's button through my sleeve. The copper is cold. It should have warmed under my skin. It has not.

Vell stares at the bodies and then toward the forward hatch. Greed fights fear in his face. Greed wins by a finger.

"Hold may still be full," he says.

I hear the sound from below.

Not a footstep. Not a voice.

A draw.

Long and slow. Like someone pulling breath through a pipe. Like thirst given lungs.

Cold pours up through the hatch.

I feel it reach me before I understand. Not against my skin. Inside my chest. The warmth in me leans toward the dark below as if water has found a drain.

"Off," I say.

The others do not move.

"Get off," I say, louder. "Get off the ship."

The dead shape rises from the hold.

It has the shape of a man the way famine has the shape of a season. Tall. Pale. Dry. Skin pulled tight over lines that should hold life and do not. Its mouth is not a mouth. Its eyes are two holes where cold comes out.

It makes no cry. No roar. Only that slow drinking sound.

Rusk stands nearest.

The pale figure turns its face to him.

The bosun stops breathing. Frost crawls up his beard. His skin goes gray. His body empties while he stands there, warmth and blood and life drawn out through no wound I can see. Rusk folds to the deck as lightly as sailcloth.

His cap falls beside him. The little charm on it cracks with a sound no louder than a flea snapping between nails.

Tam goes over the rail. I hear the splash, then nothing.

Vell takes one step toward the hatch.

Even then, his eyes flick below. Cargo. Wages. A year's worth of wanting.

The cold takes him with his hand still half-raised toward profit.

I run.

The rail is gone from me.

The rope is gone from me.

No.

That is how men lie to themselves afterward, making memory clean because terror shames them.

I remember enough.

I remember slipping on frost and striking my chin hard enough to bite blood from my tongue. I remember the pale figure turning, slow because it does not need haste. I remember a sound leaving my own throat, thin as a gull's cry. I remember Tam in the water below, one hand raised for help and then curling in on itself as frost flowers open across his wet sleeve.

I remember leaving him.

I hit the boat wrong and crack my ribs on the thwart. The oars are gone. I slash the painter with Tam's knife because my own hands will not untie the knot. The rope parts. The dead ship slides away, or I slide away from it. The water between us looks flat enough to walk.

I lie in the bilge with no oars, no courage worth naming, and no memory I will want to keep.

My breath smokes for a long time.

Then, slowly, under the killing southern sun, it stops.

My own ship finds me near dusk. They ask what I saw. I tell them. The captain writes fever in the log because ships' logs have room for fever and pirates and storm, but not for thirsting dead on cold ships in warm seas.

They do not turn back for the salvage.

By morning the stranger has vanished into haze.

Three weeks later, Tam's brother comes aboard in Lys and asks why his kin has no grave. I try to answer. The words are in me, but men do not like impossible answers when grief has made them hungry. Tam's brother strikes me once, hard enough to split my lip. I let him. It is the least false answer either of us can give.

Years later, an old woman in a northern port tells me the cold ones do not hunt the warm out of hunger. Hunger is too clean a word.

They hunt from memory.

They were warm once.

They remember.

# Chapter 2: The Shore

Heat wakes me.

Not sunlight. Not fire. Heat with weight. It lies over my face and chest like a wet hand, presses the breath back into me, and fills my mouth with the taste of rot.

For a while I lie still.

Stillness is not courage. Stillness is what you do when you wake somewhere wrong and do not yet know what watches.

Mud holds my back. Roots press through my shirt. Something small crawls over my wrist, stops at the blood there, and begins to feed.

I open my eyes.

Green.

It hangs above me in layers so thick the sky is only a white glare behind leaves. Vines cross the branches like ropes on a ship. Water drips everywhere, slow and patient, though no rain is falling. The whole place breathes. I can hear it. Insects whining. Leaves ticking. Far off, something coughs once and goes quiet.

I remember a deck.

I remember hands.

I remember the sound men make when they do not care whether a boy lives, only whether he can still be sold.

Then the memories break apart. The sea has taken some. Pain has taken the rest.

I make an inventory because inventory keeps fear from owning the whole room.

Right hand: slow, but it closes.

Left hand: better.

Teeth: three gone.

Mouth: blood.

Ribs: bruised, maybe cracked.

Knife: none.

That matters most.

I roll onto my side and vomit seawater into the mud. It comes up bitter and warm. Ants find it before I am done spitting. They come in a black thread from under a leaf, quick and certain, and take the red from the mud as if the jungle has sent them to collect what I owe.

I push myself to my knees.

The world swings. I wait it out with one hand locked around a root. The root is slick as an eel and strong as bone. When my sight clears, I see water below me.

Not sea. A brown channel, still on top, moving underneath. Roots reach down into it like fingers. Something slides just below the surface and leaves a line that opens and closes without a ripple.

I keep away from it.

Thirst scrapes the back of my throat. It wants the brown water. My empty body makes stupid arguments. I have learned to listen and then decide whether listening will kill me.

Rainwater first. Clear water if I can find it. Any fruit that does not burn the tongue. No standing brown water unless dying has already become the only other choice.

Those are not thoughts in words. Not at first. They are old lessons stored in scars and hunger.

I stand.

The heat hits harder upright. It fills my shirt, runs down my spine, gathers under my arms. I breathe through my mouth and taste flowers, mold, and something dead nearby. Too much life packed too close becomes another kind of death.

That is the first lesson this place teaches me.

It is alive enough to eat itself.

I climb away from the channel, one hand on roots, one foot testing before weight. The mud wants my boots. Twice it takes one and I have to drag it free with both hands. A fly as long as my thumb lands on my cheek. I slap it and my palm comes away red. Mine or its. I cannot tell.

The jungle watches without eyes.

I find the first body under a fan of broad leaves.

He lies face-down, half in the mud, one arm twisted under him. I know him. Not his name. The ship had not left us names. Names are wasted on cargo. But I know the shape of his shoulders and the scar on the back of his neck where a collar rubbed him raw.

He was older than me. Stronger. He had cursed the slavers once in a language I did not know and lost two teeth for it.

Now the jungle has him.

White mushrooms grow from the corner of his mouth.

One hand is closed around mud. I pry the fingers open because hunger makes even the dead into questions. There is nothing there but a bit of leaf crushed to pulp. His nails are packed black. He died holding the island, and the island did not care enough to hold him back.

I know better than to speak over him. The hold taught silence around the dead. A man dies, the slaver kicks his feet apart, and everyone else looks small so death will not be counted as laziness. Still, I find myself wanting a word.

Not a prayer. I have no god that ever answered.

Only his name.

I have no name for him.

That feels like another theft.

I crouch beside him and look for a knife. Belt first. Boots. Hands. The places men hide small blades and charms when they are afraid.

Nothing.

His pouch has a strip of dried meat wrapped in cloth so filthy I almost miss it. My mouth fills before my hand closes.

I eat too fast.

Halfway through the second bite, my stomach heaves. I force the meat down with both hands over my mouth because losing food is a sin the body does not forgive.

When the meat is gone, I search him again. Still no knife. No flint. No cord worth taking. Only a copper token on a string around his neck, green with sweat and salt.

I take it because the dead do not need luck and I have none.

Something calls above me.

Not a bird. Too low. Too wet.

I freeze.

The sound comes again from the trees, a soft clacking that moves without moving. I look up and see nothing but leaves, shadow, and ropes of vine. Then one vine opens an eye.

Not a vine.

A snake, thick as my thigh, patterned green and black. Its head rests on a branch above the dead man's back. Its tongue tastes the air between us.

I step back very slowly.

The snake does not strike. It watches the body. It was here first, maybe. Or it was waiting for the jungle to finish softening him.

I leave it the man.

Mercy has nothing to do with it. A boy with no knife does not argue over meat with a snake that size.

By the time I find water, my tongue feels too large for my mouth.

It is not much. A thread running down stone where a tree has cracked the rock open. The water gathers in a shallow cup no wider than my hand before spilling into moss.

Clear.

I smell it. Touch it to my lip. Wait.

No burn. No bitter oil. No swamp skin.

I drink.

The first swallow hurts. The second becomes everything. I put my mouth to the stone and drink until the cup is empty, then wait while it fills drop by drop. I do this again and again until my stomach cramps.

Only then do I sit back.

The rock is warm behind me. The jungle steams. My shirt clings to the cuts on my back.

I should be shaking by now.

I know fever country. I have seen swamp air take men apart from the inside. First the eyes go glassy. Then the sweat turns cold. Then the hands shake too hard to hold a spoon. Men stronger than me have breathed air like this and died before morning.

But my hands are steady.

The bites on my arms swell and fade. The cuts from the wreck sting, then tighten. The rot air goes into me and comes out again, and whatever poison rides in it does not find a place to sit.

That frightens me more than fever would have.

Fever I understand.

This has no shape I know.

I touch the copper token at my neck. It is warm from my hand, stamped with a mark I cannot read. The dead boy kept it hidden under his shirt. Maybe it was his god. Maybe his mother. Maybe only a scrap he could call his own.

The mark on it is a bird with two heads, or a cup with wings, or some king's joke. I turn it three ways and it becomes three different lies. Maybe he knew the mark. Maybe he did not. Maybe the token was only heavy enough to remind him he had once been more than a price.

I tie it under my shirt anyway.

It knocks once against my chest when I move. A small sound. Almost company.

I have one piece like that.

A name.

I hold it where the slavers never looked. Behind the teeth they broke. Under the tongue they told me to keep still.

My name.

My mother gave it to me. Her face is blurred now, and that scares me in a deep place I rarely touch. I remember warmth. I remember a low voice. I remember being lifted once, before hands meant pain.

But I remember the name.

The slavers had another sound for me. A short ugly sound made in the back of the throat. It meant come here. It meant property. It meant a hand on my neck and a boot near my ribs.

I never let it become my name.

My name stays hidden. Small, hard, alive.

I keep it silent. The air here feels hungry enough to steal it.

The seep gives me enough water to think farther than my tongue.

I climb above it because height is the only wealth I have. The rock rises through the green in a broken spine, slick in the shade and hot where the sun reaches. I go slowly. My body complains with every pull. At the top, the trees thin for ten steps and the island opens.

Sea on one side.

Jungle on the other.

Between them, channels cut the land into strips of mud and root and pale stone. The water shines brown under the trees and blue where it finds the sky. Farther out, beyond the reef, the sea breaks white over black teeth. Wreckage turns in the surf there. Not much. A plank. A barrel. A torn length of sailcloth that lifts and falls like a hand too tired to wave.

The sight of open water should mean escape.

It does not.

The reef chews the sea to foam. Between the black rocks, currents pull at each other until the water knots. Even from the height I can see places where the surface dimples and spins without wind. A man with oars might die there. A boy with no boat would be gone before he had time to regret hope.

I look anyway.

No sail.

No smoke.

No human voice carrying across the water.

The island answers with a bird landing on the stone beside me.

It is no bigger than my fist, green-backed, with a red throat bright enough to seem painted. It stands on one thin leg and studies me with a black bead of an eye. In its beak it holds a silver beetle, still kicking.

"You have more than I do," I whisper.

The bird tilts its head as if weighing the fairness of this claim. Then it swallows the beetle whole and drops a white streak of filth onto the rock beside my knee.

For half a breath I only stare.

Then I laugh.

The laugh comes easier than the first one. Still ugly. Still cracked. But it belongs to me. The bird decides my joy is suspicious and flits away.

I wipe the mess with leaves and keep one eye on the sky. Small disrespect is safer than large mercy. I almost thank the bird for reminding me of that.

On the far side of the rock, I find shells caught in a rain hollow. Not sea shells. These are thin as fingernails and blue inside, each one no wider than a coin. They lie in a crescent on dry stone with no water near them. Something brought them here to crack and eat.

I crouch.

The shells are split at the same place, neat as if cut by a tiny knife. No bird beak made those lines. No crab claw either. I pick one up and see a black thorn caught in the crack.

Not a thorn.

A tooth.

Little. Curved. Sharp.

The stone under my foot shifts.

I leap back as the whole shell crescent rises.

It is not stone. It is a creature wearing stone-colored skin and old shells across its back. A flat head lifts from a seam I had not seen. Two eyes open sideways. Its mouth spreads wider than seems possible, filled with rows of small black teeth.

It snaps where my ankle was.

The sound is soft.

That softness frightens me more than a roar.

I scramble down the spine of rock, skinning one elbow, and land hard in wet leaves. The shell-backed creature does not chase. It settles again, folds its eyes away, and becomes part of the stone.

The island laughs without making a sound.

I stand with my heart hammering and my elbow bleeding, and I learn another law.

Not every trap hides in mud.

Some wait in beautiful places.

When the rain comes, it comes like a roof collapsing.

One breath the jungle is dripping. The next it is roaring. Water hammers the leaves, the mud, my head, the stone beside me. I open my mouth and drink what falls clean from the sky. It tastes of leaf and heat and a green bitterness that does not matter because it is water.

Then the rain stops.

Just stops.

The roar becomes dripping again. Steam rises from the ground. In the sudden quiet, I hear something moving through the undergrowth below the rock.

Men would snap branches.

This does not.

I crouch behind the stone and look down through a curtain of wet leaves.

A shape steps into the clearing.

It is not a man.

It walks on two legs, but its arms are too long and its shoulders too high. Black hair hangs wet over a chest broad enough to make a door. Its face is flat and heavy-browed, with pale scars crossing one cheek. In one hand it carries a club made from a root ball hardened by age.

It stops beside the dead boy.

The snake above him lifts its head.

For a moment nothing moves.

Then the creature bares its teeth and makes a sound so low I feel it in the stone under my hand.

The snake slides backward into leaves.

I understand many kinds of strength. The strength that hits. The strength that orders others to hit. The strength of iron bars, locked doors, numbers, hunger.

This is older.

The creature crouches by the body. It sniffs the dead boy's neck, his hands, the torn cloth where I took the meat. Then its head turns.

Toward me.

I hold still.

The creature's nostrils flare.

It knows I am here.

My hand closes around a stone. Smooth, wet, too small. Not a weapon. But anything is a weapon if the enemy does not expect it.

The creature rises.

Behind it, the jungle shifts.

Two smaller shapes appear among the leaves. Then another. Watching. Waiting. Not animals, not men, but a people of some kind. A family, maybe. A hunting party.

The large one looks up the slope, straight through the rain-bright leaves, and meets my eyes.

Fear moves through me.

Not panic. Panic spends too fast.

This is cleaner. This puts weight in my feet and narrows the world to distance, stone, branch, breath.

The large one makes a sound.

Not a roar. Not a word I know.

The shape of it is almost a question.

I show my teeth.

The creature's face changes. The mouth pulls back, not quite a smile. Not kindness. Interest.

That is worse.

Kindness can be begged from, sometimes. Cruelty can be predicted. Interest does experiments.

I lift the stone.

My arm shakes now. Not fever. Exhaustion. The creature sees it. Of course it does.

It takes one step uphill.

I throw.

The stone strikes its shoulder and falls away.

The smaller ones behind it flinch. The large one looks at the place where the stone hit, then back at me.

It laughs.

The sound is enormous.

The jungle goes quiet around it.

I run.

Not down. Never toward the water unless the land gives no other choice. I run along the ridge, through dripping leaves, over roots slick as guts. Pain tears through my ribs. My lungs burn. Something behind me crashes once through brush, then stops.

It is not chasing.

That makes me more afraid.

Creatures chase when they fear losing you.

Creatures that know the land let the land chase for them.

The ridge narrows. Ahead, a fallen tree bridges a black cut in the earth where water runs far below. The trunk is wet and furred with moss. Too narrow. Too slick.

Behind me, the leaves move.

I step onto the trunk.

Halfway across, my boot slides.

For one breath I am back on the ship. Floor tilting. Hands grabbing. A mouth laughing above me.

No.

I drop to my knees, hug the trunk, and pull myself forward. Bark rips skin from my palms. The cut below breathes cold up at me, the only cold in this whole green world.

I reach the far side and roll into mud.

The large creature stands at the other end of the trunk.

It could follow.

It does not.

It watches me with those dark, unreadable eyes. Then it touches its own chest with one huge hand and makes a sound.

Slow. Deliberate.

Not for the others.

For me.

I cannot understand it.

The creature touches its chest again and repeats the sound.

A name, maybe.

Names are dangerous. Names are hooks. Names are proof that a life exists outside your fear of it.

I keep mine behind my teeth.

The creature waits.

Rainwater runs down its scarred face. The smaller shapes gather behind it, restless, murmuring in throats too deep for their bodies.

I should run again.

I stay.

Because the creature is not crossing.

Because it could have killed me and did not.

Because the jungle behind me is endless and hungry, and I am a boy with no knife, no fire, no tribe, and one hidden name.

The large creature turns away first.

It gives a short command to the others. They melt back into the green, carrying the dead boy between them. Food, maybe. Warning, maybe. Law.

The large one looks at me once more before the leaves take it.

This time the sound it makes is lower.

I hear no threat in it.

That does not mean there is none.

When it is gone, I stay on my knees in the mud with my bleeding hands under me and my name locked behind my teeth.

The jungle drips.

Somewhere below, brown water closes over something struggling.

I have survived the first day, or part of it.

The place has noticed.

That is worse than being alone.

# Chapter 3: Hunger Law

Hunger teaches faster than fear.

Fear makes noise. It throws pictures at the mind. Hands in the dark. Teeth in leaves. The brown water opening under a body and closing again.

Hunger is simpler.

Get up.

Find food.

Live.

By the second morning, it has stripped me down to those three commands.

I wake under the rock shelf with my tongue stuck to my teeth and my belly folded in on itself. Rainwater waits in leaf cups above me. Clear water beads on the stone where the seep runs. Water I can find now. Water I can keep.

Food is harder.

The strip of dried meat from the dead boy is gone. I remember chewing it too fast. I remember keeping it down by force. I remember the snake watching from the branch as if it had already decided which of us owned the body.

The jungle is full of meat.

That is the cruel part. It moves everywhere. Flashes in the brown channel. Clicks under bark. Whistles above me in the leaves. The whole island is eating or being eaten.

I am the only living body here that does not know the rules.

So I watch.

The birds teach first. Small bright birds with hooked beaks and hard black eyes. They drop from the canopy in quick bursts, land on branches, take fruit, tear at pale grubs under bark, and vanish before larger shadows pass overhead.

They avoid the clear fish.

That matters.

The fish drift in the shallows almost without color, easy to see, easy to catch. Too easy. I lie near the water and watch three birds land within a handspan of them. Not one bird strikes.

Easy food no one takes is not food. It is bait with no string showing.

I remember that.

The fish remember me too.

At first I think the current moves them. Then I cut my thumb on a shell and one red drop falls into the pool. The clear bodies turn at once. Not toward it. Away. Every fish slides backward into the shadow under a root, and the water between my hand and them opens clean.

Blood should call hunger.

Mine sends it away.

I hold my thumb over the pool until another drop falls. The fish scatter harder, striking mud and root in their panic.

I draw my hand back slowly.

The cut has already begun to close.

For a while I forget hunger.

The pool is small. The island is large. Still, the pool knows something about me that I cannot name. That is a lonely feeling, to be read by fish and not by yourself.

I follow the birds until my legs shake. They eat a soft yellow fruit split open on the branch. I wait. They do not fall. They do not stagger. They wipe their beaks and come back for more.

I climb for it.

The bark is slick. The trunk sweats under my hands. Halfway up, something with too many legs runs over my fingers and disappears into a crack. I nearly let go. I hold. Pride has nothing to do with it. Falling wastes strength.

The fruit smells sweet and rotten at once. I peel back the skin with my thumbnail and touch the pulp to my tongue.

No burn.

I eat.

The first fruit is gone in three bites. The second I eat slower because I have learned one lesson at least. The third I carry down tucked inside my shirt and eat under the rock shelf where nothing can see how badly I want it.

It stays down.

For an hour, I feel rich.

Then hunger opens again.

The jungle has a law for that too. Nothing fills for long.

By midday I find the grubs. Fat white curls under dead bark where the birds have been tearing. They burst bitter between my teeth. I gag once and keep chewing. I have eaten worse from kinder hands.

The grubs stay down.

That becomes the shape of the day. Watch. Test. Wait. Eat more if the body does not rebel.

Fruit. Grub. Rainwater. A brown frog the birds take whole from a leaf. I break its neck against a stone and eat it raw, fighting the slide of it in my throat. It stays down too.

I begin to make a map of what wants me dead.

Bright red berries: left alone by birds, left alone by me.

Clear fish: left alone.

Black water: left alone.

Yellow fruit: safe for now.

White grubs: safe, ugly.

Brown frogs: safe if I can catch them.

The frog teaches a smaller lesson. When I reach for the next one, it puffs itself round and pisses on my hand. The smell is sharp enough to make my eyes water. I drop it, and it lands in the mud with the dignity of a little king, then springs away.

I laugh before I can stop myself.

It comes out thin and cracked. It hurts my ribs. The jungle takes no notice. No guard turns. No boot comes. No one says quiet.

The second frog does not get away.

By evening, I am tired enough to shake but not empty enough to lie down and stop.

That is victory here.

Small victories are still food.

The fever comes after dark.

At first I think it is only the heat. The jungle holds the day long after the sun is gone. Warm mud. Warm stone. Warm air pressing down into the crack where I sleep.

Then the shaking starts.

Not cold. Not fear. The body turning against itself.

My stomach knots. My skin burns. The rock above me blurs, sharpens, blurs again. I crawl out from under the shelf and vomit until there is nothing left to bring up. The fruit comes first. Then bile. Then strings of spit that shine in the firefly dark.

The clear fish, I think, though I did not eat them.

The frog.

The grubs.

The air.

The jungle has too many knives. I cannot tell which one is in me.

I curl on my side in the mud with both hands over my belly. The fever climbs. I have seen men die like this in holds and camps and swamp ports. First the heat. Then the shaking. Then the eyes go loose and the mouth begins to ask for mothers, gods, water, anything.

I keep silent.

There is no one here to answer.

I hold my name behind my teeth.

My name.

If the jungle takes everything else, it can choke on that.

The dark folds over me.

When I wake, it is morning.

That is wrong.

I lie still and take the count.

Mouth dry. Belly sore. Hands steady. Head clear. No shaking. No fever. The bites on my arms have gone down. The cuts across my palms have pulled into thin red lines.

I sit up too fast and almost faint.

Weak, yes.

Dying, no.

I look at my hands for a long time.

Cuts do not close that fast. Fever does not come like a storm and leave no wreck behind. Men do not breathe this air and sleep in this mud and wake cleaner than they were.

But I have.

The thought is too large to hold. It slips away when I try to grip it.

Good. Let it go for now.

Understanding can wait. Food cannot.

The tide teaches next.

I notice it because the brown channel pulls back from its own banks and leaves the mud shining. Little pools appear among the roots. Small bodies trapped in them flick and burrow, betrayed by water that has remembered the sea.

This is better food.

Crabs first. Dark, heavy shells with claws too slow once I learn where to put my hand. I smash them with stones and eat the meat raw. It is sweet. It stays.

Then a pale eel buried under mud, all muscle and slime. I almost lose it twice before I pin it with both hands. It twists so hard I think it will break my wrist. I bite behind the head because I have no knife and no shame left for the work of staying alive.

It stays too.

At low water, the jungle gives.

At high water, it takes back.

That is a law I can use.

The tide also shows me roads.

When the water pulls away, the mud keeps every passing. Bird feet. Crab scratches. The belly-line of a snake. Once, a set of prints like small hands, too long in the finger, pressed beside the pool where the clear fish hide. They come out of the water and go back in. No bird has feet like that. No child should be here.

I crouch over them until the tide creeps around my toes.

The pool behind me dimples once.

I leave before I see what makes the second mark.

The next low tide takes me farther than sense should allow.

The channel empties itself into shining flats where the mud turns silver under a skin of water. Roots stand exposed to the knee, black and tangled, and between them lie pockets of sand firm enough to bear my weight if I test every step first. The air smells of salt now. Not open sea. Hidden sea. The kind that slips under the island and comes back carrying secrets.

I go because the easy pools near the rock have learned me.

Crabs dive sooner. Frogs keep higher leaves. Birds strip the yellow fruit before I reach the branch. A place remembers hunger. If I eat from one hand too often, that hand closes.

So I follow the new flats.

The first treasure is a field of little clams buried under the sand. I find them by watching a gray bird with legs like sticks stab its beak into the flat. The bird pulls up a clam, cracks it against a root, and swallows the meat. It does this three times and does not die.

I take that as permission.

The clams are hard to open without a knife. I use a stone and mash more shell than meat. Sand gets into my teeth. One shell pinches my fingertip hard enough to make me curse, and I spend a foolish breath glaring at a food with no eyes.

"You lost," I tell it after I pry it open.

The clam offers no argument.

That makes me feel better than it should.

Farther on, the flats become strange.

The mud is covered in round holes, each one rimmed with little pellets. When my shadow crosses them, eyes vanish down every hole at once. Hundreds. Maybe more. The ground itself seems to blink.

I kneel and wait.

One eye returns. Then another. Then a small pale crab lifts itself out and waves one huge red claw at me, angry as a lord with one arm too fine for work. Another crab answers with its own claw. Soon the whole flat is full of red waving hands.

I laugh again.

The sound sends them down their holes.

Hunger would have me dig. Sense keeps me still. Too many holes. Too much open ground. No cover. The tide will return, and I have not learned how fast. The crabs can keep their kingdom for today.

Then the wind shifts.

Something sour reaches me.

Not rot. Not dead fish.

Egg.

I find the nest under a shelf of root where the mud has dried in a black crust. Six eggs lie inside, speckled green and brown, each one warm to the touch. I look up at once.

The mother stands ten strides away.

At first I think she is a fallen branch propped on two legs. Then her head turns, and I see a beak long as my forearm, a white mask around both eyes, and a crest of red feathers lying flat against her skull. She is taller than I am. Thin, but not weak. Her knees bend the wrong way for comfort. Her feet spread wide on the mud, each toe ending in a black nail.

She opens her beak.

No sound comes out.

The silence runs colder than a cry.

I lower the egg.

"I am hungry," I say.

She does not care. I like her for that. She knows her law.

Six eggs. One starving boy. One mother with a killing beak.

I take one.

The bird strikes before I clear the nest.

I throw myself sideways. The beak punches into the mud where my throat was. She tears it free and comes again, all legs and blade and white-eyed fury. I scramble under the root shelf, the egg clutched against my belly. She cannot reach me cleanly there. She tries anyway. Her beak stabs through gaps in the roots. Once it catches my shoulder and opens a line of fire across the skin.

Blood runs down my arm.

The bird freezes.

Her head pulls back. The red crest lifts. She smells the air with quick jerks of her beak, then steps away from the root shelf.

Not fear.

Recognition would be worse.

She gathers the remaining eggs under herself with careful feet and backs toward the taller reeds, never taking her eyes from the blood on my arm. I stay under the roots until she is gone.

Only then do I break the egg.

It is rich and warm and nearly makes me sick with wanting.

I eat it raw. I lick the shell. I hate myself a little and am alive.

When I wash the cut later, the clear fish flee from the red thread again. The bird's beak should have left a wound for days. By evening it is only a bright seam.

The island keeps reading me.

I keep failing to read myself.

For three days I live by it. Sleep under the rock. Drink from the seep. Watch the birds. Work the low pools when the channel draws away. Stay out of the water when it rises.

My leg heals.

That becomes harder to ignore.

The cut from the wreck should be swollen and green by now. Instead the skin pulls tight and clean around it. Red turns pink. Heat leaves. When I wash it in the seep, old blood flakes away and new flesh shows beneath.

Too fast.

I press a thumb beside the wound until pain comes. Pain is honest. Pain says the leg is mine.

The healing says something else.

I cannot name it.

That night I dream of the slaver's hand on my neck. In the dream his fingers are cold. Not dead-ship cold. Human cold. Worse, because he is alive and choosing it.

When I wake, my name is in my mouth.

I nearly say it.

I bite down until blood comes instead.

The jungle does not get my name.

Not yet.

On the fourth evening, as rain gathers in the leaves and the channel rises brown around the roots, I hear a sound far above the water.

Low.

Long.

Made by a throat too large for any man.

The hunger in my belly goes still.

Another hunger opens under it, one I have no food for.

Not meat.

Not water.

Someone.

I crouch under the rock shelf with crab shell in my hand and listen until the sound comes again.

It is not close.

But it is not alone.

Before sleep takes me, rain clears from the eastern sky and a line of pale lights drifts over the channel.

Not fireflies. Larger. Blue-white, soft as breath, floating above the water on thin wings that hardly move. They pass over the clear fish pool, and the fish rise under them in a silent cloud, not eating, not fleeing. Only following the light until the last wing goes dark beyond the roots.

For one moment the island is beautiful in a way that does not ask anything of me.

Then something screams in the trees and beauty runs for its life.

I almost laugh.

Not because it is funny.

Because the island cannot bear to be gentle for more than a breath without remembering itself.

# Chapter 4: The First Fire

Night belongs to the jungle.

Day is cruel, but day can be read. Light shows the channel, the roots, the birds, the color of fruit, the places where mud lies about how deep it is.

Night takes the edges off everything.

The water becomes sound. The trees become weight. Insects rise in clouds from the swamp and stitch the dark with their whining. Bodies move beyond the rock shelf with a patience I hate.

The first nights, I survive by staying small.

I crawl into the crack under the stone, knees against my chest, back pressed hard to the wall. The place smells of wet mineral and old rot. It is barely large enough for me. That is why I choose it. Large places let too much in.

But sleep does not come cleanly there.

Every sound wakes me. A splash in the channel. A branch creaking. The soft patter of feet over leaves. Once, something breathes at the mouth of the crack for a long time, wet and slow, before moving on.

I have no knife.

I have stones. Teeth. Hands.

Not enough.

I need fire.

Men had fire on the ship. The slavers kept it above, near their voices and their meat. Down in the dark hold we got the smell and nothing else. Smoke. Fat. Warmth that belonged to other people.

I watched anyway.

A boy with nothing learns by stealing with his eyes.

I saw men feed flame. Dry threads first, then sticks. Too much too soon and the little light died. I saw sparks leap when steel struck stone. I saw a cook curse wet wood and throw it aside.

Now I have no steel and all the wood is wet.

That is the problem.

Problems can be worked.

I spend the morning looking down and learn nothing. Every branch on the ground is soft with rot. Some split open under my fingers and spill white worms. Some are full of ants. One log looks sound until I lift it and it comes apart like old bread.

Wet wood will not burn.

So I look up.

Dead branches hang caught in the living trees, lifted away from the rot. Sun reaches them. Wind touches them when wind touches anything here. I climb for the first one and nearly fall twice. The bark peels under my hands. My bad leg trembles. Sweat blinds me.

The branch cracks loose with a dry snap.

Dry.

The sound is almost beautiful.

I gather more. Thin branches first. Then thicker ones. I drag them to the rock shelf and stack them where rain will have to work to reach them. I strip pale fiber from vines and spread it on stone to dry. I scrape bark into curls with a sharp flake of black rock.

By sunset I have a little pile of wood.

No fire.

The next day I try stone.

I strike one rock against another until my hands ache. Nothing. I find a darker stone with a glassy edge and strike that. Chips fly. One cuts my palm. Blood wells, bright and immediate.

Still no spark.

The cut closes before evening.

I watch it happen. Not all at once. Not like magic from a tale. The bleeding slows. The edges tighten. The pain folds down into a clean line. By dark it looks a day old. Maybe two.

It should not.

I press the cut open again with my thumb because anger is sometimes stronger than sense.

Blood comes.

The small wound begins its quiet work again.

I hate it then. Whatever lives in my blood, whatever keeps fever and rot from taking me, it will not answer when I ask. It gives me no fire. It gives me no knife. It only keeps me alive in a place that seems insulted by the fact.

"What are you?" I whisper.

The jungle drips.

Nothing answers.

Good. I had not meant to speak.

I go back to the wood.

Stone will not give me flame. So I try friction.

The memory comes from before the hold, or maybe from the ship, or maybe from watching some man make fire while I pretended not to watch. A stick spun against a board. Smoke rising. Hands moving fast.

I cut a notch in a flat piece of dry wood. I set a straight stick into it and roll it between my palms.

Nothing.

I roll faster.

Heat comes. Not enough.

The stick slips. My hands blister. The blister tears. Blood slicks the wood and ruins the work.

I stop before I throw the whole fire-kit into the channel.

Rage is easy. Rage has always been easy. It gives the body something to do when the world refuses to open. But rage spends strength and leaves nothing cooked.

I wash my hands. Bind them with strips torn from my shirt. Start again.

This time I brace the board with my foot. I keep the stick straight. I lean my weight into it and roll until my shoulders burn.

Smoke comes.

Thin. Gray. Almost not there.

I freeze.

The smoke fades.

"No."

The word comes out rough and small.

It is not the first word I have spoken on the island, but it is the first I have spent on something that might answer. The sound hangs between my hands and the dead wood. I hear a woman in it for half a breath, a low voice correcting my fingers around a spoon, or a cup, or a toy I cannot see clearly anymore.

The memory is gone before I can catch it.

I want it back with a hunger that has nothing to do with meat.

I start again.

By the time the coal forms, the light is failing and my arms feel hollow.

It is only a black speck in a nest of dust. A dull red eye. I bend over it with both hands cupped around the place and breathe softly. Too much breath kills it. Too little and it dies alone.

The red deepens.

I tip it into the dry vine fiber.

For a terrible second nothing happens.

Then a thread catches.

The flame is smaller than my thumb.

It is the most powerful scrap of life I have ever owned.

I feed it shavings. Then twigs. Then split sticks no thicker than my finger. I remember the cook drowning a flame with greed and do not make his mistake.

The fire grows.

Gold light pushes against the green dark. Smoke climbs into the leaves. The insects lift away from it. The mouth of the rock crack glows. Shadows move back.

Not gone.

Back.

That is enough.

I laugh once.

The sound startles me. I clap a hand over my mouth, but there is no one here to punish joy. No slaver. No guard. No man above the hold with a boot ready for the boy who made too much noise.

Only the fire.

Mine.

I learn quickly that fire is greedy.

It eats the little sticks faster than I expect. It sulks when I give it wood too green. It spits when rainwater hides in bark. It brightens when I shave dry curls from the heart of a branch and feed them one by one, slow enough for the flame to choose.

By the second hour I am no longer making fire.

I am keeping it.

That is harder.

Making is a shout. Keeping is a promise said again and again in small ways. Turn the fish before it burns. Move the coal before ash chokes it. Shelter the flame when the wind slips under the leaves. Do the work while tired. Do it while hungry. Do it when the dark outside the light makes every muscle want to curl around the spear and wait.

I think of the slavers' lamps then, hanging beyond the bars.

They never guarded the flame. They guarded us from it.

Heat for them. Smoke for us. Cooked meat for them. The smell for us. Light on their faces, dark on ours.

I take a burning twig from my little fire and hold it up.

It shakes in my hand because my arm is worn out, but the flame stays. Small. Stubborn. Mine.

"See?" I whisper to the dark where no slaver waits. "Mine now."

No answer comes.

That makes the victory cleaner.

When the first moths find the light, I almost strike them away.

They are large as my palm, with wings clear as wet glass and blue veins running through them. They circle the fire slowly, not stupid enough to throw themselves into it. Light passes through their wings and paints moving water on the stone.

One lands on the back of my hand.

I hold still.

Its feet are softer than ash. Its body trembles with a life so slight I can feel each beat through my skin. The blue veins in its wings darken near my healing cuts, then pale again. It turns once, as if tasting me through its feet, and lifts away.

More come. For a while the whole space under the rock is full of clear wings and firelight, blue shadows opening and closing over the mud.

It is beautiful.

The word comes to me with difficulty, as if beauty is another tool I have let rust. I know useful. I know dangerous. I know edible, sharp, rotten, weak, strong, owned, free.

Beautiful is harder.

Beautiful asks nothing and still takes breath.

I try to remember my mother's face and find only pieces. A cheek in lamplight. A dark braid over one shoulder. Fingers tapping twice on a cup before she gives it to me. Was that real? The memory trembles like the moth on my hand.

My name presses behind my teeth.

For one dangerous breath I want to say it into the fire. Not to give it away. To hear it alive somewhere outside me.

I press my tongue to the roof of my mouth until the want passes.

Not yet.

The fire makes faces of the trees.

All day they were trunks, leaves, vines, walls. In firelight they become watchers. Old ones with twisted mouths. Thin ones leaning close. One broad root looks so much like a bent man that I throw a stone at it before sense catches up.

The stone bounces off.

The bent man remains a root.

I tell it to stay dead.

My voice sounds strange and rough and almost pleased. Talking to roots is foolish. No one here knows enough to mock me for it. That makes foolishness feel like a stolen luxury.

I cook a second fish and hold it up toward the root-man.

"None for you," I tell it.

The root-man accepts this with dignity.

After that I name the crooked stone by the door Captain, because it sits there doing nothing and looks important. I name a beetle Tax, because it steals a crumb of fish and leaves without asking. Tax survives my judgment by being too quick for the slap.

I smile with half my mouth.

It is an ugly smile. Three missing teeth make every smile into a broken gate. I still like the feel of it. There is no one here to own the shape of my face.

For a little while, the fire and I are very rich.

I cook a crab first. Then one of the clear fish the birds will not eat. I hold it over the flame on a green stick until the flesh goes white and splits. The smell is rich enough to hurt.

The fish stays down.

Fire changes the law.

By midnight I know three new truths.

Smoke keeps the biting clouds away.

Cooked clear fish does not poison me.

And light makes the dark honest.

Not safe. Honest.

Bodies still move beyond the ring of firelight, but now they have edges when they come close. Eyes shine. Leaves stir. Mud prints show where something crossed and thought I would not see.

Near dawn, I wake to the sound again.

The low call from the high green.

It rolls through the trees, deeper than any bird, shaped enough that my chest tightens around it. Another call answers. Farther off. Then a third, almost too low to hear.

Not beasts calling territory.

Voices.

I sit by my fire with a cooked fish in my hand and forget to eat.

There are people here.

Not human. I know that already. I saw the huge scarred one across the ravine. I saw the smaller shapes behind it. But knowing with the eyes is different from hearing voices in the dark.

Voices mean memory.

Voices mean law.

Voices mean I am not alone.

The thought opens inside me too fast. Hope is dangerous because it runs before the feet know the ground.

I crush it down.

People can be worse than jungle. People can put hands on a boy's neck and call it ownership. People can feed you and count the debt in your skin.

Still, when the call comes again, I lean toward it.

I hate myself for that.

Then I feed the fire before it can die, because hating myself will not keep the dark back.

The sun lifts white behind the leaves.

Smoke threads upward.

Somewhere in the green, the voices move.

And for the first time since the sea gave me to this place, I choose a direction for a reason other than food.

# Chapter 5: The Rot and the Den

Rain kills the fire on the fourth night.

It comes all at once, not in drops but in a wall. The jungle disappears behind it. Leaves flatten. The channel turns white where the water strikes it. I throw myself over the coals, arms curved around the little red heart I bled for, but my body is not a roof.

The fire hisses once.

Then it is gone.

For a while I crouch over the blackened pit with rain running down my face and my back and into the raw places on my hands. I hate the sky. I hate the dead coals. I hate myself for thinking a flame could belong to me just because I made it.

The jungle gives no time for grief.

Water comes into the crack under the stone before midnight. First a dark gleam on the floor. Then a stream. Then brown water full of leaves and pale worms sliding over my feet. The crack that saved me from the first nights becomes a drain for the whole hill. The smell rises with it, sour and alive.

I back out into the rain with the last coal dead behind me.

I need a place that does not drown.

Not a hole the island gave me.

A place I make.

The next morning the world steams. White breath lifts from the leaves. Every low place stinks of rot. I stand above the drowned crack and read the hill the way I have learned to read mud, tide, bird, and branch.

Water tells the truth after rain.

It pools where a man should not sleep. It runs where stone is sound. It drips from the branches that will stay wet for days and beads on the leaves that shed it quickly. The old crack was low, warm, hidden, and wrong. I chose it because I was afraid of open ground.

Fear makes bad houses.

So I climb.

The slope above the channel is broken with pale stone. It cuts through the green in shelves and hard ribs. My feet slip. My thigh aches where the old wound pulls. Twice I stop and rest with my hand against rock warm from yesterday's sun.

Halfway up, I find a tree split by lightning long ago. The inside is black and dry under a curled lip of bark. Char without fire. I scrape it out with my fingers and laugh once, short and mean, because the sky killed a tree and left me tinder.

I thank the dead tree.

Then I feel foolish and thank it again, quieter.

Near the top I find the place.

A slab has fallen from the ridge and lodged against another stone, making a dry pocket beneath. Not large. Barely high enough to crawl into. But the ground under it is powder-dry, and when I press my palm down, no damp rises between my fingers.

Dry.

The word feels almost as rich as meat.

I crawl inside and lie on my back. Stone leans over me. Stone holds behind me. Rain would have to work to reach this hollow, and work is more than rain likes when easier ground lies below.

It is not safe.

Nothing here is safe.

But it can be kept.

That is the next law the island teaches me. Fire is not made once. Shelter is not made once. Anything worth having must be defended against the rot every day.

I spend the next three days learning how to build badly, then less badly.

Stone first. Big pieces at the base. Smaller pieces wedged between. A stone set round side down will roll and punish the hand that trusted it. A flat stone wants a flat stone under it. Heavy stones do not care if a boy is tired.

One catches my fingers and crushes them hard enough to whiten the world.

I curl around the hand, breath locked behind my teeth. Blood gathers under the nails and runs down the split skin at the knuckle. By any law I know, the hand should swell and stiffen. By the green's law it should sour.

It does neither.

By evening the bleeding has slowed. By the next dawn the torn skin has drawn tight and pale, too fast, too clean. I stare at it until anger rises in my throat.

"Why?"

The word is smaller than the rain.

No answer comes. No answer ever comes. Whatever lives wrong in my blood keeps me alive and tells me nothing.

So I put the hand back on the stones.

After stone comes vine. The fresh vines rot almost as soon as I cut them, so I learn to strip them and lay them across sun-hot rock until they toughen. I weave them through the wall and fill the gaps with dry leaves, cane, bark, and mud. I leave a crawl mouth low enough that I can block it from inside with a stone.

By the third night I have a den.

Not a crack.

Not a gift.

Mine.

I mark the stone beside the crawl mouth with three scratches. Not letters. Not a name. Only proof that a hand chose this place and came back to it. The scratches look small against the rock. They are small. I run my thumb over them anyway until grit packs under my nail.

The slavers marked crates.

I mark home.

I make fire again inside it. Slower this time. Careful. I build a nest of dry fiber and coax the coal up until flame licks the little sticks. Smoke curls along the stone and escapes through a gap I left high in the wall. Heat gathers under the slab instead of vanishing into the trees.

For the first time since the sea spat me out, warmth stays where I put it.

Then the insects come.

They rise from the swamp at dusk in black clouds. The biting flies. The ants that find blood through mud. Soft white worms that drop from leaves and search blindly for skin. They climb the wall. They cover the outer leaves. They find the fish bones near the fire and turn them black.

But they do not land on me.

The cloud bends around my arm.

I hold my hand out, slowly. The flies part before it as if my skin gives off smoke they cannot bear. Ants run over the stone beside my hip and circle back. A fat pale worm drops from the roof, writhes near my knee, and turns away.

The den did not do this.

I did not do this.

Something in me makes the island refuse me even while it tries to kill me.

I sit very still in the firelight and watch a clean space hold around my body. A hand's width. No more. Enough.

I should be glad. Some low, tired part of me is. The part that has been bitten and opened and taken from its whole life is glad for one night of whole skin.

The rest of me is afraid.

Not of the flies.

Of the reason.

The swarm teaches me another law.

It will not touch fresh blood from my hand, but it loves old blood on bone. When I set a crab shell too close to the door, the black cloud gathers over it until the shell seems furred. When I move the shell with a stick, the cloud follows. When a drop from my knuckle falls onto the same shell, the cloud breaks apart as if wind has passed through it.

Not all blood, then.

Mine is different.

The den is tested before I trust it.

The test comes two nights later, in rain so steady it seems less like weather than a hand pouring water from the dark.

I wake because the clean space around me breaks.

Not the insects. They are gone from the crawl mouth, gone from the wall, gone from the meat strips hung above the smoke. The quiet they leave is wrong. A hundred little hungers have agreed to be elsewhere.

Then the stone at the entrance moves.

Only a finger's width.

But I set that stone myself. I chose it because it is flat and heavy and wedges under a lip of rock. It should not move unless a hand moves it from outside.

I take the spear.

The fire is low, red under ash. Good for warmth. Bad for seeing. I hook dry bark into the coal and breathe until flame lifts. Light pushes toward the crawl mouth.

Something is pressed there.

Not Vorth. Not swine. Too low. Too long.

I see a pale muzzle through the narrow gap where the stone has shifted. Wet black nose. Whiskers stiff as fish bones. Behind them, one yellow eye opens and looks in at me.

The mouth that follows has too many teeth for its face.

The creature pushes again.

The door-stone grinds inward.

I throw myself against it from inside, shoulder first. Pain bursts through my bruised ribs. The stone stops. The pale muzzle withdraws, then rams forward. The whole den shudders. Dry leaves sift down onto the fire.

I am not strong enough to hold long.

That truth arrives cleanly. No shame in it. Shame can come later if I survive.

The creature digs at the crawl mouth. Claws scrape stone. Mud spatters through the gap. I thrust with the spear and hit fur, then hide. The point skids along bone and comes back wet. The creature screams into the den, a thin furious sound that makes my teeth ache.

The scream stirs the warmth under my ribs.

Not fire.

Red.

It lifts its head in me. It likes the narrow doorway. It likes the trapped shape of the fight. It knows the answer before I can form a plan: open the way, let the creature in, meet teeth with teeth, turn fear around.

For one breath the idea feels simple and sweet.

Then I see a boy's teeth in the hold, red after a guard's boot, and the way all of us went smaller because smaller survived.

I am not opening the door.

I shove the spear through the gap again, not to kill. To make room. When the creature snaps at the shaft, I grab a smoking brand from the fire and jam it into the dry leaves packed beside the crawl mouth.

Smoke blooms.

At first it blinds me too. My eyes pour water. I cough until my bruised ribs feel split. But the smoke finds the gap and climbs into the pale muzzle outside.

The creature coughs.

I feed the leaves.

More smoke.

The yellow eye vanishes. The claws scrape backward. A long body crashes through wet brush outside, then stops. I hear it breathing beyond the wall. Angry. Unhurt enough to remember me.

I sit with my shoulder against the door-stone until dawn.

When light comes, I find blood on the spear point and pale fur caught in a crack. The track outside is long, low, and splayed, with claw marks deep enough to hold rainwater. The door-stone has a groove cut across it where teeth scraped the edge.

The den held.

Not because the island gave it.

Because I made it badly, then less badly, and because fear has taught my hands to check work twice.

I sleep after sunrise with the spear across my chest and wake at midday to find a small gray lizard near my foot.

It has slipped through a gap in the wall and frozen on the warm stone as if it helped with the watch. It is missing half its tail. One eye is clouded. It studies me as if I am the intruder, which is fair.

"Pay rent," I whisper.

It blinks.

I flick it a beetle. The lizard takes the beetle in a flash and stays.

So the den has two mouths breathing in it for a while. Mine, and one small thief's. When I wake before dawn, it is gone, leaving a curl of shed skin beside the fire pit like a pale little rope.

I keep the skin. The reason stays out of reach.

The thought is too large again, and this time I hold it longer. I sit with it while the insects circle the clean space around my skin.

Different can mean valuable.

Different can mean cursed.

Different can mean both, if the wrong hands learn it.

I eat fish with shaking hands. The meat is hot and smoky and good. I chew on the side where my teeth remain, careful of the gap where three were knocked loose long ago.

Behind that gap, hidden in the dark under my tongue, I keep my name.

My name.

I keep it silent. A name spoken can be heard. A name heard can be taken. The slavers had their own sounds for me, ugly commands shaped like a hand closing on the neck. Those were not my name. They were hooks. They sank them in and pulled.

My name is my mother's sound.

I hold it against the warmth in my mouth while the fire lives and the insects crawl over everything but me.

The den keeps out rain. The fire keeps back night. My blood keeps back the green in ways I cannot name.

It is almost enough.

Then, far below the ridge, a call moves through the dark.

Low. Shaped. Too heavy for a bird. Too deliberate for wind.

I go still with a strip of fish in my hand.

The call comes again, closer this time.

Not a beast crying at the moon.

A voice.

# Chapter 6: First Blood

The voice does not return with the morning.

I wait for it longer than I should. Crouched at the mouth of the den, spearless, hungry, listening while the jungle sweats and birds scream in the high leaves. Nothing answers. Whatever called in the dark has gone back into the green, and the green has closed behind it.

My belly has less patience than my fear.

The pools near the channel are empty.

I work them one by one in the low tide, knees sunk in black mud, fingers searching under roots. The first pool gives me nothing. The second gives me a shell no bigger than my thumb. The third only leeches and foul water. I have eaten the easy edge down to silence.

That is another law.

If a place feeds you, it can be emptied.

By midday the hunger is sharp enough to make the world simple. I climb to the high stone above the channel and look for what else can be eaten.

That is where I see the river-swine.

They wallow in a brown basin where the channel widens. Four of them. Heavy bodies sunk to the belly in mud, dark backs shining, blunt heads rooting through the muck. Their tusks are yellow. Their little eyes are quick.

They are fat.

The sight of it hurts.

For a while I only watch. Hunger wants me down there now, teeth first, stupid as a starving dog. I make myself stay still. The swine are slow until they are not. When a heron lifts from the reeds, one jerks its head up and the others freeze. When danger passes, they settle again. Always near the deeper water. Always with an escape behind them.

The smallest has a torn ear and a scar white across its snout. The others shove it from the best mud. When it finds a fat root, a larger sow shoulders in and takes it. The torn-ear snaps once, bold and foolish, and gets driven backward for the trouble.

I should choose the weakest because weak meat costs less.

Instead I watch it too long.

That is dangerous. Hunger can use pity as well as greed.

To kill one, I have to stand between it and the channel.

Hands will not be enough.

I go back to the den and make a spear.

The branch I choose is hard, dark wood from deadfall that has not yet softened. I strip it with a stone flake. I grind one end against rock until the point forms. The work takes the rest of the day and most of the skin from my palms.

Blood stains the shaft.

I bind a sharp stone chip below the point with root fiber, pulling the cord tight with my teeth. It is an ugly weapon. Crooked. Crude. Mine.

By firelight I test the point against my thumb.

It bites.

Good.

The next morning I go down before dawn with mud on my skin and fear under my ribs.

The wind comes toward me. I keep it in my face so my scent runs behind. Every step is chosen. Roots. Stones. Places where mud will hold my foot instead of sucking it down with a sound.

The swine are where I left them.

I choose the smallest, though small is a lie. It is still heavier than I am twice over, thick with meat, its hide scarred by old fights. It roots at the edge of the basin three long strides from deep water.

"Sorry," I mouth.

The swine does not care. That is good. If it cared, I might fail.

I move around it.

Slow.

Low.

Between the swine and the channel.

It sees me.

The head snaps up. Mud flies from the tusks. For one breath we stare at each other across the brown shallows, two hungry lives caught in the same narrow place.

Then it charges.

It is not slow now.

The mud explodes under it. The body that looked heavy as a fallen log becomes speed and anger and yellow tusk. I plant the butt of the spear against the ground and throw all my weight behind it.

The point goes in.

The swine screams.

I nearly let go.

It is a terrible sound, high and broken, too close to a child. The shaft bucks in my hands. The animal slams into me and drives me backward. One tusk opens my thigh. Pain flashes white. Mud fills my mouth.

I hold on.

The swine fights for the water. I fight for the spear. There is no clean killing, no brave shape to it, only two bodies in black mud, one trying to live and one too hungry to let it.

Blood comes hot over my hands.

So much of it.

The smell fills me. Iron. Salt. Heat. The swine weakens by degrees, then all at once. Its legs fold. The scream becomes a wet grunt and then stops.

I stay on top of it long after it is dead, both hands locked to the spear, shaking so hard my teeth click.

I have killed fish. Crabs. Small lives I could hold in one hand.

This is different.

This had a voice.

The belly-hunger goes quiet in the face of all that meat. But something deeper opens when the blood runs. It does not want the swine as food. It wants the red warmth leaving the body. It wants the moment where fear changes sides.

That frightens me more than the tusk.

I drag the carcass up to the den over half a day, inch by inch, resting whenever my legs fail. The cut on my thigh bleeds, then tightens. It should swell. It should poison. Instead the edges pull together as if some hidden hand inside me is stitching too fast.

I hate that hidden hand.

I need it.

At the den I cut the swine open with stone and sharpened shell. I have never seen so much meat belong to one death. Fat under the hide. Dark muscle. Organs slick as river stones. I make a mess of the butchering, but the fire forgives ugly work.

I waste more than I want to. That hurts almost as much as the cut in my thigh. The hide tears wrong. The gut splits and fouls good meat. My hands shake with tiredness, and the knife-stone slips twice. Each mistake is a mouth I cannot feed later.

The river-swine teaches after death too.

Its hide is too thick for my poor edge until I learn to cut from inside out. Its sinews pull into cord if I scrape them clean and twist them while they dry. The bladder holds water once I rinse it enough to stop gagging. The ribs can become hooks. The jawbone makes a better scraper than the stone I have been using.

No part wants to be useful.

I have to fight each piece into purpose.

By midday I am slick to the elbows. Blood dries on my arms in brown scales. Fat gets under my nails. Flies gather at the edge of the clean space around my skin and hang there in a trembling wall, furious at being denied.

"Wait your turn," I tell them.

They answer by humming louder.

The lizard from the den returns at the smell. It appears on the stone wall with its half-tail lifted and its cloudy eye bright in the firelight. I toss it a scrap no bigger than a fingernail. It snatches the meat and retreats to a crack.

"Rent paid," I say.

It comes back for more within five breaths.

So it is a thief and a liar. I respect that. I feed it again.

The work makes me rich in ways I barely understand. Rich with grease for the fire. Rich with cord. Rich with bones that can become tools if my hands learn patience. Rich with more meat than I can eat before the rot comes asking for its share.

The richness frightens me.

In the hold, food meant someone had chosen who would weaken first. A crust passed to one boy made three others watch him with flat eyes. A cup of water held too long could start a fight. Enough was never only enough. Enough was danger wearing a kinder face.

I catch myself hiding strips of smoked meat in three places. Under the stone lip. Behind the dry bark. Inside a hollow root near the den. Then I catch myself checking them, again and again, as if hunger has hands and might steal while I look away.

At sunset, I take one strip from each hiding place and lay them together beside the fire.

All in one place.

My hands hate it.

I make them stay open.

"Mine," I say.

Not a whisper this time.

The word sounds too large under the stone roof. I almost look over my shoulder for the man who will punish it.

No one comes.

Only the lizard, bold with rent and lies, creeping toward the edge of the meat.

"No," I tell it.

It freezes with one foot raised.

For a moment we stare at each other across the fire, two thieves disappointed by the same law.

Then it backs away with great dignity and pretends the whole plan belonged to someone else.

I laugh until my ribs hurt.

By dark I have strips hung on green sticks over smoke, a pit scraped for scraps, and the skull set beyond the den where its eyes can look somewhere else. The torn ear lies flat against the mud.

I touch it once.

"You were hungry too," I say.

The words change nothing. I say them anyway.

That night I eat until my belly aches.

Fat runs down my wrist. Smoke gathers under the stone roof. Outside, the insects find the scraps and swarm over them, black and eager. They leave me untouched.

For the first time, my body is full.

The other hunger is not.

It watches from under my ribs, awake and unsatisfied.

Near midnight the jungle goes silent.

Not quiet. Silent.

The flies lift from the meat. The frogs stop. Even the leaves seem to hold their breath.

Something passes outside the den.

I see nothing. I hear weight placed carefully on wet ground. A slow foot. Another. Too heavy for swine, too quiet for anything so large. A smell slides through the wall: rot, old meat, and a heat like breath from a mouth full of teeth.

My hand finds the spear.

The hunter stops where the swine bled into the ground.

For a long time, nothing moves.

I lie in the dark with the fire low and my own blood going cold in me. The hunger that leaned toward killing all day curls down small and silent.

At last the weight moves away.

The jungle begins again by pieces. One insect. Then another. Then the frogs.

Sleep does not come.

In the morning I find the print.

It is sunk deep in the mud below the den wall, wider than my chest, with long toes and claws that cut the earth. Not swine. Not anything I know.

I crouch above it until the sun climbs high.

Yesterday I thought the swine was the largest danger in my world.

Today I know the swine was meat for something else.

# Chapter 7: The Wreck

Three days after the swine, the river brings me the smell of men.

Tar first. Then old wood. Rust. Salt caught in places salt should not be. Under all of it, bone.

I am hunting the high bank with the spear in my hand when the wind shifts. The scent touches my face and the whole past opens its eyes.

Ship.

I should go back to the den.

Instead I follow.

The river runs brown between walls of root and hanging vine. I keep to the bank where I can, cross where I must, and move slowly when the smell thickens. Strange scent means strange danger. I know that. But this one has hooks in me.

By evening the channel opens onto a wide mud reach, and there it is.

A ship lies broken on the bar.

Not the ship that carried me. Smaller. Older. Dead longer. Its hull is split. Vines climb through the ribs. A tree has grown out of the deck and lifted part of it toward the sky. The mast lies in the mud like a fallen bone, cloth rotted to strips.

I stand in the shallows and feel the world before the island pressing close.

Men made this.

Hands cut the wood. Hands bent iron into nails. Hands tied rope and raised cloth and built a hollow belly to carry other hands across the water.

Hands also locked chains.

I stay in the shallows at first.

I read the bar first.

The bones are everywhere.

At first the green hides them. Then my eyes learn the pattern and cannot stop seeing it. A rib cage full of leaves. A skull half-sunk in mud. A leg bone washed white beside a root. More up the bank. More under the broken hull.

Many men died here.

Not torn. Not butchered. Their bones do not show the great tooth or claw. They seem to have lain down and let the island take them, one after another, until the bar was a field of what they used to be.

They tried to make order before the end.

That is what hurts most.

A ring of stones surrounds a blackened fire pit near the bank. Three poles lean together above it, still tied at the top with rope gone soft as moss. A pot hangs from the poles, empty except for rainwater and two drowned beetles. Beside it lies a row of cups, set mouth-down on a plank as if someone meant to keep them clean for morning.

Morning came.

No hands lifted them.

Farther up the bar, under a roof of torn sailcloth, I find marks cut into the ship's broken ribs. Lines grouped in fives. Then more. Then more. A count kept by men who still believed counting would save them.

The last groups are crooked.

The knife that made them must have shaken.

Beside the count, another mark has been cut again and again by a different hand. A circle. A line through it. A thorn shape beyond me. The first is careful. The last is hacked so deep the rib has split around it.

Not counting.

Begging, maybe.

Or warning.

On the ground below the count marks lies a little wooden horse, one leg snapped off, its painted eye almost gone. A child toy. Or a father's memory of one. I pick it up and feel foolish grief for a person whose name I will never know.

The island killed men with plans, cups, toys, counts, and maps.

It did not only kill bodies.

It waited until hope became a smaller and smaller fire, then breathed on it.

I look at the scummed water. The fever-haze over the mud. The insects darkening the air. The rot that never sleeps.

The green killed them.

The same air I breathe. The same water I have drunk. The same biting clouds that will not touch me.

All these men died of the place I live in.

I cannot understand what that makes me.

The wreck creaks when I climb into it.

Inside, the smell is worse. Wet wood, old death, rust. Light falls green through the broken deck. Bones lie in the hold where men crawled away from rain and did not crawl out again.

I hate them a little for being dead.

I want one of them to lift his head. To curse. To spit. To look at me and prove that the world beyond the island still has human mouths in it.

Bones only grin.

I search anyway.

The green makes round and twisted shapes. Men make straight ones. I find those first: a rusted pot, a hook, a hinge, a buckle. A chain curled in the muck.

The chain stops me.

My hand is already on it before memory strikes. Iron at the wrist. Iron at the neck. The dark hold moving under my feet. A slaver's hand closing at the back of my neck with the calm of a man lifting property.

I drop the chain.

For a moment I am not in the wreck. I am below another deck with bodies packed close around me and no room to stand straight. I smell piss, fear, bilge, sickness. I hear the command-sound they used for me.

Then the tree above me drips water onto my shoulder, and I come back.

The chain lies in the mud.

The men who carried it are bones.

That helps.

Not enough.

Under the canted deck I find a chest soft with rot. It collapses when I pull it loose, spilling its hoard into the slime.

Skins, worked thin and marked in dark lines.

I know what they are before I know how. Marks that speak. Rows and rows of them. Meaning trapped in little shapes. I touch one line with my thumb and wait for the meaning to rise.

Nothing.

There was a time before the hold when marks might have opened for me. I feel the outline of that knowing, like a door behind a wall. I cannot reach the latch.

I roll the skins carefully.

A knife lies beneath them.

Real iron. Rusted, but not ruined. Wrapped well enough that the edge remains under the stain. I work it against wet plank until the metal shows clean. When I hold it, my hand closes around the grip as if remembering a lesson my head has lost.

A knife is a tooth made by men.

I keep it.

With the knife in my hand, I can open the ship further.

That is not the same as wanting to.

I cut a curtain of vine from the broken companionway and find the stairs below. The first step gives under my foot. The second holds. Air breathes up from beneath, thick with wet timber and a sweet mineral stink that coats my tongue.

The lower deck has drowned and emptied a hundred times with the tide. Mud lies in waves across the planks. Little white crabs move through the ribs of a dead man and vanish into his coat when my shadow falls over them.

I make myself go down.

A ship is a body. I learned that before I learned many kinder lessons. It has ribs, spine, belly, mouth, lungs of sail. Men crawl inside it and trust wood to keep the sea out. Men also build cages into bellies and call them cargo space.

This one has cages.

Not many. Three, built against the inner hull with iron bars red from age. The doors stand open. One has been bent outward by a force that twisted the hinges like soft root. Inside, the floor is scratched white beneath the mud.

Fingernails.

I know the work of fingernails on wood.

My own fingers curl around the knife so tightly the grip bites.

There are marks cut into the wall above the first cage. Not counting lines. Words, maybe. A hand tried to carve them deep and failed before the end. I trace the first shape. Nothing opens in me. The marks remain marks.

In the second cage I find a bowl nailed to the floor.

That makes my throat close.

A bowl loose on a floor means a man can choose where to sit while he eats. A bowl nailed down means someone has decided how far the neck may move.

I back out and stand in the passage until the ship stops leaning around me.

There are no living slavers here. Only their iron. Only their design.

That should matter.

It does not matter enough.

Behind the cages, half under fallen planks, I find a narrow locker with a ring set in the lid. It takes both hands and a curse to open. Inside are bundles wrapped in waxed cloth, better kept than anything else in the wreck.
