More skins.

A little brass tube green with age.

Three glass beads, blue as deep water.

And a child's shoe.

Not rotten. Not whole either. Small. Stiff. One side cut open, maybe to free a swollen foot. The leather has been rubbed soft by use before the sea ever touched it.

I set it down carefully.

The ship creaks above me.

For a moment I hear a child breathing in the dark.

No. Not hear. Remember. Or fear. The two are close in places like this.

I lift the brass tube and shake it. Something taps inside. The end cap resists, then gives with a squeal. A scrap of skin slides into my palm, rolled tight around a sliver of black wood.

Marks cover it in tiny rows.

At the bottom, stamped in dark red wax, is the eye-and-thorn.

My teeth ache.

The mark on the wax is not drawn by a shaking sailor. It is clean. Official. A seal for men who expect doors to open.

That last certainty is not born in this wreck.

I know it from somewhere else.

A room with red tile.

Rain against shutters.

A woman's hand pushing my head down behind a chest.

Quiet now.

The memory vanishes so fast I gasp after it.

The knife point trembles in my hand.

Above me, water drips through the deck one drop at a time.

I wait for the woman's face to come back.

It does not.

Only the pressure of my name remains, hard behind my teeth, and a certainty I hate: the mark knew me before the island did.

The knife changes the wreck around me.

A moment ago the dark corners were mouths. Now they are corners. The vines across the deck were ropes waiting for my ankles. Now they are vines I can cut if they take hold. Iron does not make me brave. It only gives my fear a point to gather behind.

I test the edge on a strip of rotten sail.

The cloth opens.

I grin before I mean to. "Good tooth," I whisper.

It is a foolish line to say to a knife.

I have said worse to roots.

The last find is small enough to hide in my fist.

A dark metal disc on a cord gone nearly to dust. I rub the mud away and see the mark worked into it.

An open eye.

Through the eye, a thorn.

The breath leaves me.

The mark is not alone.

When I wipe more mud from the chest's broken lid, I find it burned there too, larger, half-eaten by rot. On a strip of leather. On the handle of the dead men's seal. Cut small into the back of the wooden horse, where a child's hand would cover it without knowing.

The mark was on everything they meant to keep.

Or everything that meant to keep them.

I know this mark.

Not like I know chain. Not like I know knife. Deeper than either. The eye and thorn live somewhere under the floor of me, below the ship, below the hold, near the place where my mother's voice keeps my name.

I grip the disc until its edge bites my palm.

Where did I see you?

The wreck gives no answer.

I lift the disc to my face. Eye. Thorn. Looking and pierced. I feel meaning there, close enough to hurt, but the words will not come. The memory will not break surface.

It is mine.

That much I know.

I tie the cord around my neck and tuck the disc against my chest. The metal is cold at first. Then it takes my warmth.

I should leave then.

Instead I search for one more answer.

That is how hunger works when it is not hunger for food. It makes a hand reach past sense. It tells a boy that one more door might explain the mark, one more plank might return the woman's face, one more scrap of writing might give the dead back their voices.

The captain's cabin has collapsed on one side. A tree root spears through the roof and down into the desk, pinning wood to wood as if the island has stabbed the room and left the blade there. Shelves lean drunkenly. A map has rotted into lace on the wall. The sea has eaten the ink until coastlines become bruises.

On the desk lies a cup.

Mouth-down.

Clean.

No mud inside. No insect eggs. No green mold fur.

I stop in the doorway.

Everything else in the cabin has surrendered to the island. The cup has not. It waits on the desk as if a hand placed it there this morning and expects to return before the tea cools.

The eye-and-thorn is carved into its base.

Small. Hidden. A mark for the hand that lifts it, not for guests.

I touch the cup with the knife point.

It tips over.

Dry red dust spills out.

Not tea. Not rust.

Powdered blood.

It spreads across the desk in a thin fan, and every biting insect in the cabin lifts at once. Mosquitoes from the wall. Ants from a seam. Little pale flies from a skull under the chair. They rise toward the dust, then stop as the disc on my chest warms against my skin.

The swarm hangs between me and the desk, trembling.

The warmth under my ribs answers.

Not the same as when the swine died. Not hungry. Older. Listening.

I back away from the cabin.

The swarm does not follow.

Neither does the memory.

When I leave the wreck, the sun is low and the bar of bones shines pale in the green light. I carry the knife, the marked skins, and the eye-and-thorn against my breastbone.

Men came here with iron, rope, marks, and chains.

The island killed them.

It has not killed me.

I walk back upriver in the dark with the dead men's knife at my hip and their unanswered mark on my chest.

Behind my teeth, I hold my own name tight.

My name.

The name does not explain the mark.

But it reminds me there are still pieces of me the world has not taken.

As I leave, something knocks once inside the wreck.

Not wood settling.

Too clean.

I turn with the knife in my hand.

The broken hold gapes black beneath the tree-grown deck. Water drips. A fly moves over a skull and lifts away. Nothing else stirs.

Then the knock comes again.

From below.

Slow. Patient. Almost polite.

I stay out of the hold.

I tell myself old ships make old sounds. I tell myself water moves trapped wood. I tell myself many true facts while I walk away faster than I came.

Behind me, the wreck keeps its dark mouth open.

The eye-and-thorn lies warm against my chest now.

Not from sun.

# Chapter 8: The People

There are people in the green.

I see them from the high ridge after a hunt, when the morning steam lifts enough to show the low country. At first I think they are shadows moving under the canopy. Then one steps into a break of light and the shape of it goes through me like cold water.

Upright.

Not human.

Not beast.

People.

The word rises before I can stop it.

They move along both sides of a brown channel in a loose line. Big shoulders. Long arms. Dark wet hide. Sometimes they go on two legs, sometimes on knuckles and feet, switching as the ground asks. They are larger than men, heavier through the shoulders, but their movement has thought in it.

A herd follows pressure.

These choose.

I flatten against the stone.

The old fear comes fast. Hands. Chains. A voice making a command out of my flesh. People are the shape of want, but they are also the shape of ownership. I learned both in the hold, where every voice belonged to someone stronger.

My legs want the den.

The ache under my ribs wants the ridge.

I stay.

For three days I watch them from hiding.

They have made their place on a shelf of pale stone across the low country. The shelf rises above the wet green where the air moves and the sun reaches first. Great trees lean over it. Vines hang like ropes. Smoke lifts there in the evening.

It is not a nest. That is the first truth I must unlearn. Nests are made for hiding. This place is made for returning. Paths come to it from many sides: a worn lip down to the channel, a slant of stone toward the fruit trees, a narrow crack where the small ones squeeze through and the large ones pretend not to see. The paths are not straight, but all of them know the fire.

Smoke.

They keep fire.

Not a coal in a fist of fiber. Not a little flame coaxed under a stone roof. A true kept fire, red at the heart, fed by many hands. At night it glows against the rock and paints their bodies in copper.

They have a king. I know him without needing the word. The largest one sits highest, gray at the muzzle, old scars crossing his chest. Others make room around him. Their eyes go to him before they move. Power bends the air the same in every tribe.

Near him waits another, younger, with a pale scar down his side and ambition in the way he stands too still. I have seen that stillness in animals deciding whether to bite.

There are wounded among them. One drags a leg, and the others slow for her. No herd I know would do that for long. The jungle teaches the strong to leave the weak. These people disobey the jungle.

There are small ones too.

That is what undoes me.

Little dark bodies cling to backs and chests. They tumble over stone, get scooped up, get pushed away, come back again. A broad-handed female lifts one by the scruff and folds it into her lap while it kicks and complains. Another carries two, one clinging to each side like burrs.

I watch until my throat hurts.

No one ever lifted me that way after the hold.

The dead have a place among them.

On the highest clean shelf, where the sun lays its first white hand, they set the dead out in rows. Not abandoned. Not eaten. Set. A big gray female sits beside them for hours, touching one still face, then another. Her shoulders move with a sound I cannot hear from so far, but I know grief when I see the shape of it.

They keep fire.

They keep children.

They keep their dead.

Tribe.

The word comes slowly, from the old place under the old pain. I cannot remember learning it. I only know it fits.

The fit hurts.

A word can be a blade if there is nowhere to put it.

I learn their days before I learn their names.

At first I think they all move by hunger. Dawn comes, and the adults go down to the channels or into the green. Noon comes, and they sleep with one eye open in whatever shade the stone gives. Evening comes, and meat or fruit or shell is brought to the fire. That much any pack might do.

Then I see the rest.

One broad male with a missing thumb sits every morning near the little crack where the smallest ones squeeze through. He does not hunt. He does not tend the fire. He watches the children with a patience so deep it looks like sleep until one climbs too high, then his hand is there before falling becomes harm. The small ones climb over his shoulders when they pass. He grumbles at each of them. They ignore him with joy.

An old female with one cloudy eye breaks shellfish with a stone, but she gives every third shell to the lame one who drags her leg. Not every second. Not when others are watching. Every third, as exact as tide. The lame one never thanks her. The old one never asks thanks. They both pretend the shells arrive by weather.

A young hunter with red-brown streaks in his hide brings back fruit, not meat, and gives it first to the mothers with milk. A larger male cuffs him for taking too much from the high branches. The young one bares his teeth. The mothers make a low sound together. The larger male backs away with pride stiff in every joint, pretending he has chosen mercy.

It is a people, then. Not only fire and noise. Rules. Favors. Small thefts. Shame. Pride. All the hidden ropes that tie a tribe together and trip anyone who cannot see them.

I watch until my knees ache and my hunger complains.

The high stones are not peaceful. That is another truth I must learn. The strong shove. The small bite. Two young males wrestle until one has blood on his lip and the watching adults do nothing. A mother snarls at another mother for letting a child steal marrow from her pile. A gray male takes the best place by the fire because no one dares ask him to move.

But even their cruelty has edges.

The loser of the wrestling is mocked, then given water. The thief child is cuffed, then fed. The gray male sleeps, and one of the little ones paints his foot with berry juice while the others shake from silent laughter. When he wakes, he roars so loudly birds leap from the trees, but he leaves the red mark on his foot all evening.

I have no practice with people who can hurt and keep in the same breath.

They argue over water too.

A spring seeps from the stone above their fire shelf, not much more than a shining thread, but the Vorth have shaped a hollow beneath it with clay and leaf. Rain fills the world, yet clean water still has order. Children cannot put muddy hands in the hollow. Hunters drink after mothers. Wounded drink before the proud, unless the wound is from foolishness and everyone saw it happen.

I learn this because a young male with a fresh bite on his shoulder tries to drink before the lame female.

The missing-thumb watcher blocks him.

The young male bares his teeth. He is larger by half. The watcher is old, broad, and missing strength where the thumb should be. For a breath I think blood will come.

The watcher points to the bite. Then to the young male's own teeth. Then to a laughing pair of youths with blood still on their knuckles. He makes a dry sound that carries.

Fool wound.

The children repeat it at once because children are mercy's enemies when mockery is free.

The young male endures this for three breaths, then gives way to the lame female with rage crawling under his hide. She drinks without thanking him. The watcher drinks last, when the hollow has nearly emptied and must fill again drop by drop.

That is when I first understand that the weakest are not kept by kindness alone.

They are kept by rule, by witnesses, by old hands willing to stand where young strength wants to pass.

No wonder Voss-Marr watches rules the way I watch snares.

A tribe is made of them. A ruler who cannot hold rules cannot hold people. A stranger who changes rules changes the tribe.

I cannot know this fully yet.

But some part of me hears the spring drip and understands that water can start wars before anyone is thirsty.

The dead shelf teaches me most.

At dawn the gray female goes up with a bowl of water and a bundle of leaves. Others follow only to the first step below, then wait. A small one tries to climb after her and is pulled back by the missing-thumb male. The child wails. The old female looks down, says one sharp sound, and the child goes quiet as if a law has touched its spine.

She washes the faces of the dead.

Not because they are dirty. Rain has already washed them. She does it because the hand remembers what the mouth cannot ask for anymore. She wets the leaves, wipes eyelids, ears, fingers, the hollows where the living smell gathers and then leaves. Each touch is slow. No hurry. No hunger.

When she finishes, she lays a small gift beside each body. A shell. A red stone. A stripped twig bound in grass. One dead male gets a broken tooth. The old female presses it into his palm and makes a sound so low I feel it through the ridge more than hear it.

The tribe below answers.

Every throat. Even the children.

A single rough note rises, held until the air seems to bow under it. I feel the sound in my teeth. It is not weeping. It is not song. It is a weight carried by many backs so one back does not break.

I press my hand over the disc at my chest.

In the hold, the dead went overboard if the slavers were not hungry enough to sell them sick. In the jungle, the dead became beetles, root, heat, smell. Here the dead remain people for a little while. They are given morning. They are given hands.

The wanting in me changes shape.

It is no longer only hunger for warmth.

It is hunger to be counted after I am gone.

On the fourth day, one comes down alone.

Young. Not small, but not fully grown. Long-limbed, quick, curious in a way that would get a lesser creature killed. It leaves the high stones when the tide pulls back and descends toward the channel below my ridge.

My channel.

My mud.

I should retreat.

Instead I follow from above, silent through the broken green.

The young one crouches at the bared stones and begins to glean.

It turns rocks with care. Snatches crabs before they fold away. Picks pale shellfish from roots. It works the low tide as I worked it when I first came ashore, with patience and hunger and hands that know the difference between bad mud and food.

Then it finds one of my old shell piles under a root.

The sight strikes through me. I left those shells there three nights ago, cracked and scraped clean, a secret mark of my own passing. The young one crouches over them. It sniffs the pile, tilts its head, and looks up toward the ridge.

Toward me.

I hold my breath until my ribs ache.

It could call. The whole tribe could pour down with stones and hands and teeth. Instead the young one picks through the shells and takes a piece I missed, a stubborn curl of meat hidden deep in a spiral. It eats my mistake, makes a pleased grunt, and keeps gleaning.

The insult is so small and so perfect that I nearly make a sound.

The sight cuts me more deeply than fear.

I thought the island taught me alone.

Here is another creature reading the same lesson.

A call comes from the high stones.

The young one lifts its head and answers.

The sound is shaped. Not human words, but language all the same. Need, warning, affection, place. A line thrown across the green from the high fire to the lone forager, saying: we know where you are. Come back.

My own mouth opens.

Nothing comes out but a rough breath.

I could say my name. I have it. I have kept it through hunger, shipwreck, fever, and the hold. It waits behind my broken teeth, warm and hidden.

But a kept name is not the same as a shared one.

To throw it across the channel would be to hand it to strangers. To people with hands large enough to close around me. To a tribe that might keep children or might keep slaves if the hunger turned.

My tongue stays still.

My name remains where my mother put it.

The young one gathers its food and climbs back toward the fire. Halfway up, an older voice calls again, sharper this time. The young one hurries, and when it reaches the shelf, another Vorth cuffs it lightly and then pulls it close.

Punishment and welcome in one motion.

I know neither.

I know what a blow means. I know what arms mean. I have never seen both answer the same mistake.

That night I sit in my den beside a smaller fire than theirs and listen to the far sounds of the tribe: low calls, cracking wood, the small ones' thin cries, the deeper rumble of adults settling. The eye-and-thorn rests cold against my chest.

I want to go to them.

I want never to be seen.

Both wants are true. Both have teeth.

Before sleep takes me, I hear one small voice cry out on the far stone. A larger voice answers at once.

Where.

Here.

The line crosses the dark and does not break.

# Chapter 9: Taken In

The fever finds me at the channel.

For six days I watch the young one come down with the tide and climb back with its gleaning. I learn its path. I learn where it pauses, where it looks, which calls make it hurry and which make it answer with annoyance.

On the fifth day it leaves three cracked shells on a flat stone beneath my ridge.

Not waste. Not forgotten. Set.

I stare at them until the tide comes in.

The meat is gone, but the gesture remains, and gestures are more dangerous than knives because they ask you to guess the hand behind them. I leave the shells untouched. All night I think of them sitting white under moonlight, an offering or a trap or a joke made by a creature that should not know I am there.

On the seventh day, the island turns on me.

It begins as heat behind the eyes. Then the throat closes. The gut knots. My legs go weak beneath me while the sun is still high. I drink rainwater from a leaf and tell myself the wrongness in my blood will win again.

It always has.

That does not make the burning easier.

By dawn I am out of the den and crawling downhill. Fever hates walls. It wants open air, running water, mud cool against the skin. I know better than to expose myself, but knowing is a small lamp in a storm.

The channel stones are bare when I reach them.

I collapse in the mud and drink like an animal.

That is where the young one finds me.

It stops at the edge of the stones with a crab in one hand.

I lift my head. The world swims. The young one's dark shape doubles, blurs, becomes one again. Its small eyes fix on me. Not hungry. Not cruel. Afraid, maybe. Curious.

My lips peel back.

The sound in my chest is meant to warn it away. It comes out cracked and weak.

The young one answers with a low note.

Soft. Rising and falling. I have heard the same sound from the high stones when the small ones cry. It is not command. Not threat. A sound for calming another living creature.

That frightens me more.

Cruelty I understand. Kindness has too many hidden doors.

The young one sees the flat stone where the shells were.

The tide took them. Or the young one thinks I did. Its gaze flicks from the stone to my mouth, my hands, the mud dragged by my knees. Some bright thought moves behind its eyes, too quick for fever to catch.

Then it backs away, turns, and runs up the green toward the tribe.

To bring the many.

I try to crawl.

My hand finds a root. My knees slide. Mud fills the fresh cuts on my shin. The green tilts around me, bright and sick. I get three lengths from the water before my arms fail.

Better the jungle than chains.

That is my last clear thought.

Then the fever takes the light.

I wake against warmth.

Not stone warmed by sun. Not rot. Not the little fire.

Living warmth.

It moves under me with breath. A slow heart beats against my ear. A heavy arm lies across my back, not pinning, not crushing, only holding me in place while I shake.

I know hands.

I know the hand that owns. The hand that closes on the neck. The hand that says stillness or pain, obedience or pain, breath or pain. I know that hand down to the bone.

This is not that hand.

The arm over me is vast. It could break me without effort. Instead it keeps me from rolling into the cold edge of the stone. It holds warmth around my fever and makes the soft rising sound again and again, until even the frightened animal part of me has to admit the truth.

I am being kept.

I burn for two days among the Vorth.

I have not learned that word yet. It will come later from their mouths, one sound at a time. At first they are only the people of the high stones. The many. The ones with fire.

They lay me beneath leaning trees near the shelf of the dead. Their bodies move around me in pieces: a young face peering too close, a gray hand pushing it back, the shadow of the king above the fire, the scarred ambitious one watching from the edge as if measuring whether I am omen or meat.

Mostly there is the keeper.

She is old, gray along the muzzle, heavy in the chest, with one torn ear and hands broad enough to cover my whole back. She smells of smoke, wet hide, milk-sour grief, and old leaves. When the fever throws me, she gathers me in. When I snarl, she waits. When I sleep, I wake to find her still there.

She has lost a child.

I learn that before I learn her name.

On the clean stone above us lies a small body, laid with the others. The keeper goes to it at dawn. She touches its still head, then her own chest. Her sound changes there. It deepens. Breaks. There are no words in it I know, but grief does not need translation.

Afterward she comes back and folds herself around me.

I am not her lost child.

She knows that.

I know that.

Still, there is a place in her shaped for holding, and there is a place in me shaped by not being held. For a few fevered nights those two emptinesses fit close enough to save my life.

The tribe does not all agree.

Fever carries me in and out, but I see enough. The missing-thumb male brings water once and sets it near Kala without looking at me. The one-clouded female brings bitter leaves and spits in the dust after she lays them down. A younger mother with milk-heavy breasts stands above us for a long while with her own small one clinging to her side. She looks at the dead shelf. Then at me. Then at Kala.

Her sound is soft.

Soft does not mean kind.

Kala's answer is softer.

The younger mother leaves shaking her head, and that night she sleeps with her back to us. In the morning she still sends a strip of mashed fruit through a child too small to be blamed for kindness.

I understand none of the words.

I understand the shape.

Kala's arms around me are not free. Every hour she spends keeping my fever from pulling me apart is an hour she is not sitting beside the small dead one. Every scrap she presses into my mouth comes from some other mouth's patience. Every time she shields me from curious hands, she reminds the tribe that her own child is beyond shielding.

Once, at dusk, the old seer calls from the dead shelf.

Kala rises. I feel the absence of her before my eyes open. Cold enters the hollow she leaves. I hear her climb the stone, slow for once, as if each step is a choice with teeth in it.

The dead-sound begins above.

I cannot see the ritual from where I lie, only the red edge of sunset and the shapes of heads turning upward. The tribe answers. The note moves through the stones and into my sick bones. Kala's voice is in it, larger and more broken than the rest.

Then my fever turns.

The old hold opens. The planks are wet. A man is laughing with a rope in his hand. I am small under him, too hot, too cold, and my hidden name is a coal in my mouth. I claw at the stone beneath me and make a sound that tears my throat.

The dead-sound stops.

Kala comes down before the last note is finished.

That is the cost I remember when the fever clears. Not the fruit. Not the water. Not the glares from those who think a stranger has been given too much.

She leaves her dead to hold the living.

No one says this to me.

No one needs to.

On the second morning after that, I wake enough to see what leaving costs.

Kala carries water to the dead shelf in a bowl of folded bark. Her steps are heavy. Fever has made the world bright and cruel, and every movement cuts itself into me. I watch her climb. Watch the old seer move aside. Watch the one-clouded female turn her face away.

The small body on the shelf has changed.

Not much. Enough.

The wet heat has begun its work at the edges. The leaves around it have browned. Tiny flies lift and settle. Kala makes a sound I have never heard from her before, not loud, not broken, but empty in a way that pulls the air from my chest.

She was away too long.

For me.

The seer reaches to help.

Kala refuses with one sharp motion.

She washes the little face herself. She cleans the ears, the fingers, the soft folds where damp has gathered. Her hands remain gentle. Her shoulders shake only once. When the flies return, she drives them away with such fury that one child below begins to cry.

I try to rise.

My arms fail.

The young gleaner, the one who found me, sees and scrambles to my side. It pushes me down with both hands, then bares its teeth as if daring me to disobey. The gesture is so like a tiny bad copy of Kala that I almost laugh, but fever has stolen laughter for now.

Above, Kala finishes the washing.

She puts no shell beside the small hand. The shell is on my chest.

Instead she breaks one bead from a cord around her own wrist and lays that beside the dead child. The cord is old, dark with wear. When the bead leaves it, the remaining knots hang loose.

The one-clouded female sees.

Her mouth tightens.

She does not turn away this time.

Later, when Kala comes back to me, one wrist is bare where the cord used to hold its pattern. I touch the empty place before I think.

She lets me.

Then she takes my hand and presses it flat against my own chest, over shell and mark and heart, as if to say: know what was spent here.

I know.

Not enough. Never enough.

But I begin.

It is not simple. Nothing living is.

Once, when the fever throws me hard and I strike her jaw with my fist, she catches my wrist. Her grip closes and the old hold opens under me all at once: dark planks, iron stink, breath counted out by another's hand. I bite her.

The tribe snarls.

She does not.

She lets my teeth stay in her hide until I taste blood, hers and mine together. Then she loosens one finger at a time and lays my wrist against my own chest, where my heart is hammering like a trapped bird. Her sound comes low and stern.

Still.

Not obedience. Not pain. A wall put between me and the place I have fallen into.

I hate her for needing it.

I cling to her before the hate has finished.

When the fever breaks, the tribe sees.

The green's sickness has taken their own. I understand it from the way they watch my skin cool, the way they touch the air near me but not me, the way whispers pass from throat to throat. They carried me up expecting death. I wake instead. Too soon. Too clean.

The island tried to eat me in front of witnesses and failed.

That changes the air.

The king watches me longer.

The scarred one watches me harder.

The small ones creep nearer until the keeper bares her teeth and scatters them.

The young gleaner is among them.

It holds one of the white shells from the channel. I know the chipped lip of it. Somehow it found the same piece the tide took and brought it up the stone. It sets the shell beside my hand, then darts backward when I move.

I should not care.

My fingers close around it anyway.

Then the oldest comes.

She is nearly white around the face, all angles under loose hide, her eyes clouded but not blind. The tribe makes room for her. Even the king lowers his head a little.

The old one crouches beside me.

I force myself not to move.

She studies my face, my hands, the healed cuts, the fever-sweat drying on my skin. Then her gaze drops to my chest.

To the disc.

The eye-and-thorn.

Her hand stops in the air.

For the first time since I saw the Vorth, all their sounds die at once.

The old one reaches with one long finger and touches the mark. Not my skin. The mark. Her breath leaves her in a low note that makes the keeper go still beside me.

She knows it.

The wanting in me rises so fast I nearly grab her wrist.

Tell me.

The words are in my head, useless. I have no shared speech. No bridge. Only my stare and the metal at my chest and the old one looking at me as if a dead story has crawled out of the jungle wearing a boy's face.

She bows her head.

Not much. Just enough.

The others see it.

Something passes through them. Fear, reverence, suspicion. Maybe all three. The scarred one's lips draw back. The king's eyes narrow. The keeper's arm tightens around me.

The mark still keeps its answer.

But now I know it means something here.

Meaning is not safety. I learn that from the way the scarred one watches the bow settle over me.

After the old seer leaves, the scarred one steps closer.

The tribe parts for him in a way it does not part for curiosity. He is not king, but the path opens as if practicing. His side scar catches firelight. His hands hang loose. No weapon. No lifted lip. Only attention sharpened to a point.

Kala shifts between us.

The scarred one looks at her, then at the shell beside my hand, then at the wet hides under me. His sound is low. Too measured to be a snarl. He points to the dead shelf, where the small body waits in morning's old light. Then he points to me.

Even fever cannot hide the meaning.

Which dead are you keeping?

The air tightens.

Kala's shoulders rise. For one breath she looks old enough to crumble. Then she takes the white shell from my hand and sets it on my chest, directly over the eye-and-thorn. Her palm covers both. Metal, shell, heart.

Her answer is a growl too deep for softness.

This living one.

The scarred one does not strike. That would be easier to hate. He only gives a short sound that makes several adults lower their eyes.

Waste.

No. Not exactly. The word is not mine yet, but I know its taste. Meat used where meat should not be used. A hand busy where the tribe needs it elsewhere. A mother's grief turned sideways.

Part of me agrees with him.

That is the worst of it.

If I were watching from the ridge, I might think the same. The green kills. Children die. Food rots. Fire needs tending. Why spend strength on a fevered stranger with a dead man's mark?

Voss-Marr's anger has a clean bone inside it before I know his name.

Almost right is more dangerous than wrong.

The old seer gives me weight I did not ask for. The keeper gives me warmth I have not learned how to carry. The young gleaner gives me a shell. The king gives me nothing.

All of them are gifts.

All of them can become teeth.

That night, the kept fire burns low and red. I lie against the keeper's side while the tribe settles around us. Their voices move through the dark, rough and living. The small ones sleep in knots. The dead rest on the high stone. Smoke climbs into the stars.

I go behind my teeth and touch the name hidden there.

My name.

I could give it to the keeper. Part of me wants to. Wants to set the sound in her great careful hands and see if she holds it the way she holds me.

I keep it back.

Not yet.

A name is not the first gift you give strangers.

So I keep mine hidden and take what she gives instead: the gentling sound, low and warm, laid over me whenever the old fear rises.

Its meaning is still closed to me.

I know what it does.

For the first time since the hold, I sleep among other bodies and wake unchained.

# Chapter 10: Among Them

I learn the high stones the way I learned the jungle.

First with my feet.

There are places I may stand and places I may not. The dead have the highest clean shelf, where the morning sun strikes first. No living foot goes there except the keeper's and the old seer's. The king has a bare lip of stone above the fire, and the air around it is as clear as if a wall stands there.

I learn that by crossing it once.

A great arm comes down across my chest. Not a blow. A boundary. The Vorth who stops me turns me aside without anger, the way I would move a branch from a path.

The king watches from above.

Gull-Marr. I learn the sound later. At first he is only weight and old silver hide, a scarred muzzle, flat eyes that do not warm. He does not snarl at me. He does not welcome me. He looks at me as he might look at a strange bone washed up by rain, something to be left where it lies until its use or danger proves itself.

Kala chose me.

The seer bowed to the mark at my chest.

The king has chosen nothing.

That matters.

The second country is sound.

The Vorth tongue is rough in the throat and soft at the edges when they want it soft. I steal it one piece at a time. The gentling note Kala gives me is not one sound but many. One means be still. One means back from the edge. One means where are you, answered by here.

They throw those calls across stone and green all day.

Where.

Here.

Where.

Here.

A line of sound tying one life to another so no one falls out of the world unnoticed.

I have lived too long without such lines.

The small ones teach me fastest because they have not yet learned fear properly. They climb over me, poke my scars, tug my hair, bare their teeth in delight when I bare mine back. They put their faces close to mine and give me names for everything.

Fire.

Water.

Stone.

Sky.

Dead.

Kala.

They also teach me games.

At first I cannot understand spending strength for no meat, no safety, no escape. A small one throws a round nut at another small one's head and then runs shrieking when the nut comes back twice as hard. Three of them chase each other around the fire until an adult cuffs the slowest into the dust. Two hang by their hands from a low branch, swinging their feet at each other and laughing when both fall.

I watch with the deep suspicion I give to all waste.

Marrow sees me watching.

He climbs the leaning root above the fire and hangs by one good hand and one crooked one, his bad shoulder riding high, his teeth bared in challenge. The small ones hoot at him. He swings, lets go, catches a vine with his curled hand, and lands badly enough that he rolls three times down the slope and comes up covered in ash.

The whole circle erupts.

Not alarm. Not anger.

Delight.

Marrow bows as if he meant every part of it.

I feel the bright break in my chest again.

He points to me, then to the root.

No.

He points harder.

The small ones turn toward me with their bright hungry faces. Even Kala watches from the dead shelf, pretending not to. I climb the root because refusing would make Marrow unbearable for a whole night.

The first swing goes well.

The second does not.

My hand slips. My heel catches a vine. I turn in the air and hit the dust flat on my back. For one breath the old fear strikes. Down means exposed. Down means boot, hand, hook.

Then Marrow drops beside me and copies the fall badly on purpose, all limbs and teeth and dust. The small ones collapse around us, laughing so hard one of them hiccups.

He does it again when I try to sit.

This time he lands with his rear in the ash.

That is a mistake. The ash is cold on top and hot underneath. He leaps up with a yelp, slaps both hands to his backside, forgets the bad hand does not open cleanly, and turns in a frantic circle trying to see the wound on a part of himself no eye can reach.

The small ones fall into each other.

I laugh until my chest hurts.

Kala makes the stern sound from the dead shelf, but her mouth is tucked in at the corner. I see it. Marrow sees it too and bows toward her with both hands spread, one crooked and one clean, as if he has offered the tribe a great kill.

I lie there under a sky full of green leaves and smoke and Vorth laughter, and nothing bad happens.

No one owns the fall.

No one uses it.

It is only foolishness, and for that reason it feels rarer than meat.

After that they decide I am allowed to lose.

This is a great honor among children, and a terrible burden.

The smallest ones come at me with games as if games are raids. A round-bellied child called Nomi by the others brings smooth stones and sets them in a crooked line. A long-armed boy called Brak knocks half of them aside and howls when she bites him for it. Luta, who has one white splash over her eye, explains the rules in a flood of sound, realizes I understand none of it, and explains again louder.

Marrow watches my face with grave disappointment.

Then he shows me.

The game is simple enough to wound pride. Each player throws a nut along the stone and tries to strike the farthest shell without knocking over the little tower in the middle. If the tower falls, everyone else may throw leaves at you until Kala makes the enough-sound.

I am bad at it.

The children delight in this with pure hearts.

Nomi hits the far shell twice. Brak misses everything and claims victory anyway. Luta throws with her feet for no reason except that she can. Marrow misses on purpose the first time to make them laugh, misses by accident the second time, then becomes so offended by the nut that he stalks it across the stone and slaps it into place with his curled hand.

It works.

The shell clicks.

The tower stands.

The small ones go silent.

Marrow looks at his hand as if it has betrayed him by being useful.

Then he lifts his chin and struts the length of the fire shelf.

Nomi copies him. Brak copies Nomi. Luta copies Brak badly. Soon half the children are strutting in a line, shoulders hunched, chins high, every one of them pretending to have Marrow's crooked hand and rocking walk.

My chest tightens.

Mockery can be a hook. I know the bite of it. But Marrow turns, sees them, and bares his teeth in a grin so wide it becomes answer. He makes his walk larger. Ridiculous. Proud. A king of crookedness. The children shriek and follow.

By evening they have made his weakness into a game they must practice to play well.

It does not erase cruelty.

But it steals one knife from it.

Later, when Luta trips and splits her lip on the stone, Brak laughs too hard. Blood runs down her chin. Her face folds toward tears, not from pain, but from the sudden loneliness that comes when laughter turns sharp.

Marrow reaches her first.

He shoves Brak with his good shoulder, not hard enough to start a fight he cannot finish, hard enough to make the laughing stop. Then he presses his curled hand over his own mouth and makes the same foolish, wounded face Luta has made, exaggerating until even Luta snorts through blood.

He gives her the last nut from the game.

Not because he is sweet.

Because he knows the exact moment when a circle becomes a pit, and he refuses to let her fall into it.

I see him more clearly then.

He is not only the crooked one kept warm by my naming. He is a reader of hurts. Small ones learn fast or they do not last. Marrow has learned where shame enters. Sometimes he opens the door himself for laughter. Sometimes he bars it with his shoulder.

They say her name often. Two falling notes. Ka-la. When they need comfort, they call it. When the dead must be tended, the adults call it. When I hear it enough, the great gray female beside me stops being only warmth, only keeper, only the arm that held fever from my bones.

She becomes Kala.

A name changes what the eye can hold.

There is one small one who comes more than the rest.

He is crooked. One shoulder rides high. One hand curls inward and will not open cleanly. His walk rocks side to side until suddenly it becomes speed, and then he is through a gap before any larger creature thinks to close it.

The tribe has a sound for him. Short. Flat. Thrown downward.

Leavings.

I know the shape of that sound before I know its meaning. I have been named downward. I have worn sounds made to push someone out of the circle.

The crooked small one does not fear me. He rides my chest and names the world into my face, waiting for me to take each sound. When I stay silent, he gives another. Fire. Stone. Hand. Eye. Kala. My name holds still behind my teeth, silent and hidden, while this small wrong-made creature fills the air between us with names.

He is greedy with it. If I learn one sound, he demands two. If I shape a word badly, he shoves his face against mine and shows me his tongue and teeth until I do it better. When I fail too long, he throws himself backward in despair, as if the whole tribe has been cursed with the stupidest soft-skin ever dragged from a channel.

I begin to look forward to disappointing him.

One night, while he is asleep against my ribs and Kala hums the stilling note above us, I answer.

Not with my name.

With her sound.

Low. Broken. Barely shaped.

The noise scrapes my throat raw. I have used my voice for warning, breath, pain. Not for return. Not for gentleness.

Kala stops breathing for one beat.

Then she makes the sound again.

I make it back.

Better this time.

Across the fire, the old seer lifts his milk-pale eyes.

The tribe hears.

I have not given them my name. I have not opened the last hidden door. But a sound has gone out of me toward another living creature and landed without becoming a command, a hook, or a chain.

That is enough to change the night.

Later, the crooked small one wakes and presses his curled hand against my throat, where the sound came from. He gives his downward tribe-noise, then points to himself.

I shake my head.

That is not what I will call him.

I reach into the old human tongue, the one beneath the slavers' commands, and find a word from bone.

"Marrow."

It comes out rough, but I mean it.

Marrow: the rich center the strong crack the bone to reach, then miss because they are careless.

He does not know the word. He knows the throw of it. Upward, not down. Calling, not dismissing.

His curled hand stills.

"Marrow," I say again.

The name settles on him before either of us understands what I have done.

It is the first name I have ever given.

Marrow carries it like stolen fire.

For three days he makes me say it whenever he can catch my eye. He points to himself while eating. While climbing. While hanging upside down from Kala's arm. Marrow. Marrow. Marrow. Each time I give it, he straightens as if the sound has put bone under his skin.

The old downward tribe-noise does not vanish. Others still use it when they are careless or cruel. Voss-Marr uses it once as he passes the fire, low enough that most do not hear.

Marrow hears.

So do I.

The little shape beside me goes still. Not hurt in a simple way. Measuring. Learning where the ground has broken under him.

Voss-Marr does not look back. He does not need to. A creature like him has been obeyed since before he knew the shape of wanting. He speaks downward because the world has always been arranged beneath his feet.

I say, "Marrow."

Loud enough for the nearest heads to turn.

The crooked hand opens against the dirt.

Voss-Marr pauses at the edge of the firelight. Only a moment.

Then he walks on.

Afterward Marrow pretends not to care.

He pretends with his whole crooked self. He climbs too high. He steals a strip of liver from a sleeping male's hand and nearly loses his tail-bone for it. He hangs upside down above me and makes the downward sound in a foolish deep voice until I bare my teeth and say his chosen name for him again.

Not true the way mine is true.

True because I chose it.

That is the first time I understand that a name can be a kindness and an insult in the same breath. It can lift one creature and strike another. It can be a small fire set on a line of dry leaves.

The next day, Marrow uses the fire.

Not with flame. With sound.

He gathers Nomi, Brak, Luta, and two others near the root where we fell. He points to himself and says the downward tribe-noise in a voice so deep and foolish it belongs to a sleeping old male. The children laugh. Then he points to himself and waits.

They say it again.

He drops to the ground as if slain.

More laughter.

Then he sits up, points to me, and demands the chosen name.

"Marrow," I say.

He makes them try it.

Their mouths shape it badly. Marru. Mah-wo. One makes a sound like a frog choking. Marrow accepts none of them. He grips each small face in both hands, one crooked, one clean, and turns their mouths with great seriousness until the sound comes closer.

By dusk, five children can say it.

By the next morning, three adults have heard it.

By the next rain, one of the mothers uses it without noticing when she calls him away from the fire.

Marrow goes so still that I fear he has been struck.

Then he runs straight into the green, climbs the nearest tree, and hangs there upside down until the whole tribe must look at him.

"Marrow," he says to himself, using my human sound in a Vorth mouth.

The word is ugly there.

It is also alive.

Voss-Marr hears the children say it.

His face gives away nothing. His eyes give away enough. A name that spreads without the heir's leave is not a child's game. It is a small law growing roots.

I wanted to lift Marrow.

I did not understand I was also teaching the tribe that names can come from me.

# Chapter 11: The Weaker, the Smarter

Among the Vorth, I am weak.

This is not shame. It is weather.

A half-grown female moves me from a warm stone one morning because she wants it. She takes my wrist and lifts me aside with no anger, no effort, no thought that I might resist. By the time my feet find the ground again, she has already forgotten me.

On the shore, I was the longest tooth.

On the high stones, I am something that can be moved.

Good.

A hard truth can be used.

The Vorth hunt with weight. They pour down both sides of a channel, drive swine and deer toward the arms waiting below, and close around the prey like a fist. Their strength is terrible. A blow from one adult can break a neck clean. Their hands can tear roots from mud that would hold me all day.

But strength has edges.

The Vorth do not like deep rot. Their feet sink. Their weight is too much for the skin of moss and leaf that floats over black water. They do not climb the thin high branches where small meat nests. They cannot catch the burrow-runners on the channel flats, quick gray animals that live between hole and hole and vanish before a heavy hand can fall.

I can go where weight fails.

So can Marrow.

I take him down at low tide before the others wake. He follows close, rocking and quick, his curled hand tucked against his chest. At the channel flat I show him the pale line in the mud where a burrow-runner travels. I lay a loop of plaited fiber across it. Bend a green branch. Set the trigger light.

Marrow wants to touch everything.

He touches the knot before it is ready and snaps the branch into my cheek.

For a breath we stare at each other. My eye waters. A red line opens under the bone.

Then Marrow makes a horrified little sound, points at the branch as if it acted alone, and glares at it with such injured dignity that I cannot hold my anger. I cuff him lightly on the shoulder. He cuffs the branch. The branch does not learn.

Marrow watches once.

The second loop he sets himself.

His crooked fingers are better than mine.

We hide in the reeds. I send him wide to scratch near the burrow mouth. The runner bolts from its hole, low and fast, exactly where its own feet have worn the mud. It hits the loop. The branch snaps up. The small animal kicks twice and hangs still.

For a moment Marrow and I only stare.

Then he makes a cracked, delighted sound.

I answer with one of my own.

Laughter, I think later. The word takes time to come. At first it is only a bright break in the chest, useless and good.

We bring the runner up to the high stones.

The Vorth gather around it, puzzled. They look for the blow. The broken neck. The place where strength entered meat. There is only the loop.

Kala sees first.

She crouches and follows the fiber with one finger, from the dead animal's neck to the knot to the spring of the branch. Her eyes lift to mine, then to Marrow's curled hand.

She makes the question sound.

What is this?

I show her.

At the next low tide she comes with us, great and silent, stepping in my prints where the ground will hold her. I set the run. Marrow lays the loop. I guide Kala's huge hand to the bent branch so she can feel the stored force in it.

Then we wait.

The runner comes. The loop takes it.

Kala's laugh is a deep bark that startles birds from the trees.

After that, the tribe begins to look at me differently.

Not kindly. Not all of them. But with use.

When prey goes into soft ground, heads turn toward me. When small meat vanishes into holes, Marrow is sent for. When rain threatens the fire, I bring dry heartwood from the fallen giant, fat-soaked fiber, and the trick of feeding flame slowly from its own center.

The Skell come after the first three burrow-runners.

I smell them before I see them: sour hide, old blood, wet bone cracked for marrow and left too long in heat. They wait where the high stone spills into scree, low shapes among low rocks, ribby and gray, their eyes bright as wet seeds.

The Vorth hate them.

No. Hate is too simple.

The Vorth do not look at them until they must, and then they look as the strong look at what feeds from what they leave behind. Not enemy. Not kin. Necessary ugliness. A reminder that meat can become scrap and scrap can still move.

A half-grown Vorth throws a chewed haunch bone down the slope. The Skell scatter, then flood back over it in a low snarling knot.

Marrow watches them too closely.

I know why.

Leavings know leavings.

One Skell does not join the knot. It is smaller than the others, one hind leg twisted, hide scored by old bite marks. It waits until the fight is done, then creeps toward a splinter of bone no bigger than my finger.

A larger Skell sees and snaps.

The small one flinches but does not run. It lowers itself flat and gives up the bone before it has touched it.

Something in me goes hard and quiet.

I take a strip of runner meat from our pile and toss it past the larger Skell, not to the center of the scramble, but to the shadow where the twisted one waits.

The meat lands at its feet.

Every Skell freezes.

So do the Vorth above me.

This is not done. I know that at once. Food falls as waste, not as gift. Waste has no chooser. Gift does.

The twisted Skell looks up at me.

Its face is not Vorth. Too narrow. Too hungry. Too much tooth for the thin head. But thought lives there. Old fear too. It waits for the trick.

I make the stilling sound Kala gives me.

Be still.

The Skell does not understand the sound.

It understands my hand staying open.

It takes the meat and vanishes between stones.

The silence after is worse than snarling.

An old female Vorth makes a disgust sound low in her throat. A young male laughs. The laugh dies when Kala looks at him. Gull-Marr does not move on the high lip, but I feel his attention like sun on the back of my neck.

I have not fed a hungry mouth.

I have chosen a place in the order and moved meat across it.

Marrow makes a small pleased click in his throat.

Above us, Voss-Marr bares one tooth.

"Waste," Voss-Marr's posture says, even before I know the word.

Maybe.

Or maybe waste is only what the strong call someone before they learn its use.

The Vorth have kept fire longer than any living mouth remembers.

They have not always understood how to remake it.

That matters in the wet season.

One night their kept fire shrinks to a dull red under rain and bad wood. The adults crowd close, uneasy. A kept fire can die. If it dies in the long wet, the tribe becomes sicker, smaller, pushed closer to the rot.

I go down to my old stores and return with dry pith wrapped in hide, dead heartwood, and rendered fat. I build the flame as I built my first one: small, patient, never greedy. Fiber. Splinter. Stick. Heartwood. Fat only when the coal can bear it.

The red stands up.

Light fills the stones.

The tribe goes quiet.

Even Gull-Marr looks down from his bare lip.

Voss-Marr comes close enough to see the work of it. Rain beads on his shoulders. His face is hard, but his eyes follow the order: dry heart, patient fiber, fat only after coal. He understands quickly. Too quickly for comfort.

He reaches toward the pile.

Marrow makes a warning click, small but sharp.

Voss-Marr looks at him. Looks at me. Then he takes one wet stick from the edge and sets it where the heat will dry it without choking the flame.

Correctly.

I hate that.

I am still weak. A young Vorth could lift me aside tomorrow. But now they know weakness is not emptiness. It has its own roads, and some of those roads lead to meat, fire, and ground the strong cannot cross.

The seer comes to me after the fire is high.

She touches the eye-and-thorn at my chest, then lays one pale finger against the side of my head. Thinking. That is what she means. Or maybe old blood. Or maybe a story I cannot read.

The mark.

The mind.

The blood that does not die of the green.

She sets them near each other with her touch and bows.

I hate how much I want her meaning.

I have sounds now, more every day, but not enough for the questions that matter. Why does the island spare me? Why does this mark matter? Why does the oldest Vorth look at me as if I am less a boy than a door left open?

Marrow sleeps beside me that night with his curled hand open in the firelight.

I watch the flame I saved burn clean through the wet.

The strong take what they can reach.

The weak learn how to make the world reach farther.

Near dawn, when the fire has settled into red eyes under ash, something clicks once from the scree.

I open my eyes.

The twisted Skell crouches at the edge of the light. It has dragged something with it: a pale shell from the warm shallows, spiral-curled and polished by tide. Not food. Too clean for that. It sets the shell on the stone, backs away, and lowers its narrow head.

Marrow wakes and sees.

For once, he does not turn himself into a joke.

I take the shell after the Skell is gone. It fits in my palm like a small listening ear.

The Vorth give meat for strength, meat for place, meat for hunger.

The Skell has given me a gift no hunger can explain.

By morning there are seven Skell below the stones.

Not close. They know better than close. They crouch among the scree and pretend to be shadows with teeth. The twisted one is there too, smaller than the rest, keeping to the left where a split boulder gives it room to flee. It watches me. The others watch the meat piles.

The Vorth wake into tension.

No one throws a bone.

That is how I know I have made a problem.

A child runs too near the lower stones and two mothers snatch it back at once. The missing-thumb male takes his place by the crack earlier than usual, shoulders high. Torn-Ear comes down with a haunch in his hand, sees the waiting Skell, and lets the haunch hang at his side until blood drips on his knuckles.

Voss-Marr sees me seeing it.

He comes to the edge of the fire shelf and points down. His words are still mostly closed to me, but enough has opened.

Waste brings waste.

He points to the children. Then to the meat. Then to the Skell.

His meaning is clean enough to cut.

You fed one. Now all come. When all come, children cannot play. Meat cannot dry. The dead cannot rest without watchers. Your soft mercy has made work for everyone.

The old heat rises in me because I want him wrong.

The accusation has teeth.

That is unfair of him.

The twisted Skell did not ask the others to come. Hunger called them. My hand opened the road. But the road is real now, and every road leads both ways.

Marrow comes to my side. He has the pale shell cupped in his crooked hand. He offers it to me as if it can answer.

It cannot.

A gift does not erase a danger.

I go down the first slope with no meat in my hands.

Kala makes a warning sound. Voss-Marr makes no sound at all. That silence follows me harder than a snarl.

The Skell shift as I come. Their bodies are low, their mouths wet. One darts forward and back, testing whether my softness has meat hidden inside it. I bare my teeth and strike the stone with the butt of my knife. Once. Twice. Three times.

The sharp sound cracks through the morning.

The Skell freeze.

I take one old haunch bone from yesterday's pile, stripped clean enough that no Vorth will miss it, and set it on a flat rock halfway between their hunger and our fire. Then I back away.

Not thrown.

Set.

The difference matters. I make it matter with every motion. This stone. This bone. This distance. No closer.

The twisted one understands first.

It creeps forward with its belly low and noses the bone. A larger Skell lunges. I slap the rock with the knife again and snarl hard enough that my throat burns. Marrow echoes me from above, a ridiculous fierce noise that turns heads even in danger.

The larger Skell stops.

The twisted one takes the bone and retreats.

Only then do I point to the old waste hollow below the scree, where the Vorth throw cracked shells and bitter rinds. I point to the flat rock. I point to the Skell. I strike the stone once more.

Even with no shared tongue, law can be built from hunger and fear.

Torn-Ear grunts.

Not approval.

Interest.

That evening, the Vorth throw the bones to the flat rock instead of down the slope. The Skell fight there, away from the fire shelf. The children creep back toward their crack. The missing-thumb male keeps watching. Voss-Marr stands above the new feeding stone for a long while, then sets two heavy rocks on either side of it, shaping a boundary I had not thought to make.

Better than mine.

I hate that too.

The twisted Skell comes after dark.

It does not bring another shell. It brings a warning.

I wake to Marrow's hand patting my face. Not his usual rude slap. Quick. Careful. His eyes shine in the firelight. He points toward the lower water.

A clicking sound comes from the dark.

The twisted Skell waits near the root path, limping in place. When I rise, it turns and moves downhill. Stops. Looks back.

I follow with Marrow behind me, because trying to leave him would only make more noise.

At the channel, one of my snares is gone.

Not sprung. Gone.

The branch has been cut or chewed through. The loop taken. Mud holds the track of webbed fingers and a dragging tail.

Mure.

The word is not mine yet, but the shape from the ruin returns to my blood. Smooth pale head. Silent mouth. Water that does not ripple.

Marrow hisses.

From the black channel comes a small splash, then nothing.

The Skell crouches beside the ruined snare and makes three clicks. It taps the cut branch with one claw, taps the water, then bares its teeth toward the high stones.

Not gift.

Warning.

The Mure have noticed our traps.

This changes more than meat. If something in the water learns fiber and loop, then low tide is no longer only ours. If the Skell can warn us, then waste may guard what strength cannot see.

When I bring the cut branch back at dawn, Voss-Marr is the first to understand.

He takes it from me. Smells the wet end. Looks toward the channel.

For a moment, we stand on the same side of a fear.

Then he hands it back and makes the useful sound again.

This time it is not only for me.

His eyes flick to the scree where the Skell hide from morning.

Useful has become wider.

That is how trouble grows. Not from one mercy. From mercy proving practical.

# Chapter 12: Marks and Meaning

I unroll the dead men's skins by firelight.

For weeks I have carried them wrapped in fat-soaked hide, safe from rain, safe from rot, safe from my own fear of what they might not tell me. Now the Vorth sleep around the stones and the fire burns steady. Marrow is curled against Kala's side. The seer watches from beyond the light, as if she knew I would come to this before I did.

The skins crackle under my fingers.

Marks cover them in ordered rows.

I know they mean.

I cannot make them speak.

That loss is different from hunger. Hunger claws and demands. This sits behind my eyes and aches. There was a time when marks might have opened for me. I feel the shape of that skill the way the tongue feels the space of a missing tooth.

Once, maybe, I could read.

Now I bend over a dead man's message like an animal over bones.

But all marks are not the same.

Some are little and crowded, too many to hold. Others stand apart: lines of coast, bends of river, a black bar where the wreck lies. I know those because my feet know them. The dead man drew the island small. The channels I have crossed. The mud reach where his ship broke. The high stones where the Vorth keep fire.

Then I find the eye.

Ringed. Open. Pierced by the thorn.

The same mark hangs cold against my chest.

I set the disc beside the drawn mark. Metal and ink. Same shape. Same wound through the looking. The sight of them together makes memory stir, but not wake.

I press my thumb to the drawn eye.

Nothing comes.

Only certainty without language.

Mine.

The map runs inland beyond the wreck, deeper than I have gone with the Vorth. Up a black channel. Past two bends. To a shape drawn larger than the rest: stone steps, a crown of walls, and the eye marked above it again and again.

I have seen that place.

Once, before Kala found me, I followed a fat-tailed animal too far into the interior and came to a clearing the jungle had tried and failed to swallow. Stone rose there in tiers. Not natural stone. Made stone. Steps climbed into the canopy, slick with moss. Channels had been carved down them, long grooves running from the top to a basin below.

Old darkness stained the stone.

The eye-and-thorn was carved everywhere. Worn by rain. Split by roots. Still looking.

I did not climb to the top.

Fear refused before thought could dress itself as courage. Every hair rose. My teeth locked. I left that place quickly and told myself it was only another ruin.

Now I see the dead men were going there.

They had the map. They had the mark. They brought chains, iron, and hunger from across the water. They came toward the eye and died on the road to it, killed by the same green that cannot seem to kill me.

The seer moves into the firelight.

She does not touch the skins at first. She crouches over them, old head lowered, milk-pale eyes following the rows. She cannot read them. I see that. But she knows they are made to be read, and that is its own knowledge.

She brings ash.

Not by accident. She reaches into the dead edge of the fire and takes a pinch of gray between two fingers. Then she draws on the stone beside the skins.

One mark is a circle with a break at the top.

She points to the fire.

Kept.

One mark is a line crossed by three short cuts.

She points to the dead shelf.

Remembered.

One mark is a bent shape like a root diving under stone.

She points down toward the rot country.

Hidden.

Marrow has woken by now. He crawls closer, eyes wide, and immediately tries to draw his own mark with a whole fist of ash. It becomes a smear. He looks offended by ash as a substance and by his own hand as its servant.

The seer flicks his ear.

He flicks ash at her foot.

The tribe would be horrified if they saw. Maybe. Or maybe old seers are allowed one foolish child the way kings are allowed scars. Her mouth folds inward as if she is hiding laughter where age cannot steal it.

She takes Marrow's curled hand in hers and guides one finger through the ash.

This time the mark holds.

A small crooked line. A short hook. A dot beside it.

Marrow stills.

The seer points to him.

Not leavings.

Not my human sound.

Some Vorth mark for him, old enough that no mouth has used it aloud where I can hear. Marrow stares at the ash as if he has found a pool showing a face he had never been allowed to own.

Then the seer wipes the mark away.

Marrow makes a betrayed sound.

She presses his hand flat to his chest and gives the meaning-sound.

Kept in here.

Maybe.

Or maybe I only want that meaning because I need it.

Marks are dangerous that way. They let longing pretend to understand.

Then she touches the disc.

The long meaning-sound rises from her chest, the one the Vorth use for the dead, for old gifts, for anything carried forward from before.

Kept.

That is the closest word I have.

She touches the drawn eye. Then the metal one. Then my throat, where the pulse beats.

I go rigid.

The throat is a dangerous place. A hand there means ownership, silence, death.

Her finger is light.

She listens to my blood.

When she feels it, her face changes.

The old eyes sharpen. She looks from my pulse to the mark, from the mark to the map, from the map back to me. She sees a pattern I cannot reach: the green that kills men and leaves me standing, the eye carved in old stone, the disc that stirs my lost memory, the dead expedition, the blood under her finger.

I want to seize her and shake meaning from her mouth.

Instead I hold still.

She bows over my blood.

That is worse than an answer.

An answer can be refused. Reverence gets under the skin.

I am not holy. I am not chosen. I am a boy with scars, a knife from a dead ship, and a name hidden behind broken teeth. Yet the oldest Vorth bows to the mark on my chest and the blood in my throat as if both belong to a story older than her bones.

When she leaves, I sit awake long after the fire lowers.

The map lies open in front of me. The ruined steps wait in ink and memory. The eye looks from skin and metal both.

A made place in the jungle.

Dead men walking toward it.

Vorth reverence.

My blood.

No piece explains the whole, but they are pieces of the same whole. I feel that much.

I roll the skins carefully and bind them again.

A mark made to mean does not stop meaning because the reader is broken.

One day I will read it.

Or I will find someone who can.

The seer takes me to the ruin three mornings later.

Kala tries to stop us.

She plants herself across the path down from the high stones, one arm heavy before my chest. Her keeping sound is not soft now. It is command wrapped in grief. Stay. The old fear in her knows what old places take. The dead shelf behind her is proof of every life she has failed to keep.

I touch her wrist.

Not to move it. I could not.

To tell her I know the cost of going.

She huffs hot air over my hair and bares her teeth at the seer. The old one does not answer with anger. She only lifts the eye-and-thorn disc in her two long fingers and gives the meaning-sound that belongs to dead stories.

Kala looks at the mark.

Then at my face.

For a moment I think she will pull me back into her arms and hold me there until the world ends.

Instead she comes with us.

So does Marrow, because Marrow comes where I go unless something larger than weather ties him to stone. The twisted Skell follows from the scree at a distance, limping between roots. I pretend not to see it. Marrow pretends badly.

He keeps glancing back with his whole face, then snapping forward as if innocence can be performed by walking too stiffly. Once he trips over a root because of it. The Skell freezes. Marrow freezes. I look away before either of them can decide I have seen too much.

We go by a path the Vorth do not use.

The high stones fall behind. The green rises over us, wet and layered and full of small hungers. The seer moves slowly, but the jungle seems reluctant to touch her. Kala breaks branches when they lean too close. Marrow ranges ahead and returns with signs in his hands: old stone, dead water, a smell like iron left in rain.

By midday we reach the made place.

The ruin stands in a bowl of black trees.

It is larger than memory. Memory had made it a broken stair and a bad feeling. The truth is worse. Stone rises in five tiers, each one carved with channels that run from a flat top down into a basin at the foot. Roots split the steps. Vines hang from the upper walls like old hair. The jungle has covered the place, but not eaten it. That is the first wrongness.

Everything else on Saqarro rots.

This waits.

The eye-and-thorn is cut into every level. Some are small as fingerprints. Some are wider than my spread arms. All of them look outward, pierced through the center, the thorn entering from above or below or the side, never the same way twice.

Marrow will not step onto the first stair.

The twisted Skell makes a thin sound and backs under a root.

Kala's hand closes around my shoulder.

The seer climbs.

I follow.

At the top lies a stone basin shaped almost like a mouth and almost like a hand. The channels run from it down the face of the ruin. Old darkness stains them. Rain has washed them for years beyond counting and still the color remains under the moss.

Blood.

The word comes from some deep place in me.

The seer hears the shape of my breath. She gives three sounds, one after another.

Mouth.

Rot.

Taking.

The words are not exact in me. I know enough to feel the disagreement inside them. She is not explaining. She is naming three doors and refusing to open any one.

The meanings come to me as broken images. A mouth in the stone. A family mark turning sick in the blood. A hand taking red life from something too large to die quickly.

The seer lays her palm flat over the carved eye.

Then she lays the same palm over my heart.

The warmth behind my breastbone answers.

Not heat. Not the red hunger from the kill.

Only a pulse, low and unwelcome, as if something in me had heard its name spoken through stone.

Kala feels me tense. Her arm comes around me at once.

The warmth goes quiet.

Below, in the trees, something splashes.

We all turn.

At the edge of the black water below the ruin, a pale head rises from the brown channel. Smooth skin. Wide eyes. Fingers webbed around a root. It watches us without blinking.

Mure.

I know the name only later. In that moment I know only that the sight of it makes my blood step backward. It is not Vorth. Not Skell. Not beast. Too human in the wrong places. Too drowned in the rest.

The Mure opens its mouth.

No sound comes.

Then it slips under the water without a ripple.

The seer makes the meaning-sound over the empty channel.

Not grief.

Warning.

We do not leave fast enough.

The channel below the ruin begins to move in places where water should be still. A pale hand appears around one root, then another farther along the bank. Not one Mure. Three. Maybe four. They do not rise all the way. Only eyes. Fingers. A slick curve of shoulder. Patient as stones under water.

Marrow backs into my leg.

Kala lifts him with one hand and puts him behind her without looking. Her lips peel away from her teeth. The sound in her chest is not fear. It is hatred kept old and fed carefully.

The seer taps her staff against the top stone.

Once.

The water stills.

Twice.

The pale eyes blink.

Three times.

One Mure opens its mouth wide. Too wide. The inside is dark blue, lined with small teeth angled inward like fish bones. Still no sound comes. That silence is worse than a scream. It feels aimed at the blood behind my ears.

The warmth under my breastbone answers again.

This time the answer is sharper.

For one breath I see the ruin clean of moss. Fire on the top tier. Lines of red running down channels. Pale heads in the basin below, mouths open, drinking what they are given or what they have stolen. Vorth shapes on the lower stones, not worshiping. Bound.

Then Kala's hand clamps around the back of my neck and the vision breaks.

I am on my knees.

The seer is staring at me, not at the water.

Marrow makes my name-sound for him. Not my hidden name. His need for me. Rasped. Frightened.

I breathe.

The Mure vanish beneath the black.

On the stone where my hand has fallen, blood beads from my palm. I have cut myself on a chip of carved eye. The blood does not run down the channel. It gathers, round and bright, then creeps upward along the shallow groove toward the top basin.

Only a finger's width.

Enough.

Kala snarls and smears it away with her own hand. The seer makes a sharp sound I have never heard from her before.

Fear.

Not of the Mure.

Of me touching the stone with open blood.

We leave before the rain comes.

On the path back, Marrow walks close enough that his shoulder bumps my leg every few breaths. Kala does not release me until we can see the high stones. The twisted Skell follows with its belly low and its eyes too bright.

When the high stones come into view, the Skell stops.

I make no call. I have no sound for it. I only take the pale shell it gave me from the pouch at my waist and hold it up where the light can catch.

The Skell lowers its head.

Marrow makes a soft sound beside me, not mockery this time. Recognition.

Then the Skell slips back into the green.

That night I dream of the ruin.

In the dream the channels run red.

Not down from the basin.

Up toward it.

# Chapter 13: Belonging

The rains break in the night.

I wake because the world has gone quiet.

For a long moment I lie against Kala's side and listen to the absence. No hammering leaves. No water pouring down stone. No endless hiss through the green. Only drips, breath, the low fire, and the sleeping noises of the tribe.

Above the broken canopy, stars show.

I used to count lights when I was alone. One light for each night survived. One light for each distance between me and whatever I wanted but could not reach.

This night, I leave them uncounted.

There is no between.

That frightens me when I notice it.

When I was alone, distance kept me alive. Every want had space around it. Every sound could be tested before it reached me. Here, Kala's breath warms the back of my neck, Marrow's toes are wedged under my ribs, and some sleeping child has one hand tangled in my hair. I could not run without waking half the tribe.

For a moment the old part of me counts exits.

Then Marrow kicks in his sleep and mutters my chosen name for him, badly shaped but clear enough.

I stop counting.

At dawn the Vorth pour down from the high stones to take what the lifted rain has left. Fruit knocked loose. Shells in new pools. Fat animals driven from flooded dens. The whole tribe smells change and moves with it.

I go with them.

Not behind them.

That is new.

An old male with a torn ear waits at the head of a slope where rot ground begins. He is massive, scarred, strong enough to break a swine's spine with both hands. But the rot will not hold him. He looks down at the wet thicket, then back to me, and makes the asking sound.

Where?

I step past him onto the green skin of the ground.

He follows in my footprints.

I read the safe line by pressure, color, smell, the trembling of roots under the mud. The old male sets his huge feet exactly where mine were. I become the eye. He becomes the hand. Together we bring down the wallowing beast he could smell but not reach.

When we return, he gives me part of the liver before the others.

Not charity.

Share.

Marrow sees it and struts for both of us.

He is mine now in the way any living creature can belong without being owned. The tribe knows it. When larger young ones shove him too hard, he comes to me. When he finds a new burrow-run, he pulls me by the wrist to show it. At night he sleeps wherever my warmth is, unless Kala steals him into the curve of her arm.

This causes trouble.

Two older young ones take his food one morning and toss it between them, high over his head. Not enough to maim. Enough to remind. Marrow's high shoulder drops in that quiet no of his, but his hand stays empty.

I step between them.

The fire shelf goes still in small degrees. First the little ones. Then the nearest adults. Then Voss-Marr, who is sitting above us with one knee up and both hands loose, as if he has been waiting for a lesson someone else would start.

I hold the strike back. I take one of my own strips of liver and put it in Marrow's curled hand. Then I look at the two older ones and eat the stolen piece they meant to throw next.

Marrow's mouth falls open.

The small ones make delighted scandal sounds.

The older young ones stare at me, insulted and uncertain. They expected anger. They do not know what to do with manners used as a knife.

Kala makes no sound at all, which means she approves too much to show it.

One evening he brings me a gift.

A frog.

It is fat, green, alive, and offended.

Marrow lays it at my feet with solemn pride. I look at the frog. The frog looks back. Its throat pulses. Then it launches itself between us and vanishes into the dark.

A sound bursts out of me before I can stop it.

Laughter again.

Marrow startles, then copies it badly, which makes the sound come harder from me. The tribe turns to stare. Marrow laughs louder because they are staring. For a few breaths we are both useless with it, bent over empty hands while the best gift he owned escapes uncooked.

Kala watches us from beside the fire.

Her eyes are sad and warm.

The next morning, she takes my hand and leads me to the shelf of the dead.

I resist at the first step. That ground is not mine. The dead lie there squared to the sun, faces lifted, gifts arranged beside them. Only Kala and the seer tend that place. But Kala does not let go.

A small one died in the night, the wet still in its chest.

Kala carries it up wrapped in leaves. She kneels on the clean stone and sets my hands beneath hers. Together we straighten the little limbs. Turn the face toward morning. Lay a shell beside the hand. Smooth the fur with wet fingers.

My hands shake.

I have left the dead to rot. I have robbed them. I have slept near them. I have never helped make one kept.

When the small one is arranged, Kala guides my hand to an empty place beside the row.

She presses my palm flat to the stone.

Then hers beside it.

She makes the meaning-sound.

Kept.

It takes me too long to understand.

She is showing me where I will go when I die.

Not into the channel. Not into a pit. Not over the side of a ship when my flesh stops being useful. Here. On clean stone. Faced to the sun. Among the Vorth dead, with hands that remember the shape of me.

There are gifts that fill hunger.

There are gifts that make hunger irrelevant.

I kneel there with Kala's hand over mine and cannot breathe.

It is too much.
