The hand in the hold promised I would be used until I was empty. Kala's hand promises I will be remembered when I am empty of everything. I have no practice standing under that kind of mercy. It is heavier than fear.

Gull-Marr comes up onto the death shelf while my hand is still on the stone.

Every Vorth goes still.

Kala does not remove her hand.

The king stands above us, immense and old, the gray in his hide bright under the new sun. This is his tribe. His stone. His dead. I am a soft-skinned stranger with a mark no one will explain, kneeling in a place that says I belong past death.

His eyes move from me to Kala, from Kala to the empty place, from the empty place to the eye-and-thorn on my chest.

The seer has come behind him. The old one makes the meaning-sound once, low.

Gull-Marr looks at me a long while.

Then he gives a short grunt.

Allowed.

No warmth. No welcome. But no refusal.

Voss-Marr is there when it happens.

He stands below the shelf with the young strong ones behind him, and I understand by the stillness in their shoulders that he has brought them to see me refused. Or to see Kala corrected. Or to see the king put the soft-skin back where a soft-skin belongs.

Instead Gull-Marr leaves my hand on the death stone.

Voss-Marr's face does not change.

That is how I know the change is deep.

He turns and goes down.

The tribe breathes again.

Kala's hand remains over mine until he is gone.

That night, under the returned stars, Marrow sleeps against my chest and Kala's heartbeat moves slow against my back. I touch the hidden name behind my teeth.

My name.

Still mine.

But now there is a place where that name could be laid down, if the day ever comes when I choose to give it.

That is belonging.

Not being taken.

Having somewhere to be placed when all taking is done.

It is also debt.

I understand that before sleep. If I belong here, then this place can be hurt through me. If I am placed among their dead, then I can shame their living. Home is not a shelter only. It is a skin you can be cut through.

The next day the tribe eats too much fruit and becomes foolish.

The rains have shaken loose the yellow clusters that hang high over the east slope. They split when they strike stone, spilling sweet pulp that makes the small ones shriek and the adults pretend not to hurry. Even Gull-Marr takes three and sits with juice in his gray muzzle like a king too dignified to notice he is sticky.

Marrow steals one from under Torn-Ear's hand.

This is a mistake.

Torn-Ear roars and gives chase, slow enough to be insult and fast enough to be danger. Marrow bolts across the fire shelf, leaps over a sleeping child, ducks under Kala's elbow, and throws the fruit to me.

I catch it because my hands are foolish too.

Now Torn-Ear chases me.

The tribe loves this.

I run because there is no way not to run when an old scarred Vorth comes after you with juice on his chin and murder in his shoulders. I cut across the lower stones, over the root where I fell in the game, around the kept fire, up the slope toward the dead shelf and stop just before the forbidden line.

Torn-Ear cannot stop as cleanly.

He skids, arms windmilling, and sits down hard enough to shake dust from the roots.

For one breath I think I have insulted him beyond repair.

Then the old male laughs.

The sound rolls over the stones and breaks the tribe open.

Kala covers her face with one huge hand. The small ones throw peelings. Marrow falls over from delight. Even the seer makes a dry clicking sound that might be laughter if old bones remember how.

I stand holding the stolen fruit, breathless, alive, and not hunted.

For once, running has not ended in hiding.

Torn-Ear reaches up from the dust.

I give the fruit back.

He splits it with one thumbnail and gives me half.

Share.

It is a small gift.

It is not small to me.

Later, when the sun lowers and the fruit-sweet air turns sour with heat, Voss-Marr finds me beside the water jars.

He takes my half-cleaned fiber rope and studies the knots. His hands are strong and clever enough. That surprises me. I expected contempt to make him clumsy. It does not. He understands the snare after one turn, sees the branch-law under it, and tightens the knot better than I did.

Then he tosses it back.

His sound is short.

Useful.

Not praise.

Not insult.

Something worse. A truth neither of us can own.

For a moment I see the shape he might have had if I had not come from the green with a mark on my chest and a seer bowing to my blood. He would have been hard. Proud. Maybe cruel. But also capable. The tribe's future, trained into his bones from birth.

He has learned the knots because he should know all tools that might matter to his rule. He has watched me because a ruler watches anything that changes weight in the hand. There is no stupidity in him to comfort me.

I did not only find a home here.

I stepped into another son's promised place.

The thought sits badly in me.

That night, while Kala sleeps, I take the shell the Skell gave me and set it near the small dead one on the shelf, not among the gifts exactly, but close enough that morning light will touch it.

The shell is from the shallows. The small one never saw the shallows. Now it has something from there.

Maybe that is not Vorth law.

I know Kala sees it at dawn.

She says nothing. She only presses her forehead to mine, carefully, where no one else can make a lesson of it.

The lesson comes anyway.

At first light, the one-clouded female finds the shell.

Her grief-sound is small and sharp. Not for the dead child. For the wrongness beside it. She lifts the shell between two fingers as if it has slime on it and turns toward the fire shelf. Heads rise. Sleep falls away. Kala stands slowly.

The old female holds the shell where everyone can see.

Her words come fast, but enough of their shape is mine now.

Skell gift.

Dead child.

Wrong shelf.

The twisted Skell is nowhere to be seen, but its thin gift has crossed into Vorth morning and become argument.

I step forward.

Kala's hand catches my shoulder. Warning. Not command. She has learned that commands wake old wounds in me, and I have learned that warnings can still be love.

The one-clouded female points the shell at me. Her sound is not cruel. That makes it harder. She is not Voss-Marr, hungry for my fall. She is a keeper of rules because rules have kept something alive longer than any one grief. The dead shelf is not a place for every found token and soft wish. If I can lay Skell gifts beside Vorth dead, what else can cross? Skell bones? Mure signs? Human iron?

The tribe listens because she is right to ask.

Voss-Marr steps from the shadow beneath Gull-Marr's stone.

He says very little.

He does not need much.

His hand points to the high shelf, then down to the scree, then out toward the black channels. One line. Above. Below. Away. His meaning is clear even to the children.

If every border is love, no border remains.

I hate him for the thought because it reaches me.

I have spent my life being kept out. Now that a door has opened, I want all locked places proved cruel. But the Vorth are not wrong to have edges. The dead shelf is clean because hands guard it. The children sleep because someone decides who may come close. A home without borders is only meat waiting for teeth.

The shell feels suddenly heavy in the old female's hand.

Marrow slips beside me. He looks at the shell. Looks at me. Then, before anyone can stop him, he goes down on his knuckles and climbs the first step toward the dead shelf.

The tribe inhales.

Marrow stops before the forbidden line.

Not over it.

Before it.

He takes a fruit seed from his cheek, a disgusting secret he has apparently been saving for no good reason, and sets it on the lower step. Then he points to the shell, points to the same step, and gives a little click.

Not dead shelf.

Memory step.

The audacity of it nearly breaks my face open.

The one-clouded female stares.

Marrow waits with the solemn patience of a creature who has never respected solemnity in his life until this exact breath.

The old seer arrives late, leaning on her staff. She studies Marrow's seed on the lower step. Studies the shell in the old female's hand. Then she takes the shell and places it beside the seed.

Not among the dead.

Not thrown away.

Between.

Her staff taps once.

Allowed.

The word goes through the tribe in ripples. Some approve. Some do not. Voss-Marr's mouth tightens, but he cannot call the seer soft in front of the dead, not when she has kept their names longer than he has been alive.

Marrow climbs down and tries to look as if he planned every breath.

I touch his crooked hand.

He bumps my fingers away, embarrassed by gratitude.

By evening, three more small gifts rest on the memory step: a red leaf, a cracked nut, and one tiny fish bone placed there by Nomi for no reason she can explain. The one-clouded female removes none of them. She only sets them in a straighter line.

That is how a law begins here.

Not by kindness alone.

By argument surviving morning.

# Chapter 14: Standing

The rot-thicket takes Torn-Ear at midday.

He should have waited.

He knows it before the mud takes him. I see that in the quick flattening of his ears, in the little turn of his head toward me when the wallowing beast breaks from cover. Pride moves faster than wisdom. Hunger faster than both.

The old male has followed my tracks for weeks, learning which moss lies, which root mat holds, where the black water waits under green skin. But strength has its own pride. A wallowing beast breaks cover within scenting distance, fat and slow, and Torn-Ear goes after it before I can read the ground.

His first step holds.

The second sinks to the knee.

The third opens the thicket beneath him.

He drops to his chest in black soup.

The Vorth gather at the bank and stop. They know that ground. They have watched it eat. Torn-Ear thrashes, and the rot takes him deeper for every effort. Strength is making him drown.

Someone makes the gone-sound too early.

Kala snarls it quiet.

I hear the gone-sound begin in the tribe.

Too soon.

Marrow is beside me. He looks at the thicket, then at me, and his curled hand opens once.

Go, then.

So I go.

Light feet. Wide steps. Never straight toward the drowning Vorth, because he has churned that ground to nothing. I run the long firm line where old roots knot beneath the surface. The mud breathes under me. One slow step would lose me. Speed is part of the path.

Torn-Ear sees me and goes still.

That stillness nearly breaks me.

I know it. The stillness of someone who has measured the stronger force and found no space left to fight. I wore it in the hold when the hand closed on my neck.

I cannot pull him out.

If I take his arm, he will drag me under.

So I make the thicket give him back.

A vine as thick as my wrist hangs from a drowned tree. I cut it with the dead men's knife, throw one end across the soup, and slap it against his shoulder.

He stares.

"Take it," I rasp.

The words are not Vorth. Not quite human either. But want has its own tongue, and he understands that.

His hand closes on the vine.

The first pull nearly kills me.

He is too frightened, too strong, too deep in the sucking dark. The vine jerks tight and drags me one step from the root-line. Mud opens cold around my ankle. For one breath the thicket has both of us.

Marrow screams from the bank.

Not warning. Anger.

He flings himself onto the root-line behind me, light enough to cross where the others cannot. His curled hand catches the back of my hide wrap. His good hand hooks a root. He is not strong enough to pull me free.

He is strong enough to keep me from losing the next breath.

I run my end over a broken limb high on the leaning tree, then down. A turn. A lift. The same law as snare and branch, only larger. The tree becomes the arm I lack.

Then I call the strong.

The Vorth cannot cross the thicket, but they can pull from the firm line I show them. One by one they come, setting their feet where mine went. Huge hands close on the vine behind me.

Voss-Marr comes too.

Of course he does. He will not let the tribe see fear in him where a soft-skin has gone first. He takes the vine behind Torn-Ear's eldest daughter and sets his feet exactly in my prints, his face flat with concentration.

I am the eye.

They are the force.

Together we pull.

For a moment the rot holds him.

Then the soup breaks with a wet sucking roar, and Torn-Ear rises inch by inch from the black. Mud pours off his shoulders. His torn ear is flat against his skull. His eyes are wild and alive.

The Vorth drag him onto the bank.

He lies there heaving.

The tribe has gone silent.

Everyone saw.

The weak one crossed where the strong could not. The weak one made a path for their strength. The weak one brought back someone already half given to the dead shelf.

Torn-Ear gets to his knees.

He comes to me dripping black water and smelling of the thicket's belly. His great hand closes around my wrist.

Not to move me.

To lift me.

He raises my arm before the tribe and makes a sound deep enough to stir the mud.

This one.

The meaning hits harder than any blow.

Their full language is still beyond me, but I have this. He is naming me before them. Not as Kala's kept stray. Not as omen. Not as the marked soft-skin the seer bows toward.

As the one who did the work.

Marrow presses against my side, trembling with pride he is trying not to show.

Then the air changes.

Voss-Marr comes down from the stones.

Gull-Marr's son. Prime blood. The heir in everything but death. He is young, huge, clean of scars because the world has not yet found courage to mark him. I have watched him from the corner of my eye for weeks. He is strength without apology, and the tribe has been shaped to make room for his future.

Now he looks at my lifted wrist.

At Torn-Ear bowing his head.

At the others looking toward me.

No anger shows. That is worse. Anger spends itself. Calculation saves its teeth.

Voss-Marr speaks to Torn-Ear, not to me.

One short command.

Torn-Ear's ears flatten. My wrist lowers. The pride in his shoulders folds away.

Just like that, Voss-Marr reminds the bank whose stone this is, and whose it will be.

Only then does he look at me.

I have been looked at by predators. By slavers. By men deciding price. His gaze is none of those. He looks as one rival looks at another before either has earned the word.

He sees what happened here.

A soft-skinned weakling aimed Vorth strength and made the tribe turn. A boy who can do that once might do it again. A mind that can make the strong follow a path not chosen by the heir has become more than useful.

It has become possible.

Possible is dangerous.

Voss-Marr turns away without touching me.

That is its own mark.

High above, Gull-Marr has watched everything and moved not at all.

The tribe does not scatter at once.

That is how I know the rescue has become larger than one life.

Torn-Ear's daughter, a thick-armed hunter with a split lower lip, kneels beside her father and scrapes rot from his chest with both hands. She keeps glancing at me as if thanks has lodged in her throat and will choke her if she lets it out. The missing-thumb male checks the root-line I crossed, pressing each place with his foot, learning the path after danger has passed. Nomi and Luta are being held back by three adults because children think survival is a game until it swallows someone they know.

Marrow, of course, is telling the story already.

He throws himself flat in the mud, kicks, makes Torn-Ear's drowning sound with such disrespect that Torn-Ear's daughter bares her teeth at him. Then he leaps up, becomes me, runs in six directions at once, becomes himself, becomes the vine, becomes Voss-Marr pulling with great dignity, and finally becomes Torn-Ear bursting from the mud by launching his whole body backward into a puddle.

No one laughs at first.

Then Torn-Ear does.

Weakly. Mud still in his teeth. Alive enough to be foolish.

The laugh breaks the bank open, but not fully. It has fear inside it. Everyone can smell how close the dead shelf came.

Voss-Marr lets the laughter spend itself.

Then he speaks.

More words than I can hold, but not so many that the shape is lost. He points to Torn-Ear. To the rot. To me. To Marrow. To the line of footprints across the green skin. Then he slaps his own chest and points to the firm bank where the strong waited until I called them.

His meaning cuts through the wet air.

A rescue that needs a soft-skin path is still a Vorth rescue. A vine pulled by Vorth hands. A life returned to Vorth stone. Do not forget whose strength lifted the old male free.

Some of the young strong ones answer him.

Red-Back, the fruit climber with brown streaks in his hide, thumps his chest. Long-Jaw, who once took Marrow's food and learned my manners-knife, gives a low agreement. Two others follow because agreement is easier than choosing.

I feel anger rise.

Then I force myself to look at the vine still trembling over the branch.

Voss-Marr is not wrong.

My path alone would have left Torn-Ear drowning. Marrow's hand alone would have left me under the mud. Cleverness found the road. Strength dragged death back from it. A tribe that forgets strength because it loves the clever weak will bury many strong bodies.

Again, truth has found its worst speaker.

Again, worse than wrong.

Because he does not only protect the truth. He uses it to put me back below him.

Gull-Marr descends then.

No hurry. No drama. The old king comes down the slope with mud dry on his feet and silver in his muzzle, and every mouth closes. He stands over Torn-Ear first. Touches the old male's shoulder. Receives the low grateful sound from Torn-Ear's daughter. Then he turns to the vine.

He studies the knot.

My knot.

Marrow's grip mark on the hide.

The places where Vorth hands burned bark smooth by pulling.

Gull-Marr takes the vine in both hands and breaks it.

For one terrible breath I think he is rejecting the work.

Then he gives one half to Torn-Ear.

The other half to me.

A murmur moves through the bank.

I hold the wet vine and understand very little except that Voss-Marr has gone utterly still.

The king has split the rescue.

Not mine.

Not theirs.

Ours.

It is not love. Gull-Marr's eyes remain cool. But he has made a public shape that cannot be easily bent. Torn-Ear's life is tied to the hand that crossed and the hands that pulled. If anyone mocks one side of that, he mocks the king's division.

Voss-Marr lowers his head.

Only as much as he must.

Red-Back lowers his faster. Long-Jaw follows after seeing which way safety has gone.

Marrow looks from my vine to Torn-Ear's and makes a small awed click.

Then he reaches up and tries to chew the end of mine.

I knock his hand away.

He looks wounded, as if kingship has ruined me.

That is when the laugh comes from me. Short. Unwise. Torn-Ear hears it and laughs again, which makes his daughter curse him in a voice that needs no translation.

The bank breathes.

But Voss-Marr has learned too.

He has seen his father answer a new kind of usefulness in front of everyone. If he is to beat me, he cannot only bare teeth. He must make the tribe need his answer more than mine, or break the place where mine begins.

Later, when the bank clears, Kala comes to me by the thicket. She settles her vast weight beside mine and gives the stilling sound.

Be held.

Nothing comes.

I answer it because she needs me to answer.

But I am looking up at the stone where Voss-Marr stands with the green behind him and the future around him like a hide no one has yet dared cut.

Kala follows my gaze.

Her hand settles on the vine half across my knees. She turns it once, feeling the broken fibers. Her eyes are wet, though no tear falls. Vorth grief rarely spills. It stays in the chest and changes the sound of breathing.

She makes two sounds.

Son.

Danger.

At first I think she means me.

Then she touches her own chest. Points up at Voss-Marr. Points to Gull-Marr's stone. Her face folds with a grief I have not understood before this moment. Not only for the child she lost. For another child she cannot hold because his place is already armored.

Voss-Marr was small once.

The thought feels foolish because he seems born from stone and pride, but of course he was small. Someone lifted him. Someone kept fever from him. Someone laughed when he fell, or failed to. He was shaped to inherit a tribe that survives by strength, and now a marked stranger teaches the tribe to look elsewhere.

Kala does not ask me to forgive him.

She asks me to see the wound before it becomes teeth.

That is harder.

At dusk, Torn-Ear's daughter comes for me.

She stands at the edge of Kala's warmth with rot still dried on her forearms. Her name is not easy in my mouth yet. It sounds like stone cracking under water. I call her Split-Lip in my head, which is not kind, but names begin where the eye can hold them.

She holds the broken vine half her father received.

Not asking for thanks. Asking for work.

She leads me to the lower stones. Voss-Marr is already there with Red-Back, Long-Jaw, and three young strong ones who pretend not to be listening too hard. Gull-Marr sits above, shadowed by the last light. The missing-thumb watcher has brought Nomi, Luta, and the other children close enough to see, far enough to live.

The thicket lies dark below us.

Torn-Ear sits wrapped in leaves, furious at being alive and unable to stand. His daughter places the vine at my feet, then points to the rot. Her meaning is plain.

Show us.

My first answer is no.

Not aloud. In the hand that tightens on my knife. In the old animal keeping its roads hidden. A path no one else knows is life. A safe root-line through death is power. If Voss-Marr wants to make me small, why should I give him the map that made me useful?

Marrow touches my wrist.

He is watching Torn-Ear's daughter, not Voss-Marr. Her face is hard, but fear leaks from her in small places. Her father nearly drowned in ground he thought he knew. Tomorrow another hunter may chase hunger into the same green skin. A secret path kept for pride can kill as surely as a bad command.

I hate how often belonging asks me to open my hand.

I step to the edge.

I show them the first sign: moss bright as new bile means water under leaf. Moss dull and torn means root close beneath. I show where bubbles rise when the mud breathes. I show the little white reed that grows only where the skin is thin. I place my foot on the first safe knot, then the next, then leap back before the ground can decide I am a meal.

The young strong ones watch with narrowed eyes.

Voss-Marr watches best.

He sees patterns quickly. Too quickly. After the third crossing mark, he points to a place I have not named, a brown patch between two black roots.

Safe?

I almost say yes because the lie would be useful.

The thought is there. Clear. Shameful. Easy.

If he steps there later, he sinks to the waist. If he lives, he learns fear. If he dies, the future changes.

The Red under my ribs likes the shape of that.

I bite the inside of my cheek until blood touches my tongue.

Then I shake my head.

I take a stick and push it onto the brown patch. The ground swallows half its length in silence.

Voss-Marr looks at the patch. Then at me.

He knows.

Maybe not the whole of it. Enough.

Split-Lip exhales through her nose. Torn-Ear makes a low sound from his leaf wrappings. The children whisper the reed-sign to each other. Nomi says it wrong. Luta corrects her with great authority and no understanding.

Voss-Marr steps forward at last.

He points to the safe root-line. Points to the strong ones. Then to the children.

His words come clear enough.

No child crosses.

No hunter crosses alone.

No rescue begins without vine.

He takes my lesson and makes a rule from it.

He is good at that.

The tribe hears. The rule settles. It will save lives, and it comes from his mouth. My knowledge. His shape around it. Our rescue already becoming useful to him.

I should be angry.

I am angry.

I am also relieved.

Power that only feeds pride is a hook. Power turned into rule can hold a child back from mud.

Voss-Marr looks at me once more before he climbs away. Not grateful. Never that. But the look has changed by a hair.

I am not only rival.

I am resource.

Among Vorth, a resource can be guarded, used, stolen, or broken so no one else can use it.

Something comes.

Not now.

Not tomorrow.

But it has seen me, and I have seen it.

I earned standing today.

The high stones answered by giving that standing an enemy.

# Chapter 15: The Apex

The jungle goes silent before the Hesh comes.

Not quiet. Silent.

The insects lift from the meat. Birds vanish into the high green. Even the Skell leave their midden piles and crawl into dark places. A hole opens in the sound of the island, and every living creature knows the shape of what belongs there.

The Vorth know before I do.

That is what frightens me.

I have seen them hungry, angry, grieving. I have seen young males cuff each other bloody beside the fire and females bare teeth over meat. I have not seen the tribe afraid.

Now fear moves through them like cold smoke.

Small ones are gathered up. Half-grown hunters leave the lower paths. Torn-Ear will not go beyond the first bank. Even Voss-Marr stands quieter, contempt pressed over something he does not want anyone to see.

The seer lifts his ash-pale head toward the rot-thickets and gives the long meaning-sound over empty air.

Grief for what has not happened.

The Hesh shows itself the next morning.

I am below the stones with Torn-Ear and Marrow, reading a rot edge where burrow-runners cut across the mud. Marrow clicks once from his root perch.

Look.

The thicket parts.

The Hesh comes through.

It is longer than two Vorth lying head to foot. Low, black, ridged, wet with swamp water. Its head is broad and flat, its eyes high, its mouth too long. When it opens that mouth, the teeth are not made for one clean bite. They are made to hold, to roll, to drown, to take a body apart while the water fills its throat.

It wears the swamp on it. Weed hangs from the ridges of its back. Tiny white crab-spiders cling in the plates behind its jaw, feeding on old blood and scraps of skin. Along one flank, scars cross over scars, pale ropes under black hide where spears, teeth, claws, maybe Vorth hands, all failed to matter. The tail moves last and moves least, a slow rudder through mud.

The smell reaches after it.

Rot. River. Old fish. A musk thick enough to taste.

Every small biting creature in the reeds lifts away from that smell.

It does not hurry.

Nothing has taught it haste.

Torn-Ear makes a sound I have never heard from him. Fear, crushed down in a strong chest. Then he runs for the bank.

The Hesh turns after movement.

I stay motionless.

The old hold-cunning saves me. The lesson beaten into small bodies by cruel hands: be nothing, make no shape, let the eye pass over. I flatten into the mud beside a root while the Hesh drags its weight past me, close enough that I smell old meat between its teeth.

It follows Torn-Ear to the edge of firmer ground and stops.

There.

That is the first useful fact.

The Hesh is king of water, mud, and soft bank. It does not climb. It will not spend its long body on high stone. Its strength has a border.

Borders matter. A leech owns a wound until salt finds it. A vine owns a tree until fire comes. The strongest jaw in the green is still only strongest where the ground agrees with it.

The Hesh tests the border anyway.

It lays its flat head on the first stone and keeps it there, watching Torn-Ear pant above it. The mouth opens a little. Water slides from the teeth in bright strings. One front foot comes forward, claws spreading on rock slick with algae.

For one breath I think the island has lied to me.

Then the foot slips.

The Hesh does not fall. It is too old a killer to spend balance foolishly. It only draws back and strikes the stone with its tail hard enough to crack a white vein through the surface. The sound pops through the bank. Torn-Ear flinches. I flinch too, deep in the mud where no one can see.

The Hesh waits after that.

Not confused. Not angry in the way Vorth anger is loud and immediate. It waits as if the stone has made an argument and the Hesh is considering how long hunger can sit beside insult. Its eyes hold on Torn-Ear. Then, slowly, they slide along the bank.

Over the roots.

Over the mud where I lie.

Past me.

My lungs burn. A mosquito feeds at the corner of my eye and I let it. Mud crawls into my ear. My fingers ache from keeping still. There is a kind of fear that makes a body flee, and another that nails every part of it to the world.

The Hesh teaches the second kind.

At last it turns back into the rot pool.

It does not leave quickly. It drags itself through the soft bank with such weight that the mud folds after it like a mouth closing. Tiny fish leap from the brown water and strand themselves rather than stay under its passing shadow. A frog, bright blue and stupid with panic, lands beside my wrist. I could eat it. I stay still.

The Hesh moves.

The frog vanishes.

Not eaten. The water takes it when the tail sweeps past. A small life caught in the labor of a larger one.

That is the Hesh too. Not only teeth. A wake of harm that keeps harming after the hunger has gone.

The second fact comes after it leaves.

The Skell come out of hiding first.

Not to feed. Not yet. They move wrong for feeding, low and spread, eyes fixed on the path the Hesh took. The twisted one I fed limps at the edge of them. It sniffs the mud where the great belly dragged, then jerks back as if the smell has bitten it.

One by one, the Skell press their narrow heads to the ground.

Not bowing.

Listening.

Marrow looks at me.

Neither answer is in me.

Then the brown channel below the bank ripples.

A Mure hand rises through the water, pale fingers spread against the mud. Another follows. A face breaks the surface, eyes black and round, hair slicked flat. The Mure does not climb out. It only watches the Hesh track, throat fluttering with silent breath.

Skell above.

Mure below.

Vorth on the stone.

All three peoples marking the same path and none willing to cross it.

The Hesh is not only a large beast, then. It is a border in the island's mind. When it passes, even the old hatreds make room.

The twisted Skell sees me watching and scratches three lines in the mud with one long claw. Then a fourth line crossing them.

I understand less than I feel.

Three peoples.

One hunger above them.

The Mure sinks before I can read more.

The Skell scatter when Torn-Ear groans.

Only the lines remain, already filling with black water.

Later, when I crawl back to the place after Torn-Ear has been taken up the bank and carried home half-staggering, I find more signs.

The Hesh's belly has crushed a nest of reed-rats without eating them. Their small bodies lie pressed flat in the mud, pink mouths open. Around them, the Skell have placed bits of shell in a half circle. Not a feast marker. Not a warning I know. A count, maybe. Or a kindness. Or only the shape grief takes when hands are made for tearing.

At the waterline, Mure fingers have drawn loops in the silt. Each loop breaks on the side facing the pool.

Do not enter.

Or, entered and not returned.

I make myself look at every mark until the fear becomes knowledge.

The Hesh has been here before. Not once. Many times. It moves through the lower green in seasons, when heat thickens and river-pig breed in the mangrove shallows. It takes Mure from channels, Skell from midden paths, Vorth from the low hunts. The island does not forget. It has built manners around the mouth: when to vanish, where to stand, which pools go silent, which branches are too low over brown water.

The Hesh is not an interruption.

It is a harvest.

That is why the old ones fear it so cleanly. Fear without surprise. Fear that has had children.

It lifts its head and bellows.

The sound moves through the mud into my bones. Up on the high stones, the tribe falls silent under it.

The Hesh is naming the lower green its own.

Torn-Ear lives because he reached the bank in time. I live because I was small enough to be missed. Neither feels like victory.

That night the tribe crowds close around the fire.

Kala holds Marrow and me under one arm. Her stilling sound starts, then fails before the last part. She cannot say nothing comes when something has come and named the edge of our home.

Gull-Marr stands on his lip and looks outward, not at us.

The king has no answer.

Strength cannot answer a mouth that turns strength into meat.

He tries to make one anyway.

At moonrise he sends three hunters down the old stone throat toward the first bank. Voss-Marr goes with them. Of course he does. His ribs are not yet opened by any boar. His pride is whole enough to be loud. They carry heavy spears with fire-hardened points and slabs of river-pig fat wrapped in leaves. Bait and courage. The tribe watches them go as if watching men walk into weather.

Kala's arm tightens around me.

I slip free before she can stop me.

Marrow slips after me because he is a fool and my shadow, and because there is no rope strong enough to tie him to wisdom when danger has my shape.

We follow high along the root shelf, above the hunters and downwind. I tell myself I only need to see. That is nearly true. I also need to know whether strength can do what I cannot yet imagine.

The hunters set the fat at the edge of the lower mud and retreat to stone. Voss-Marr stands too close. The others know it. He knows they know. That keeps him there.

For a long while nothing comes.

Then the mud breathes.

The Hesh rises where no beast so large should have been hidden. Brown water sheets from its back. It does not go for the fat. It goes for the far hunter, the one whose spear tip dips one finger lower than the rest.

The strike is too fast for its size.

Stone saves him. Not skill. Not courage. Stone. The Hesh's teeth close on air and splinter the spear instead of the arm behind it. The hunter falls backward, screaming. Voss-Marr roars and drives his own spear down into the flat skull.

The point bites.

It breaks.

The Hesh turns its head with the broken shaft standing in the hide above one eye. It looks at Voss-Marr as if he has touched it with a reed.

Then it takes the river-pig fat and sinks.

The whole attack has meant less to it than the bait.

Voss-Marr stands shaking on the bank, empty spear in hand. The other hunters pull the fallen one back. Blood runs from his palm where splinters have opened him. No one is dead.

That is the victory Gull-Marr can claim.

No one is dead.

The tribe receives the hunters with hard relief and no song. Voss-Marr throws the broken shaft into the fire. It burns badly, wet wood hissing. His eyes find me in the crowd. I have mud on my knees from where I crouched on the shelf. He knows I followed. He knows I saw.

His shame wants somewhere to go.

It chooses me.

I lie awake against Kala's side while the tribe sleeps badly. The Hesh owns the lower paths now. It will take from the edges. A careless hunter. A child too near water. Torn-Ear, if pride leads him down again.

Gull-Marr will hold the high stone and call the losses necessary.

That is what kings do when they meet the edge of their power.

I am not king.

I am weak. Light. Hard to see when I choose stillness. I read mud better than any Vorth alive.

The Hesh is too strong to fight.

It is not too strong to read.

Before dawn the seer comes to me. He touches the eye-and-thorn on my chest, then points into the dark where the Hesh vanished. The meaning-sound rises from him, first over the mark, then over the dark, joining them in some old grammar I cannot read.

This one.

That one.

One telling.

The seer thinks I am an answer.

Whether that makes him wise or mad is beyond me.

But by morning I have decided to hunt the Hesh.

Marrow decides it too, though no one asks him.

He appears at my side before dawn with two coils of fiber over his good shoulder, a stone knife in his teeth, and the solemn face of someone doing a very bad job of seeming older than he is.

I point back to Kala.

He points at the Hesh track.

I bare my teeth.

He bares his wider.

For a moment, despite the fear waiting in the green, I almost laugh.

The island is full of old gods, drowned warnings, hungry mouths, and stone marks no living tongue can explain.

It is also full of Marrow, who has decided the largest killer in the low country is best answered with rope and attitude.

That almost makes the fear bright enough to bear.

I put my forehead against his for one breath, the way Kala does when she wants him still. He goes solemn at once, pleased with himself and trying not to show it.

Then he trips on one of his own rope coils and nearly falls down the path.

I catch him by the scruff. He pretends he meant to do it.

For the first time since the Hesh came through the thicket, the green lets me laugh.

# Chapter 16: The Long Hunt

No one hunts the Hesh with courage.

Courage is meat that has not yet understood the mouth.

I hunt with patience.

For three days I sharpen nothing, throw nothing, strike nothing. I watch. I lie in warm mud downwind while the Hesh sleeps with only its eyes above the rot pool. I learn where it basks, where it turns, where it drags its belly between water and bank. It is strongest in the soft edge and almost useless on stone.

Watching makes a person of a monster.

Not human. Never that.

But pattern is a kind of self.

The Hesh hates white birds. It snaps at their reflections even when no meat waits beneath them. It will not cross fallen ironwood unless rain has darkened the bark. It sleeps with its head toward the channel and its tail in the reeds, always leaving one path for retreat, though nothing on Saqarro is large enough to make it retreat.

It marks its ground without lifting a leg like a tree-cat or dragging claws like a Skell. The whole low country marks for it. Crushed reed. Stripped mud. Trees polished smooth where its hide has rubbed away moss and beetle nests. Pools emptied of frogs. Paths where even flies are fewer. Wherever it rests, the green gives way and then grows back wrong, bent toward the shape of its passing.

Once I find a pile of dung high as my knee and full of little white bones.

Bird bones. Fish spines. Two Vorth finger bones, too large and dark to be human.

Marrow sees them when I do. He makes no sound. He only touches his own fingers, then mine, as if counting what a mouth can take from the hand.

Once, near dusk, it drags a dead Mure from the shallows.

The pale body hangs from its mouth, webbed hands limp, eyes open to the leaves. The Hesh does not eat at once. It carries the body to the edge of the black pool and lays it there as if making an offering to its own hunger. Then it waits.

More Mure rise in the channel.

Five. Seven. Maybe more under the skin of water.

They watch the dead one.

The Hesh watches them.

No sound passes between them.

After a long while the Mure sink.

Only then does the Hesh eat.

I lie in the mud with the insects tasting my eyelids and understand something I wish I did not.

Even monsters have politics.

The Hesh does not only kill. It reminds the drowned ones where the border lies.

The Mure do not come for the body. They come to be seen not coming. That is a kind of speech. The Hesh understands it. So do they. So do the Skell watching from the scree with their bellies pressed to stone.

Every strength is tied to a ground.

Change the ground, and strength becomes a weight.

Marrow comes with me on the second day. I try to send him back. He bares his little teeth and follows anyway. He is light enough for the rot skin and quick enough to vanish when the Hesh lifts its eyes. More important, he sees what I see before I finish showing it.

There is one run the Hesh trusts.

A strip of firmer root mat between the pool and a deeper thicket. Beside it lies a black throat of mud, hidden under leaves and moss. A Vorth would vanish there. Even the Hesh avoids it by the width of a hand.

So we move the truth.

Where the ground is firm, we loosen the roots beneath and leave the skin whole. Where the mud throat waits, we lace vine and root across it and cover the weave until it looks safe enough for one breath.

One breath is all we need.

We are wrong the first time.

That is the part songs would cut away if the Vorth made songs the way men do. Songs like clean teeth and clean victories. The island does not.

Our first snare lies too high. I think of Vorth ankles and Hesh forelegs. I forget the belly.

At dusk the Hesh comes for a half-rotten river-pig we drag near the root mat with hooks of bent thorn. It smells the bait, circles once, and slides forward. The vine loop tightens around the ridge behind its foreleg.

For one glorious breath the line holds.

Marrow's eyes blaze.

Then the belly rolls over the vine and the vine disappears into mud with a soft tearing sound. The Hesh does not even thrash. It keeps going, dragging half our labor under its weight as if the green itself has reached up and failed to hold it.

The river-pig vanishes into the mouth.

The Hesh chews twice.

The vines float up behind it in ruined strings.

Marrow kicks a root so hard he hurts his foot and has to bite his wrist to stay silent. I want to do the same. Not kick. Bite. Something in me cannot bear the calm of the great mouth. It should know we tried. It should turn. It should fear the clever little shadows working at the edge of its sleep.

It does not.

That night we rebuild lower.

The second failure costs blood.

A Skell finds our bait before the Hesh does. Not Grist, not the twisted one, but a younger Skell with white scars over its muzzle. Hunger has pulled it too far from stone. It darts from the reeds, snatches at the pig flesh, and sets one claw through a loop we buried under leaves.

The line snaps tight.

The Skell screams.

Every bird in the low green lifts.

Marrow looks at me in horror. I move before thinking, crawling fast through the black water to cut the vine loose. The Skell sees only a body coming from shadow. It bites my hand hard enough to meet bone.

Pain flares white.

The Hesh lifts its eyes from the pool.

All sound in me stops.

I cut the vine. The Skell tears free and scrambles into the reeds, dragging one bad leg. Its blood marks the leaves in bright drops. The Hesh turns toward that color.

I press my bitten hand into the mud.

Not to hide the wound. To hide the scent.

The Red stirs under my ribs, warm and eager as a coal given air. Blood wants answer. Teeth want answer. The Skell bit me after I freed it. There is a clean cruel shape to that, and the Red loves clean cruel shapes.

Marrow reaches me and shoves his curled hand over my mouth.

The pressure is clumsy and damp and full of fear.

It brings me back.

The Hesh slides through the reeds toward the Skell's trail. We lie in the mud, my hand burning, Marrow half on top of me to hold me down though he is smaller than my terror.

The Skell escapes because it knows stone.

We escape because the Hesh chooses the easier trail and loses it at the rocks.

When the green goes still again, Marrow takes my bitten hand and turns it over. Blood wells black in the moonlight. He makes a small broken sound.

By morning the bite has closed.

The shame has not.

I did not mean to spend another body's pain as lesson. I did not mean to pull a Skell into our trap. Meaning does not matter to the bitten leg. The island does not ask whether a plan was noble before it names the cost.

We change the trap again.

No free bait. No high loop. No line that can catch a hungry small mouth before the large one comes. The ground must be the snare. The Hesh must choose the snare itself.

That is harder.

Better.

The work takes days. We move when the Hesh sleeps. We freeze when its eyes lift. A poisoned spine enters my forearm and sets the blood on fire. The arm swells, tight and shiny.

By night the swelling is gone.

Marrow watches the healing with a face too still for a child.

I know.

I want answers too.

He touches the place where the swelling was, then touches his own crooked hand.

No healing there.

The question is plain.

Why you?

I could turn away. That is what I do with questions that press too close to the mark and the name and the places in me that return whole when they should not.

Instead I put my hand over his curled one.

I have no answer.

He studies my face.

Then, with great seriousness, he licks the back of my healed forearm.

I jerk away.

He spits into the mud, offended by the taste of me, and gives the Vorth sound for spoiled fruit.

The laugh that comes out of me nearly gets us killed.

The Hesh lifts its eyes.

We freeze.

My chest shakes silently. Marrow's shoulders shake too. For a terrible breath the largest mouth on the island stares toward two fools hidden in reeds, and the only fact saving us is that laughter can be held in the body if terror holds it down hard enough.

When the Hesh lowers its head again, Marrow gives me a look of deep accusation.

As if I am the one who licked poison-healed skin.

I love him then.

The thought arrives clean and simple and almost knocks the breath from me.

Not hunger. Not use. Not debt.

Love.

I have no safe place to put it, so I put it where I keep my name and go back to tying the trap.

The trap is only half the hunt. The Hesh must come fast enough to trust the ground it knows. For that, it needs bait.

Me.

Marrow understands before I finish the gestures. His curled hand closes on my wrist. For a moment he looks very young.

"High," I tell him, pointing to the leaning tree and the vine line. "Pull. Hold."

He hates it.

He goes.

At dawn the Hesh leaves its pool.

I step into the open.

Its eyes find me at once.

The great head swings. The mouth opens. For the first time, the Hesh hurries.

The world narrows to distances.

Sixteen strides to the false ground.

Ten to the tree where Marrow waits with the vine.

Four to the little rise that might keep my legs above the first rush if the Hesh strikes from the side.

None of those distances care that I am afraid.

I show it blood.

Only a thumb cut, shallow, but the scent opens in the damp air. The Hesh's mouth widens. The crab-spiders behind its jaw scatter deeper into the plates.

I run.

Not fast at first. Fast enough to promise panic, slow enough to keep the great head behind me. A hunted body wants to spend all its strength at once. I have been hunted too long to obey that want. I count mud patches. I step where root holds. I hear the Hesh behind me, not as footsteps, but as the collapse of everything soft.

The breath of it reaches my back.

My blood answers.

There is a joy under the fear. I hate it. The clean edge of being chased by death, the whole world stripped down to move or be meat. The Red likes that clean edge. It hums under my ribs, asking to turn, asking to meet the mouth with hands and teeth and impossible heat.

No.

Run.

Then Voss-Marr appears on the bank.

He has followed me.

Of course he has.

The cruel part of me wants to call it pride only. Pride must witness failure to feed itself. But that is not all of him. Voss-Marr has been bred to stand where danger comes. The tribe looked to his back before it looked to mine. If a wrong-blood child walks into the mouth of the island and returns with the answer, what is left for the son of the king except to follow and prove he was never afraid of the question?

He steps onto the old firm run, the ground I loosened beneath its green skin, and drops to the thigh in black mud.

The Hesh is coming.

There is no time to hate him.

I run to Voss-Marr instead of away.

He thrashes once. The mud takes him deeper. I slap his wrist and give Kala's stilling sound, harsh and broken.

Be still.

He looks at me with fury, terror, disbelief.

"Still," I rasp.

He obeys.

That saves him.

It also costs me the lead I built.

The Hesh is close enough now that its shadow falls over both of us. I smell the dead Mure between its teeth. I see my own blood bright on the mud where I cut my thumb. One eye fixes on Voss-Marr. The other fixes on me.

It knows two meals when one offered itself.

Voss-Marr's hand closes around my wrist. For one wild instant I think he means to pull me into the mud so he can climb over me. I see the thought cross his face too, not chosen, not yet, but alive. A drowning body will build stairs from love, hate, bone, whatever lies nearest.

Then his grip changes.

He pushes.

Not enough to free himself. Enough to give me the width of a breath.

I hate him less in that breath than I did before.

Then there is no room for hate.

I put his hand on the true root line and roll away as the Hesh lunges. Its whole weight comes down where I stood a heartbeat before.

The ground opens.

The Hesh drops.

Mud swallows its front half. The long body thrashes, teeth snapping on air. Marrow screams from the tree and throws his whole crooked weight onto the vine line, pulling the woven skin tight over the throat so the Hesh cannot find root to climb.

I get to the line and pull with him.

The Hesh fights like strength always fights when the ground betrays it. Harder. Wilder. Deeper into its own death.

Voss-Marr crawls onto true ground and does not run.

He watches the two smallest creatures in the green hold the apex under.

Mud covers him to the ribs. It has taken the beauty from him, the clean sure shape of the heir, and left a young male with terror in his teeth and shame bright in his eyes. He sees me see it.

That wound will last longer than the mud.

For one moment the three of us are joined by the line.

Marrow above, all crooked fury and clenched teeth.

Voss-Marr on the bank, mud to his waist, pride stripped from him in black ropes.

Me between them, pulling until my palms split.

The Hesh thrashes below us.

If the line breaks, we all die.

Voss-Marr sees that.

He gets his hands on the vine.

Not for me.

Not for Marrow.

For the tribe, maybe. For himself. For the pride that says he is not a body to be saved and set aside.

For the future that was supposed to be his, and is slipping by inches through my split palms.

He pulls.

The vine tightens until bark fibers cut under his nails.

Together, we hold.

When the thrashing slows, something in me leans toward it.

Not hunger of the belly. Not glory. The red wanting that opened when the swine bled and grew quiet when the Hesh first passed me. It rises now as the great killer goes still.

Take the neck.

Make the fear yours.

I set my teeth around my hidden name until the wanting passes.

I will kill to live.

I will not live only to kill.

We cannot drag the whole body home, so I take the head.

It costs hours and most of my strength. The dead men's knife saws through hide and gristle. Marrow helps with both hands. Voss-Marr does not help, but he stays near enough that the jungle reads him as with us.

For him, that may be a kind of help.

At the last, when the head finally parts and drops heavy into the mud, he puts one hand under the lower jaw before I can. He lifts for one breath, no more, just long enough for me to get the vine under it.

Then he lets go as if he has touched fire.

I leave thanks buried. Every sound I could make would turn the moment sour.

We reach the high stones after dark.

The tribe is gathered, waiting for death.

I lay the Hesh's head beside the fire.

For a moment no one moves.

Then the fear breaks.

Torn-Ear bows first. Others follow. Kala pulls me against her and gives the full stilling sound, all the way to its end.

Be held.

Nothing comes.

But Gull-Marr watches from the lip with narrowed eyes.

Voss-Marr stands below him, wet to the waist with rot, and whatever passed between us in the mud is already hardening into something sharper.

I saved him.

That will not make him love me.

Some men hate most deeply the hand that proves they needed one.

# Chapter 17: The Challenge

Gull-Marr waits.

That is worse than striking.

After the Hesh, the tribe turns toward me too brightly for him to move at once. The dead head lies by the fire. Small ones creep close to touch the teeth. Hunters speak my sounds more often. Torn-Ear will not cross rot without looking for my mark stones.

The king lets the brightness fade.

I keep it bright.

Each day I make myself useful where everyone can see. I mark unsafe ground with pale stones. I reset the traps with Marrow. I show the half-grown hunters how to read a mud skin before it opens. I feed the fire through rain. I catch a falling child from a branch before his mother sees the limb crack.

None of it is accident.

Power is not only strength. Power is where eyes go when danger starts.

More eyes come to me now.

They come with needs, not songs.

A mother brings a young one with a swollen foot and waits to see if I know which thorn did it. A scarred male drops two fish at the fire and watches whose hand I send them to. The old she with the torn lip grunts over a sleeping place taken by a stronger cousin, and the whole shelf looks up to see whether my new law has teeth when no monster is bleeding in front of it.

I learn that a king spends more time with hunger than glory.

Meat first to the hurt foot. Fire space back to the torn lip. The strong cousin sent to the outer ledge with Torn-Ear watching. None of it wins a roar. It only keeps the night from bending wrong.

Gull-Marr sees all of it.

So does Voss-Marr.

Voss-Marr feels it.

He stands at the edge of every lesson like a storm deciding where to break. The line of succession was supposed to run through him cleanly: Gull-Marr, then Voss-Marr, then whatever sons his strength made. I have stepped onto that line without being bred for it, without even understanding all its words.

He cannot forgive that.

He is not alone.

That is what I learn in the days after the Hesh.

Hatred by itself is simple. One body. One mouth. One set of hands. Faction is harder to see because it hides inside agreement. A glance held too long. A grunt answered from the wrong side of the fire. Meat passed around my place instead of through it. Young hunters standing nearer Voss-Marr than they used to, not touching him, not making a promise anyone can point at, but leaving their weight in his shadow.

There are five I begin to count.

Red-Shoulder, who took the first strip of Hesh hide and has not forgiven the narrow wrist-binding I gave him instead of the whole glory. Bent-Jaw, old enough to have followed Gull-Marr through more seasons than I have survived. Two half-grown brothers with matching white scars across their knuckles, always laughing a breath after Voss-Marr laughs. And a lean female with one blind eye who never speaks in my hearing but watches Kala as if measuring whether a mother's claim can be broken.

They are not fools.

That is the trouble.

When I give extra meat to the coughing young, Red-Shoulder bares his teeth at the lean hunters who came back empty. He is right that hunger will make them weaker tomorrow. When I mark a lower path forbidden after rain, Bent-Jaw grunts that river-pig will breed there and fatten without us. He is right too. When I allow Grist to creep close enough to the fire for warmth one wet evening, the blind-eyed female lifts her lip and points toward the dead shelf.

Skell eat the dead if hunger drives them hard enough.

I know that.

So do the Vorth mothers sleeping under bones they keep clean with their own hands.

Being right does not make them kind.

Being unkind does not make them wrong.

That is a harder law than mud.

Voss-Marr does not speak first. He lets them bring the edges of the argument. Then he stands behind those edges and becomes their shape. He is good at it. Better than I want him to be. He can make his silence feel like injured order. He can make his wounds visible without seeming to beg. He can make the tribe remember that before I came, every body knew where to stand.

I have brought meat and paths and Hesh teeth.

I have also brought confusion.

One rain-sour afternoon, the confusion breaks open.

A child from the lower mothers carries a strip of roasted liver to Grist where he crouches under the lip. He means kindness. Or mischief. With children those roads cross often. Red-Shoulder catches the child by the wrist and yanks him back hard enough to make him cry.

Kala rises.

The blind-eyed female rises too.

All at once the fire has two sides.

Grist freezes under the stone, liver fallen in the ash at his clawed feet. Marrow starts toward him, and I catch his shoulder before he can put his small body where every large anger wants to land.

Red-Shoulder points at Grist and gives the old carrion-sound.

Not tribe.

Not fire.

Voss-Marr stands behind him, not smiling, not stopping him.

The child sobs against his mother's leg. The mother looks at me, then at Kala, then at Gull-Marr on the lip.

Gull-Marr says nothing.

He wants me to spend myself first.

So I do.

I take the liver from the ash, wipe it clean on a leaf, and break it in half. One half I give to the child. One half I set on the stone between Grist and Red-Shoulder.

Then I point to the outer ring of the fire.

There.

Not inside. Not among the sleeping young. Not under the dead shelf. But not driven into the rain either.

Grist stares at the meat. Red-Shoulder stares at me.

It is not enough for anyone.

That is how I know it may hold.

Grist takes the meat and backs to the outer ring. Red-Shoulder spits into the ash but does not strike. Kala lowers herself slowly. The blind-eyed female stays standing one breath longer than Kala, long enough to make sure the tribe sees she chooses to lower herself, not obey.

Voss-Marr's gaze meets mine across the fire.

He has won part of that moment. So have I. That means neither of us has won enough.

I try, once, to give part of it back.

It happens after a hunt in the east slope, where the fruit trees crowd so thick the air smells drunk. Voss-Marr corners a young boar against a root wall and holds it there without killing it, waiting for the half-grown hunters to form correctly around him. His command is good. His hands are good. No one moves until he moves.

This is what he was made for.

I see it, and because I see it, I step back.

Let the eyes go to him.

Let the meat be his.

Let the tribe remember the future it expected.

Marrow looks up at me, confused.

The boar breaks left.

A half-grown hunter slips. Voss-Marr catches the error before anyone else. He throws himself across the path, takes the boar's shoulder in his ribs, and drives it back into the waiting arms. Clean. Brave. Costly.

The hunters roar for him.

I roar too.

His eyes find mine through the bodies.

For one breath I think he understands.

Then Torn-Ear claps my shoulder and makes the sound for my mark stones, telling the young ones that the kill worked because the east slope path had been cleared earlier by me.

The eyes split.

Some to Voss-Marr.

Some to me.

There it is again. The theft I cannot stop committing by being useful.

Voss-Marr's ribs bleed where the tusk opened him. He presses one hand to the wound and looks at me as if I put the tusk there myself.

He had done it right. That is the worst of it. No foolishness. No empty showing. He took the hit a leader takes, and the meat would have been his moment if my usefulness had not already laid itself under the ground like a root.

I want to give him the whole kill.

Wanting does not move the eyes back.

The seer makes it worse.

Before dawn, the old one comes through the sleeping tribe and touches the eye-and-thorn at my chest. He gives the meaning-sound. Then he points to Gull-Marr on the lip and gives it again.

This one.

That one.

One telling.

His finger passes over Voss-Marr without stopping.

The heir is awake enough to see it.

So is the king.

So are the five who have made a weather around him.

Red-Shoulder's mouth opens, then closes when Bent-Jaw's hand touches his arm. The blind-eyed female makes a soft click in her throat. The two scarred brothers look at Voss-Marr first, not Gull-Marr.

That is the true insult.

The seer has not only skipped Voss-Marr.

He has made everyone watch everyone else notice.

Voss-Marr's face becomes so still it is almost beautiful. All youth and anger pressed under the mask of a future king who has just been told the future walked around him.

For a breath I pity him.

Then I remember pity is also a height one body stands on above another, and he would rather be struck than pitied by me.

Gull-Marr comes down.

The whole shelf tightens. Small ones vanish into adult arms. Marrow slips off my shoulder. Kala rises behind me with a sound in her chest I have never heard, low and dangerous.

She would stand between us.

I put my hand on her arm.

For once, I give her the stilling sound.

Be still.

It hurts her. I feel that. She has kept me through fever, fear, and night. Every law in her body tells her to put herself between danger and the life she chose.

But if she stands for me, my standing is only her shadow.

I step out from under her arm.

Onto the open stone.

Gull-Marr towers over me. Old strength, scarred strength, the whole law of the high stones in one body. He could kill me quickly. He will not. Quick killing would read as fear. He needs the tribe to see strength put cleverness back in its place.

He gives the challenge-sound.

Low. Full. Bare.

Here.

This stone.

You and me.

Every Vorth goes still.

I should step back.

The part of me that survived the hold knows how. Yield. Flatten. Make no shape. Let the hand pass over.

But the home behind me is not lent anymore. Kala gave me a place among the dead. Torn-Ear lifted my wrist. The tribe followed my marks. Marrow carries my name for him like a coal.

If I step back, all of that becomes something I borrowed.

I touch my name behind my teeth.

Then I answer.

My sound is rough, smaller than his, scraped raw by a throat that still remembers silence. It carries the meaning anyway.

Here.

This stone.

You and me.

The tribe hears it.

Voss-Marr hears it most of all.

I have answered over his head. The challenge that should have been his to give one day has passed him by and found me instead.

Gull-Marr watches me for a long breath. Something almost like respect moves behind his flat eyes, too old and buried to soften him.

Then he turns and walks back to his lip.

Not refusal.

Promise.

The fight is named now.

Kala folds down around me after he goes. She does not say nothing comes. She will not lie to a child she has chosen.

Instead she rests her great head against mine, and for a little while we breathe together.

Marrow creeps back to my side.

Across the stones, Voss-Marr stands very still.

I have gained the king's answer.

I have made the heir my enemy.

# Chapter 18: King of the Wild Things

Gull-Marr clears the stone at first light.

The tribe moves to the edges. Small ones are held high. The dead look down from their clean shelf. The seer stands apart with his pale head lifted.

Voss-Marr watches from the side.

This should have been his future. His father on the stone. His own strength waiting below. The line whole and clear. Instead I stand in the center with bare feet, a dead man's knife at my hip, and no strength worth naming.

Gull-Marr comes slowly.

The slow walk is part of the fight. It says I have already measured you. It says the stone is mine until I choose to let your blood touch it.

I believe him.

That helps.

Believing a danger lets you read it.

He rushes only at the end.

Old strength becomes sudden strength. One arm swings to take me from the stone. I drop under it, not away, in. The arm cuts air over my back. I go low at his knee with all my weight, not striking muscle but borrowing it. His leg expects the ground to hold one way. I ask it another.

The knee folds.

Gull-Marr falls.

The whole tribe inhales.

He is already rising.

Of course he is. I cannot break him. The fall only proves the lesson I brought with me from the mud: strength can be read. Strength can be turned. Strength can be made to answer ground it never noticed.

The second rush is not the first.

That is why he is king.

Gull-Marr does not spend anger where I expect it. He slows. One broad hand lowers, fingers spread, and he begins to circle. The stone is not large enough for circling, but he makes it feel large by making me choose where to give ground. Behind me is the lip. To my left, slick ash. To my right, the shallow groove where rain gathers and makes a foot lie.

He sees all of it.

So do I.

For a moment we are both reading the same page.

Then he tears it.

He stamps into the ash, not slipping, making the gray dust leap into my eyes. I turn my face too late. His backhand catches my shoulder and throws me across the groove. Pain lights my arm from neck to fingertips. The tribe barks sound. Kala's sound cuts through them, half roar, half grief.

I roll because falling bodies taught me to roll long before anyone taught me to stand. Gull-Marr's foot comes down where my ribs were. Stone cracks under his heel.

If that lands, I am meat.

I come up half blind, one arm numb, mouth full of ash.

Gull-Marr waits.

Not mercy.

Instruction.

He wants the tribe to see I can be touched. He wants the young hunters to see that cleverness bleeds. He wants me to know the first fall did not make him small.

It works.

I am afraid again.

Good.

Fear keeps count.

My left arm hangs badly. Not broken, I think. It does not answer well enough to know. The dead man's knife rests at my hip, allowed by no rule anyone has spoken. I could draw it. A sharp edge solves distance.

It also answers the wrong question.

This is not a killing.

This is witness.

Gull-Marr comes again, low this time, one hand feinting high while the other sweeps for my leg. I let him have the leg.

Not all of it.

Enough.

His fingers close around my ankle. The grip is iron. He yanks, and the stone leaps sideways under me. I fall onto my back and he is over me, all gray hide and old scars, one hand rising to pin my throat.

The hold comes back.

Dark planks. Wet breath. A hand on the back of my neck. The command to become small or be made smaller.

The Red opens so fast it has no heat at first. Only clarity.

Bite the wrist.

Take the eye.

Open the soft place under the arm.

No.

His fingers brush my throat.

I twist toward them, not away. My good hand catches his thumb and folds it back against the joint. Not enough to break. Enough to turn the whole arm for one breath. My knee comes up under his ribs where the boar tusk once taught me how even strong bodies make hinges.

Gull-Marr grunts.

That sound is worth more than blood.

I slide out from under him and leave skin from my shoulder on the stone.

The tribe has gone silent again.

Voss-Marr is leaning forward now despite himself. He wants his father to crush me. He also wants to know how I keep finding doors in walls.

Gull-Marr rises slower.

So do I.

My breath tears. My shoulder screams. The world has too many edges. The Red stays close, offended that I will not use it properly. It offers me an easy road every time Gull-Marr's throat turns bare.

Easy roads often lead to cages.

I spit ash and blood onto the stone.

Gull-Marr sees the blood.

He smiles.

Not kind. Not cruel. A king's smile, old and tired under the teeth.

Then he comes for the end.

For one breath his neck is open.

The red wanting rises.

Take it.

Make him small.

Put your hand where the slavers put theirs.

I see it clearly. My fingers in the gray hide at his neck. My knee on his back. The king held the way I was held, made into property by the fear he gave others.

I step away.

I take the lip instead.

Before Gull-Marr has fully risen, I am on the king's stone above him. His place. The place from which he weighed every body on the shelf and let it stand or not.

Now I stand there.

Gull-Marr rises below me.

He could come up.

He could take me. Everyone knows this. I know it. He knows it.

But the tribe has turned.

Not because I am stronger. Because I put him down and did not take his neck. Because the strength that ruled them has been read in front of them. Because the small wrong boy on the lip has brought them meat, fire, paths through rot, the Hesh's head, and now a new answer:

The strong may rule.

The one who reads the strong may choose what strength becomes.

Gull-Marr looks at the tribe.

At Torn-Ear, who bows his head to me.

At Kala, still as stone below the dead shelf.

At the seer, whose old eyes are fixed on the mark at my chest.

At Voss-Marr, rigid with a wound no hand has touched.

Then the old king lets it stand.

He turns from the lip and walks down into the green alone.

No one follows.

The jungle takes his shape by degrees: gray hide, scarred back, old shoulders, then nothing.

I watch until he is gone.

Mercy feels nothing like softness.

It feels like holding a blade by the edge and refusing to drop it.

Voss-Marr does not bow.

He does not need to. His hatred is clearer than any bow would be. I spared his father and took his future in the same breath. Worse, I spared him too. I stepped over the heir, defeated the king, and left the line alive beneath me.

That kind of mercy makes enemies who remember.

He stands with one hand pressed to the place where the boar opened him. His blood has dried black there. Mud from the Hesh hunt still clings in the fur at his thigh, though he has washed twice. He could scrape it out. He has not. Maybe he wants the tribe to remember he was there. Maybe he wants me to remember he fell.

The tribe comes to the lip.

Torn-Ear first. He stands beside me and faces the green. Others follow. Hunters. Mothers. Half-grown males with uncertain eyes. The discarded ones come too: the limping, scarred, crooked, too-small bodies the old order pushed toward the edges. Marrow climbs last, using one hand and his teeth where the rock is steep, and plants himself at my side as if he has always stood there.

Then come the disputes.

Not later. Not after song or rest. At once.

A young hunter lays a strip of Hesh hide at the foot of the lip and pounds his chest for the right to wear it. Torn-Ear answers with an old sound that gives it to the hunt, not the hunter. Two mothers bare teeth over a sleeping hollow near the fire, each with a small one coughing in the wet heat. A scarred male drops old bone counters on the stone and waits for me to read a debt I have never seen made.

The crown is not a clean height above the tribe.

It is where the tribe brings its knots.

I solve the first badly. I give the hide to Torn-Ear because he is oldest in the hunt, and the young hunter's face goes dead-flat with insult before I understand the Hesh head was mine to give and his to ask for in front of everyone. Kala rumbles low behind me, not anger, warning. Marrow taps twice on the stone and points with his chin at the young hunter's bleeding palms.

Right.

Not hide. Blood.

I cut a narrow strip from the hide and bind it around the hunter's wrist myself. Then I set the rest by the fire for all who held the line, even those who held only by waiting. The young hunter lifts his wrist. Torn-Ear grunts approval.

One knot loosens.

I breathe again.

The twisted Skell comes after Marrow.

No one calls it.

No one stops it either, because the whole tribe is trapped inside a moment larger than its old laws. It climbs badly, claws scraping stone, bad leg dragging. Halfway up, a young Vorth hisses and lifts a hand to knock it down.

Marrow bites him.

Not hard enough to wound. Hard enough to make a point.

The young one jerks back, outraged. Marrow plants himself between him and the Skell with his curled hand high and his teeth bared.

The tribe waits to see what I will do.

So does Voss-Marr.

So does the Skell.

Here is a kingdom, then. Not the lip. Not the sound they give me. This small ugly choosing in front of everyone. What happens to the castoff after the old order has been broken and the new one has not yet learned its own shape?

I look at the Skell.

It lowers its narrow head and sets the shell it once gave me on the stone between us.

The same shell I placed near the small dead one. It must have stolen it back from the shelf, or taken another like it from the shallows. Either way, it offers what it has.

The Vorth mutter.

The dead shelf is sacred. The shell is not meat. The Skell is not tribe. Every old line crosses here.

I pick up the shell.

Then I set it on the king's lip beside my bare foot.

Allowed.

The twisted Skell crawls the last length and crouches below Marrow, shaking with fear and triumph.

The smell of it brings the old order up in every Vorth throat. Carrion. Mud. Fear-sweat. The edge of dead meat. It knows how it smells to them. I see that in the way it keeps its belly low and its eyes down. The Skell have survived by making themselves smaller than hunger.

I know that lesson too.

When it reaches my foot, Voss-Marr's mouth curls.

Not disgust only.

Judgment.

This is what you choose, his body says. These over blood. Leavings over heirs.

He is wrong.

The judgment still has teeth.

Later I will need a name for him. Not now. A name is not a stone thrown to make a moment pretty. It must fit the body it lands on.

But I know the shape of it already.

Grist.

What the strong grind off and leave.

What still feeds a fire if someone bothers to gather it.

Kala comes after them.

She folds down beside me and lays her arm around my back. Not to hold me up. Not to keep me in place. Simply because she can, and I allow it.

She gives the stilling sound.

Be held.

Nothing comes.

This time it is true for the length of the sound.

The seer touches the eye-and-thorn, then lays his palm over my heart. He gives the meaning-sound with a question hidden inside it.

He still does not know what I am.

Neither do I.

But I know what I am to the Vorth now.

I am the one on the lip.

The one who brought down the Hesh.

The one who read the king and let him live.

The one the weak gather to first because I remember the taste of being thrown away.

King is not the word the Vorth use. Their sound is older, rougher, full of tooth and shelter both. I take it in, but behind my teeth I set my own meaning beside it.

King of the wild.

The title is too large for me.

So was the jungle.

So was hunger.

So was the Hesh.

I stand on the lip while the sun rises over the green, with Kala's warmth at my back, Marrow at my side, the tribe below, and my name still hidden where no one can take it.

For the first time, I have a place no hand is holding for me.

That is why I know it can be taken.

# Chapter 19: Smoke on the Water

For three days after Gull-Marr yields, the island gives me peace.

Not safety. The green never gives that. Safety is a word men invented for places they have not read closely enough. The island still has teeth under leaves, fever in the mud, water that looks sweet until the gut cramps around it. But the tribe turns when I move. The young ones watch me openly now. The scarred and crooked ones come nearer the fire. Even the strong males who used to look through me have learned to look at the ground first and then at my face.

I sit on the lip where Gull-Marr sat.

It is only stone. Wet in the morning, warm at noon, slick with old ash near the fire. But a place can be more than itself when enough bodies agree to it. On that stone, the tribe's anger comes to me first. Its hunger. Its fear. Its small disputes over meat and sleeping space and which young hunter took more marrow than he earned. I have no words for most of it. I have eyes, hands, stillness, and the lessons the hold beat into me.

Men taught me power by putting a hand on the back of my neck.

The Vorth teach me a different shape. A look can turn a quarrel aside. A piece of meat set down in front of the weakest can shame a stronger hand away from it. A silence can weigh more than a blow if everyone knows the blow is waiting behind it.

I am learning.

Learning costs more than winning.

The Hesh head draws flies for two days before we burn what is left of it. Its teeth become blades and charms. Its hide becomes strips around wrists, spears, carrying poles. The white crab-spiders that lived behind its jaw scatter into the stones, and small ones chase them with shrieks until Kala's look puts every child back on a safer ledge. The tribe laughs at that. Even the torn-lipped old she laughs, a dry sound like bark cracking.

I laugh too, late and quiet.

The sound feels strange in my mouth.

Kala watches me learn with one dark eye and one hand always near enough to close around my shoulder. She does not crowd me. That is the kindness of it. She lets me sit on the lip alone while making the whole tribe know I am not alone at all.

Marrow understands the new order before I do. He has spent his life reading where not to stand. Now he finds the places beside me where no one can push him off without pushing against my shadow too. He does not become proud. Pride is for creatures born straight enough to expect room. Marrow becomes exact. He carries messages I cannot say. He brings the scarred ones forward. He sends bold young males away with one flat look from his yellow eye, as if my crown has given his crooked body a longer reach.

It warms a place I keep hidden.

Sometimes, when no quarrel needs me and no hunger has teeth in it, he climbs the lip and sits with his shoulder against my knee. He pretends to watch the green. Mostly he watches me.

If I sit too straight, he copies me until his crooked back complains and he makes a grave show of dying from kingship. If a young hunter struts below, Marrow struts along the lip on three limbs with such terrible majesty that two small ones fall over laughing.

The first time he does it, I try to look stern.

I fail.

For three days, I am almost young.

On the second evening, the tribe brings me a peace that is not peaceful at all.

It starts with water.

The upper spring runs thin after three rainless days. The lower pool is fuller, but fever blooms there in green threads, and the Hesh's old path still cuts too close to the bank. The mothers want the clean spring for the small ones. The hunters want it for washing wounds before rot finds them. The old want it because old mouths dry quickly and pride dries faster.

Everyone is right.

That is what makes the shouting rise.

Voss-Marr stands with Red-Shoulder and the blind-eyed female near the spring path. He says little. He has learned how little he needs to say. His presence gives shape to the claim: hunters bring meat, meat keeps children alive, therefore the water that keeps hunters whole is not stolen when hunters take it first.

Kala stands with the mothers.

She says nothing either.

Her silence has a different weight. It remembers fever. It remembers me burning under her hand. It remembers every small body she has seen carried to the dead shelf because water looked clean and was not.

The tribe looks to the lip.

I want to run into the green and fight another Hesh.

A mouth with teeth is easier than thirsty people.

Marrow nudges my ankle. He has a river snail in one hand and the expression of someone prepared to solve kingship by eating during it. Then he points with his chin toward the old carrying poles left from the Hesh hide.

Poles.

Skin.

Hands.

The answer is not who owns the water.

The answer is labor.

I climb down from the lip and walk to the spring. No one moves as I take the first bowl. I carry it to the smallest coughing child and set it in his mother's hands.
