Then I take the second bowl to Bent-Jaw, whose gums are cracked and bleeding. The third goes to a hunter with a red wound at his thigh. After that, I point to the Hesh hide stretched near the fire, then to the carrying poles, then to the lower pool, then to the flat stones where fire can boil water in shell bowls.

No one understands at once.

Marrow does. He always does when there is work in a plan. He scuttles to the hide and drags at it with his teeth until Torn-Ear snorts and rises to help. Soon the hide is slung between two poles, a sagging skin basin. Two hunters carry it to the lower pool with Voss-Marr watching, too proud not to join once Red-Shoulder does. The lower water comes back in the hide. We heat stones in the fire and drop them into shell bowls until steam lifts. The first bowl goes to a child. The second to the wounded hunter. The third to Bent-Jaw.

By the time moonrise silvers the lip, the spring is not empty, the hunters have water for wounds, and the old have stopped baring gums at the young.

No one roars.

No one bows.

I am beginning to understand that good rule often sounds like people no longer shouting.

Then Grist creeps close with a bundle of bitter leaves in his claws.

Every Vorth eye turns.

He freezes. Rain-thin ribs show under his hide. The bundle shakes once. Skell eat rot and bone and soft dead meat. Skell steal from middens. Skell do not walk into Vorth firelight with medicine.

Grist drops the leaves and backs away.

The blind-eyed female hisses. Red-Shoulder starts forward.

I pick up the leaves first.

I know the smell. Kala used them when fever took me, crushed into mud and pressed behind the knees to pull heat. The Skell know them too. Of course they do. A body thrown to the edges learns every bitter mercy the center ignores.

I carry the leaves to the wounded hunter and set them beside his thigh.

There.

Allowed.

The word has no clean Vorth sound yet, but the gesture holds. Torn-Ear grunts. Kala watches Grist with narrowed eyes, then makes room at the outer fire. Not much. A handspan. A kingdom can begin with less.

Grist does not come closer.

Not that night.

But he does not leave either.

Later, after the shouting has become eating and the eating has become the sleepy murmur of safe bellies, Marrow performs Gull-Marr for the children.

He climbs the lip, puffs his narrow chest, and lowers his brows until his whole face becomes a storm. Then he takes one grand step, slips on ash he placed there himself, and falls backward with all four limbs in the air.

The young ones explode.

Even Kala makes a coughing sound that might be laughter if laughter wore a mask. Torn-Ear turns away too late to hide his teeth. The blind-eyed female does not laugh, but her mouth softens at one corner before she remembers herself.

I should stop Marrow. Gull-Marr is alive somewhere in the green, and mockery has teeth that travel.

But Marrow rolls up, sees me looking stern, and immediately becomes me instead.

He sits on the lip with one solemn hand over his heart, stares at an imaginary dispute, and gives the smallest possible nod, as if solving thirst, hunger, succession, and the turning of the moon with the movement of one chin.

Then he ruins it by scratching under his arm.

I laugh so hard my hurt shoulder pulls fire.

For a while the tribe laughs with me.

Not at me.

With me.

The difference is a shore I never thought to reach.

After, when the fire lowers and the small ones sleep in warm knots, Kala brings me a cracked shell cup of boiled lower water. She drinks first, to show the tribe it is clean enough. Then she hands it to me.

No speech. No ceremony.

Only water made safe by many hands and given back through hers.

I drink. It tastes of smoke, stone, and bitter leaf. It tastes like a law that has survived its first night.

Marrow steals the last mouthful and looks deeply pleased with the theft.

I let him have it.

On the fourth morning I climb the seaward stack to read the tide for river-pig.

Marrow comes with me because he comes when I go, high shoulder raised against the wind, curled hand tucked near his chest. Below us the mangrove mouth opens into pale water. The low tide has drawn the flats bare in long shining ribs. White birds stab at pools. Farther out, haze blurs the line between sea and sky until the world looks unfinished.

I almost miss the shapes because I am happy.

That is the danger of happiness. It puts softness over the eye.

Marrow sees first. His body goes still beside me, so completely that the air seems to move around him and not through him. The curled hand opens once, closes, opens again.

I follow his gaze.

Far out on the pale water, long dark shapes hold against the swell.

Not logs. Logs yield. Not tide-beasts. Tide-beasts roll with water and vanish under it. These cut through the sea with intention. Their noses point toward the mangrove mouth. Small upright forms bend along their sides, pull together, rise together, pull again.

The word comes up whole from the drowned place inside me.

Boats.

My throat closes around it.

For a moment the island falls away. The stack, the birds, Marrow's quick breath, the lip waiting behind me, Kala's arm. All of it goes thin as mist, and I am back under wet planks with bodies pressed on every side and a hand teaching my neck the shape of ownership.

Boats mean hands.

Hands mean the hold.

Marrow makes a low sound. Not fear exactly. Something wiser than fear. He looks from the water to me, then back toward the high stones.

Go back, his body says.

Wake them.

Hide.

He is right.

I should go to Kala. Move the tribe into the broken ridges above the fever pockets. Set watchers on the rotten channels. Let the green do what the green does to men who cross it without permission. I know the island's tolls. Bad water. Swarm fever. Mud that drinks a boot and keeps the leg. The Mure under warm shallows. The Skell in the scree. Poison vines fine as hair.

The men in the boats know none of it.

They are already dying.

I know that before they reach shore, and still I stay turned toward the water.

That shame is its own wound.

If the boats had carried only hooks and teeth, I could have been wise. I could have run to the high stones and worn my crown like a duty. But they carry voices. They carry the old shape of my hands. They carry the chance, thin and poisoned as a thorn, that somewhere below there is a person who can look at my face and know the word human means me too.

The first boat grounds on the bone-spit where I found the wreck. Men jump into the shallows and haul the hull forward. Their voices carry in broken pieces across the water. Human voices. Not Vorth grunts, not Mure clicks, not Skell chittering from stone.

Words.

Thrown from mouth to mouth and caught easily.

The sound goes into me like water into cracked earth.

Marrow touches my arm with the back of his curled hand.

I look at him.

His face is hard with warning. He has never seen a boat. He does not need to. He reads me reading it, and he knows the danger is not only below.

I start down the stack.

Not toward the high stones.

Toward the coast.

Marrow bars my way for one breath. Small body, high shoulder, teeth showing. The sight of it should shame me. Maybe it does. I step around him anyway, and after a moment he follows, furious and faithful.

We go low through the wet rocks and root cages to the old lookout above the spit. The place smells of salt, rot, and dead history. The wreck lies half-swallowed by mangrove roots below, its ribs black in the mud. Men are building beside it as if bones are not warnings.

There are more of them than I first counted. Three boats. Crates. Hide bundles. Spears with hooks. Nets. Iron cages in pieces, unloaded and dragged above the tide line. Owners and owned, easy to tell once you know the trick. Owners carry nothing heavy. Owned men carry until their shoulders shake. Owners point. Owned men bend. The camp is still only a wound in the green, but already the wound has rules.

I hate them.

I lean toward them.

Both truths live in me without touching.

A man near the center gives orders without raising his voice. He is not the biggest. The biggest men are usually tools for smaller men with better eyes. This one stands still while everyone else moves around him. Even at this distance I can read that. Power has a scent even when the wind is wrong.

He turns once, and sunlight catches worked leather at his chest.

The eye-and-thorn.

My hand closes around the disc hidden under my shirt.

The same mark lay with the dead men. The same mark made the seer bow his old head and touch my blood like a question. Now living men wear it openly while they unload cages onto my shore.

Mine, the mark seems to say against my palm.

Theirs, the camp says back.

Both cannot be true.

Marrow presses his head hard into my shoulder. Not comfort. Command. He points uphill with his chin, and there is no mistaking him now.

Leave.

I should. I know every reason. Kala is behind me. The tribe behind her. The lip. The first home I ever earned instead of stole. I have meat, fire, standing, a place among the dead, and a crooked shadow at my side who would follow me into a pit if I let him.

I was full.

Then human voices come out of the boats and prove there was another emptiness under the full one.

The ache disgusts me. It is not noble. Not wise. It does not care that men put me in the hold. It does not care that men branded sound into my bones. It only knows that those figures below have my shape. Their hands work like mine. Their mouths hold the road I lost. Somewhere among them may be the word for the mark, the name of what I am, the answer to why the island failed to kill me when it killed every other human who came before.

Behind my broken teeth, my true name waits.

My name.

I could throw it down to them.

I could hear a human mouth catch it.

The want is so sharp I almost make a sound.

Below, a caged Mure is dragged from the second boat. Its webbed hands flex weakly in the ropes. Men laugh when it hisses. One prods it with a hook to make it move faster. The laugh cleans the want out of me for a breath.

Only for a breath.

That is the ugly truth. Cruelty should make the whole wanting die. It does not. It only teaches the wanting to hide from my judgment. I can hate the laugh and still lean toward the mouths that made it because those mouths know roads mine forgot.

Not kin, Marrow's body says beside me.

He is right again.

The captain turns toward the jungle as if he has heard us thinking.

We flatten into the wet green. My cheek presses mud. Marrow's breath stops. For three long counts the man with the eye-and-thorn studies the line of trees above the spit.

He cannot see us.

Still, I know when a hunter is reading cover.

At last he looks away.

I breathe.

Marrow does not move until I do. When I start downhill for a closer line, he catches my wrist. His grip is small and hard.

No.

I pull free.

The hurt in his face lands worse than teeth.

I tell myself I only need to read the camp. A king must know what comes to his shore. A hunter must know the trap before the trap closes. All true.

Truth is useful that way. It makes a good roof for shame.

By dusk the men have fires burning where no human fire should hold. Smoke climbs into the hot still air and stands over the water like a dark thread stitched between the camp and the sky.

Smoke on the water.

A sign any creature can read.

We came. We burn. We are not afraid.

The island hears.

So do I.

When the camp settles, Marrow finally stops trying to pull me home. He lies beside me with his curled hand over his eyes, as if he cannot bear to watch me watching them.

I stay until the first fever-swarms rise from the mud and pour toward the human fires.

Only then do I turn back.

Kala is waiting at the high stones.

She smells the smoke on me before I reach the fire. Her nostrils widen. Her hand closes once in the air, empty, then opens. She looks past me for Marrow. He comes out of the dark at my heel, shoulders tight with anger and fear.

Kala touches his head first.

Then mine.

The order of it wounds me because it is fair.

The seer comes from the dead shelf and lays two fingers on the disc beneath my shirt. He gives the danger-sound toward the coast, low and sharp enough that the nearest young ones turn.

Danger.

There.

Yes, I think.

And something else.

But I have no words for the else. Not in Vorth. Not in the broken human scraps left to me. So I say nothing while the smoke from the water clings to my skin, and the human voices keep moving inside my head long after the camp has gone quiet.

# Chapter 20: The Slavers Camp

They have built where the dead built.

I know it before I reach the old wreck bar. The green is wrong there. Open. Hurt. Men have hacked the canopy back from the mud and dragged whole roots aside, leaving pale torn wood exposed to the hot air. The jungle closes every wound it can. Vines drop, moss creeps, leaves turn toward any break in the sky.

Only men hold a wound open and call it camp.

Marrow stops at the last drowned root.

His whole body says no.

The high shoulder drops. The curled hand works once against his chest and goes still. He looks toward the clearing, then up the hidden trail toward the high stones, then back at me with the patience of someone trying not to bare his teeth at a fool.

I should listen.

Instead I touch his shoulder and go on without him.

The smell reaches me first.

Tar. Smoke. Iron. Sweat. Piss. Sickness starting under all of it, still faint, the first sour thread in cloth not yet rotted through. Then the hold comes back in one breath: dark planks, bodies too close, water under the floor, a hand on the neck, the sound that meant come, be still, be owned.

I lie under cut brush and make my skin stop crawling.

The camp moves by rules.

That is the first truth I read once the fear settles enough to let my eyes work. Not chaos. Not just many men. Rules. Owners walk slowly, hands mostly empty, as if weight itself is beneath them. Owned men carry crates, poles, water skins, bundles of net, iron hooks wrapped in hide. If an owner turns his head, an owned man moves before the order is finished. If an owned man slows, a lash touches the air near his back. Not always the skin. Often near it.

That is worse in its way. A blow teaches pain. The near blow teaches the body to carry pain before it arrives.

I know that lesson.

There are more men here than Vorth on the high stones. Two score at least, perhaps three when I count the ones moving in and out of the boats. Some wear leather marked with the eye-and-thorn. Some have no mark but carry themselves like they belong to it. Some are collared. Some not collared but owned all the same, by posture, by hunger, by the way they look at food before looking at the man who gives permission to eat it.

Cages stand near the rear fires.

Not finished cages. Working cages. The kind men can take apart and carry over water, then build again wherever the world offers something worth stealing. In one, a Mure lies web-fingered and dull-eyed, its wet skin drying wrong in the open air. In another, a Skell breathes through broken ribs. Its claws scrape the wood in a rhythm that means pain, pain, pain, though no human head turns toward it.

The cages are clever.

That makes me hate them more.

Each corner comes apart with pins. Each bar has leather wrapped where hands must lift. Hooks fold flat for carrying, then bite into the ground when the cage is set down. Men have thought carefully about how to make a prison travel.

The whole camp is built the same way.

Not settled. Ready.

The tents can come down fast. The racks fold. The smoke pots hang from chains that can be lifted and carried still hot. Spears lie in bundles of five, points wrapped in greased cloth. Nets are dried on slanted frames so rot will not take them before the next shore. Even the fire pits have clay plates under the ash, as if men have learned to bring a little ground with them and leave less trace for rain to find.

At the edge of the clearing, an older owned man sits with a slate across his knees. A young owner stands over him and speaks numbers. The older man's fingers move charcoal over the slate, neat, fast, practiced.

Mure. One.

Skell. One.

Hides. Six bundles.

Teeth. Three strings.

Dead. The owner pauses before that count, then gives it with irritation, as if death is a leaky barrel. The older man marks it down.

Two men carry a body past the slate while he writes. The body is wrapped in sailcloth, feet bare and gray where they show. No one sings. No one touches forehead to forehead. The owner only looks at the slate and asks another question.

Cost.

The word has not come to me yet.

I know the shape.

The body becomes a mark in charcoal. The mark becomes part of whether the camp stays or goes. A life has been changed into a number that can argue with profit.

Men do not only cage bodies.

They cage meaning until it stops crying out.

I touch the scar at the back of my neck and remember a different cage, built of planks, rope, stink, and dark. The shape changes. The lesson does not.

Men are caged too.

That makes something in me go colder than fear.

The men inside the bars have the same hands as the men outside. Same mouths. Same eyes that understand what iron means. The difference is not shape. It is only who holds the key.

One collared boy carries water from the boil rack to the marked tents.

He is near my size.

That is what hooks me.

Not pity first. Recognition. His wrists are too thin for the bucket weight. His hair has been cut unevenly close to the skull, and the back of his neck is rubbed raw under the iron ring. When a guard speaks, the boy lowers his head before the words are done. The movement is so familiar that my own neck answers.

For one breath the trees between us vanish.

I could be there.

He could be me.

The difference is only that the sea broke one cage before it drowned me and this cage still knows its key.

He stumbles near the cook fire. Water slops over his bare feet. A guard lifts a hook, not to tear, only to remind. The boy flinches so hard the bucket almost falls. Before the hook lands, a rower with a gray beard steps close and catches the bucket's rim with two fingers.

Small help.

Dangerous help.

The guard sees. The hook lowers. The gray-bearded rower looks at the ground. The boy looks at the ground. Both stand very still while the guard decides how much order costs today.

At last the guard spits and walks on.

The boy breathes.

So do I.

Then he does a foolish, human, impossible act.

He smiles at the gray-bearded rower.

Only for half a heartbeat. Only one corner of the mouth. It is gone before anyone with power can punish it.

It still reaches me.

There. Not all men are the hand on the neck. Not all human mouths laugh at cages. Some catch a bucket. Some share one hidden breath in the middle of owning.

The wanting opens again, worse because it has kindness in it.

I hate the owners.

I want them to see me.

Both truths sit in the same skin, not fighting, which makes them worse.

Near the center of camp, men raise a pole and hang a strip of black cloth from it. The eye-and-thorn is stitched there in pale thread. The symbol lifts in the sea wind, opens, closes, opens again.

My hand finds the disc under my shirt.

The dead men carried that mark. The seer bowed to it. Now it flies above cages and orders.

The answer is here. I can feel it the way I feel rain before the leaves turn.

The captain comes into the cage yard near dusk.

The camp changes around him.

Not loudly. No call, no horn, no scrambling panic. A quieter change. Men straighten. Conversations shorten. Owners who were careless with their hands become careful. The owned go still in the middle of their work and then move faster. Even the caged Skell stops scraping for a breath, animal enough to read what men pretend is invisible.

He is not the largest man in camp. The largest carries an axe and stands near the boats with arms thick as young trees. The captain is lean, dark-haired, his beard cut short against a jaw too still for kindness. A scar cuts through one brow and misses the eye by a finger. He wears the eye-and-thorn on his chest, worked into boiled leather. The same mark hangs at his belt on a metal seal. Another is stamped onto the pommel of his knife.

Some men wear symbols.

This one uses them like teeth.

He stops before the Mure cage. He does not prod it. Does not laugh. That would be easier to hate. He crouches and studies the creature with the patience of a good hunter. Its gills flutter dry. Its webbed hands clutch at the cage floor.

The captain says something.

A smaller man answers too quickly and is slapped without the captain looking at him. Not hard. Hard enough. The answer comes again, corrected.

The captain listens with his eyes on the Mure. When the smaller man finishes, the captain lifts one finger.

Not enough.

The smaller man swallows, looks toward the water, toward the shade, toward Yara where she stands beside a stack of hides. He calls her over.

She comes without hurry. That is brave. Around men like him, hurry gives them a handle.

The captain speaks again. Yara listens to the smaller man's words, then crouches by the cage and makes the wet throat-click I heard from the brown channels. The Mure's black eyes open wider.

The camp falls a little quiet.

Yara asks another sound. The Mure answers with a thin whistle that makes the hairs rise along my arms.

She turns the whistle into human speech.

The captain smiles.

Not because he is pleased with her. Because a locked box has made a noise and he has found the key.

Power, I am learning, does not always need strength. Sometimes it is the shape of the space everyone leaves around one man.

The captain points to the Mure's throat, then to a slate in another man's hand. The man writes. Points to the drying skin. More writing. Points toward the water. An order follows. Two owned men lift the cage and carry it under shade.

He is not only taking.

He is studying what he takes.

That makes him more dangerous.

Before full dark, the camp becomes a market without buyers.

At least, that is how it looks first.

Men bring what the day has stolen from the island and lay it in lines near the black banner. Hesh teeth not from my Hesh, smaller and yellow with age. Skell claws cut from dead hands. Bundles of blue reed that make fever smoke when burned. A netted sack of shellfish clicking against one another. Three jars of dark mud sealed with wax and marked in pale chalk. The older owned man writes each line while the young owner with him names value in a bored voice.

The bored voice is a lie.

I see his eyes linger on the jars.

So does the captain.

He asks for one to be opened. The young owner hesitates because every sealed jar is a promise to someone far away, and promises seem to matter when coin hangs from them. The captain waits. That is all. The young owner breaks the wax.

The smell reaches even my blind.

Sharp. Mineral. Blood under rain.

The captain dips a twig into the mud and touches it to a scrap of pale hide. The hide blackens where the mud sits, not burned, changed. Men lean closer. Even two collared rowers stare until a guard cuffs one back to work.

The captain smiles the way he smiled at the Skell cage.

Not joy.

Use.

The island is not only meat to him. Not only slaves or hides or monsters dragged screaming into cages. It is medicine, poison, dye, story, weapon, charm. Every root and claw and fever has a price somewhere beyond the water. Every price is a road bringing more boats.

I had thought the camp came to steal bodies.

It came to read the island in the language of sale.

That thought makes the high stones feel suddenly smaller behind me. A king can guard a path, a spring, a sleeping hollow. How does a king guard against hunger that lives across the sea and sends men with slates to count the world apart?

The young owner reaches for the opened jar after the captain turns away.

A woman catches his wrist.

She is broad through the shoulders and gray at the temples, with a burn scar roping one side of her neck. Not collared. Not marked with the eye-and-thorn. Her belt carries needles, glass vials, and a short curved blade kept cleaner than any spear in camp. I have seen her dress wounds, cut rot from a man's foot, pour bitter drops into a caged mouth. The owners speak to her with impatience and caution both.

She says something to the young owner.

He laughs.

She presses his hand toward the open jar until one fingertip hovers above the dark mud.

The laugh dies.

She lets him go.

He steps back with anger on his face and fear in his throat.

The woman seals the jar with fresh wax, then marks the top with a symbol of her own. Not eye-and-thorn. Three short cuts crossing a circle. A warning, maybe. Or a claim.

Another power in camp.

Not soft. Not kind. Necessary.

The captain sees the mark when he returns. For a breath they look at each other over the jar. The camp bends around their silence. He could overrule her. She could refuse him in some way that would cost more than pride. Neither chooses the open fight.

He nods once.

She nods back, smaller.

I store her away in my mind beside the captain, Yara, the slate man, the boy with the raw collar. The camp is not one mouth. It is many mouths learning to chew in rhythm.

That is worse.

He walks the camp after that, and the camp becomes a map of his wanting.

At the net racks he tests knots with one hand and rejects three without raising his voice. The net-master's face goes pale. At the boil rack he smells the water, listens to a report, and sends one barrel to the collared men instead of the marked tent waiting for it. The owner beside him protests.

The captain turns his head.

The protest dies.

At the slate table he reads the charcoal marks. His finger stops on the line where the wrapped body became a number. He asks a question. The older owned man answers with his eyes lowered. The captain listens, then takes the charcoal himself and changes one mark.

Not softer.

More exact.

He has the patience to care whether cruelty is counted correctly.

That makes my skin crawl.

A stupid brutal man can be tricked by his own hunger. This one trims hunger into tools.

He asks after rope. After medicine. After the cages. After the waterline. After the paths cut into the green. Every answer becomes movement. Two men sent to gather bitter leaves. Three to raise the rack higher. One guard replaced because he keeps slapping at insects instead of watching the trees.

Then he stops beside the collared boy with the raw neck.

The boy freezes with both hands around an empty bucket.

The captain crouches, takes the bucket, and looks at the rubbed skin under the collar. He says something to a woman with needles at her belt. She brings cloth and grease. The captain points. She wraps the wound while the boy stares at nothing.

Kindness, the ache in me whispers.

No.

Maintenance.

Both may use the same hand.

The boy will carry more water if the collar does not eat him too quickly. The captain knows it. The boy knows it. I know it, and still the sight of human fingers tending human pain goes into me where I hate to be touched.

At the Skell cage he bends close enough to lose fingers if the creature had strength left. It snaps anyway. He smiles then, and the smile has no heat in it.

One day, I think, he will look at me that way.

The thought should send me back to the high stones.

It keeps me where I am.

Then I see her.

She is not owner and not fully owned.

At first I read only the wrongness of her position. She moves among owners without belonging to them. She carries bundles, but no chain links her to the collared men. She eats from a wooden bowl near the marked tents, not with the caged and not at the captain's fire. When an owner speaks to a prisoner, she steps forward and gives the meaning back in another tongue. When the caged Mure clicks wetly through its drying throat, she listens, flinches, and turns the sound into human words.

She carries meaning across.

My breath catches.

No Vorth can do that. Kala can hold me. Marrow can read me better than any living creature. The seer can touch blood and mark and hear old echoes. But this girl stands between two roads of speech and moves sense from one to the other.

She has the bridge I lost.

She is near my age, maybe, though hunger and labor make age a poor map. Her hair is bound back in wet coils. Rope marks scar one shoulder. A bruise darkens the side of her face, old enough to yellow at the edge. She keeps her eyes lowered around the captain, but when his back turns, the eyes lift and count everything.

Not broken.

Careful.

There is a difference.

The captain calls her with two fingers.

She goes at once.

He speaks without looking down. She listens. The stillness in her face is practiced, but her hand betrays her, closing once in the cloth at her side. Then he gestures toward the cages and the green beyond them. She nods.

The words are closed to me.

I know the shape.

He is asking about what the island says.

About what has been seen.

About us.

Yara answers carefully. The words are closed, but a map is plain when a hand makes one. Her fingers sketch shore, mud, high stone. She leaves spaces. Twice the captain's gaze sharpens at a place she does not fill. Twice she fills it with something else before he can set his finger there.

She is lying for someone.

Maybe herself. Maybe the caged. Maybe the monster in the trees she only half believes in.

The captain knows she is trimming the truth. He lets her.

That is worse than catching her. A patient hunter lets the trail run until it leads home.

My mouth goes dry.

The captain turns his head toward the trees.

Again, not directly to where I lie. A little left. A little high. Reading cover, reading distance, reading where eyes might hide. I sink until mud touches my lip. The insects crawl over my neck. I stay still.

The girl looks too.

For one impossible breath I think her eyes find me.

They pass on.

Near dark, the camp eats.

Owners first. Then the uncollared workers. Then the collared. The caged get bowls shoved between bars, thin broth and scraps not fit for dogs. The Mure does not understand the bowl until the girl crouches and clicks softly in its tongue. It lifts its head, weak and furious, and drinks.

The captain watches her do it.

Not kindly. Never that. With calculation.

He knows what she is worth.

So do I.

Low tide pulls the mud flats silver under the moon. The camp settles into watches. Fires burn low. Fever-swarms gather around the men and sing in black knots above the lamps. One owner slaps at his neck and curses. Another laughs at him.

The island is patient.

The girl slips away when the watch changes.

She carries a water skin and a small knife, both openly enough that no one stops her. I follow from above as she walks down the spit to the edge of the mangroves. She stops where dry mud darkens to wet, just short of the first roots.

Her shoulders are raised around her ears.

The posture of a body waiting for a blow.

I name her in my head because I will not call her owned.

Yara.

Maybe it is not her true name. Maybe it is only the sound I make for a bridge still beyond me. But it is better than girl. Better than owned. Better than translator, which is a tool's name.

Yara looks into the dark.

When she speaks, the voice is not command. Not the closing sound. It is soft and careful, a hand set palm-up on the ground.

I know none of the words.

I know the offering.

She points to the shell near her foot and says a sound. Waits. Points again. Says it again. Then the tide. Then the mud. Then herself, hand pressed to her chest.

The sound she gives for herself is not Yara.

I keep my name for her anyway.

I move one hanging frond.

She goes still.

Not like prey. Like someone whose guess has become a door.

Her eyes find the frond. Then the root shadow above it. Then the deeper dark where I hold my breath. She should run. She should call the watch. She should be afraid of the monster in the trees because that is the sensible shape of the moment.

Instead she points to the shell again and repeats the word.

Teaching.

The ache in me opens so hard I nearly answer with the only word that matters.

My name.

I bite it back.

She keeps going. Shell. Tide. Mud. Water. Fire. Camp. Captain.

The last word is different. Harder. Her mouth tightens around it.

Captain.

Then she points back toward the camp and closes her fist slowly.

Hand.

No. More than hand.

Ownership.

Warning.

Both.

She looks at the closed fist for a moment too long.

Then, with two fingers, she opens it again.

Not free. Not safe. Only possible.

The gesture is so small a watchman would see only a girl flexing a cramped hand. I see a door crack in darkness.

The road between us is only a few stones long, but it is a road.

I take every sound she gives and hide it behind my teeth.

A branch cracks far behind me.

Yara's face changes at once. The soft teaching vanishes. The still mask drops over her again. She lifts the water skin as if she came only to fill it from a bad pool no one else wanted, then turns back toward camp.

I wait until she is gone before moving.

Marrow is up the slope exactly where I left him.

No. Not where I left him. Closer. He came down after me and stopped where his courage and his hatred of the camp met and could go no farther. His eye moves from my face to the place where Yara stood, then toward the camp fires.

He does not need words.

Your shape is not your kin.

I know.

I cannot tell.

Both are true, and neither helps.

We climb back through the mangroves in silence. The high stones are dark by the time we reach them. Kala sleeps near the young ones, but her head lifts before my foot touches the shelf. She smells the camp on me again. Smoke. Iron. Human hunger.

This time she does not reach for me at once.

She looks first at Marrow.

He goes to her and presses his head against her arm like a child, though he is no child by Vorth counting. Her hand closes over his high shoulder. Only then does she look at me.

I have stood before Hesh with blood on my hands. Before Gull-Marr with the tribe watching. Before a snake large enough to swallow a boy.

None of that made me feel as small as Kala's silence.

I go to my place near the fire and lie down with Yara's words warm in my mouth, the captain's mark burning against my chest, and Marrow's warning laid beside both.

Above us, smoke from the slavers' camp stains the stars.

# Chapter 21: The One Who Sees

Yara comes back.

Not the next low tide. That would be too easy, and nothing around the camp is easy. The next low she stays near the fires, carrying bowls between owners and cage yard while I watch from the mangroves until my legs cramp and Marrow's patience goes sour beside me.

The low after that, she comes.

She walks openly, water skin over one shoulder, knife at her belt, eyes lowered until the last watcher turns away. Then she slips down the spit to the wet line where roots begin and crouches as if checking a pool.

Her first word is shell.

She says it twice. Touches the shell. Sets it down.

Then mud. Tide. Root. Water. Fire.

I give no answer.

The first rule of the camp is that sound carries iron behind it. A voice from the green would bring watchers, nets, hooks, and men with orders to burn what they cannot cage. So I lie in shadow and take her words like stolen food.

Yara seems to understand the bargain. She never asks for my voice at first. She builds the road one stone at a time and pretends not to see the shape hiding at the other end.

Camp.

Cage.

Pain.

Captain.

That word she lays down like a blade.

Sometimes she gives me a word and then takes it back.

Not aloud. Not where any watcher can follow. With her hands.

She points to a guard and says the camp word for guard. Then her fingers move low against the mud and make another sign: dog. No. Not dog. Leashed tooth, maybe. A mouth that bites where a hand points. She points to the older owned man with the slate and says one word. Her fingers give another: memory that can be bought. She points to the black banner and gives the formal word with a face as empty as a bowl.

Then she spits into the mud when no one can see.

That teaches me more than the word.

Human speech has layers. A sound for what power says. A sign for what fear means. A silence for what no one can afford.

The Vorth have that too, but theirs lives in weight and posture, in who eats first, in how far a body sits from flame. Human lies can wear cleaner clothes. They can stand upright and call themselves order.

Yara teaches me both, though I cannot tell whether she means to.

Once she points toward herself and says the camp sound the captain uses for her.

Her mouth hates it.

Her fingers press to her chest afterward and give another sound, so small the rain almost eats it.

Her own.

I store both and understand that a name can be a collar if someone else fastens it.

On the fourth lesson, I give her something back.

Not my voice.

A stone.

She has no word for the pale markers I use along the rot paths, or she does not think to give it. When she says root and points at the path behind me, I push one pale stone into the open with the tip of a reed. She goes still, then looks at the ground around it. She reads the warning before I know how to teach it.

Soft.

Sink.

Bad ground.

Her mouth parts. Not fear. Understanding.

She says a word over the stone, then a different word over the mud beyond it. I take both.

The road is not only hers now.

I learn quickly because hunger teaches. A starving mouth remembers the path to meat. A starving mind remembers the path to meaning. Soon I know when she points to water and when she warns me from it. I know the difference between fire for warmth and fire as threat. I know the word for rope. For net. For iron. For owned.

That last one makes her look away.

Marrow hates every lesson.

He waits above the line with his shoulder low and his crooked hand tight against his chest. Some evenings he brings pieces of home and sets them near my elbow: a river snail, a strip of roasted meat wrapped in broad leaf, a shard of bright shell from the high stones, once a little twist of dried moss from Kala's sleeping place. He does it without drama. That is worse than complaint.

Remember, each gift says.

I eat the meat.

I keep watching Yara.

She has rules of her own. Never the same path twice. Never more than a handful of words when the watch is fresh. Never fear on her face where the camp can read it. Around the owners she lowers her eyes. Around the caged she softens her voice. Around the captain she becomes so still that stillness itself looks obedient.

But at the mud line, when the dark hides her mouth from camp, she changes.

Not free. No. Freedom is too large a word for someone who must measure every breath against the nearest lash. But more herself. The eyes lift. The voice loses its flatness. Sometimes anger breaks through before she catches it. Sometimes pity. Once, when the caged Skell screams behind her and an owner laughs, she closes both hands in the mud until the fingers sink.

I understand that better than words.

I also see what happens after.

That is the part I try not to see.

When Yara leaves the mud line, she does not return straight to the cage yard. She circles by the water rack, stops at the slate table, says two words to the older owned man, then carries a bowl to the captain's black awning. The first time I think it is only work. Everyone in camp carries something to someone above them. That is how the camp breathes.

The second time, the captain asks her questions.

I am high in a leaning mangrove, wet bark pressed to my belly. I cannot hear every word. I hear enough rhythm to know he is not asking for water or cloth. His hand draws the line of the shore in the air. Then the mangroves. Then the high green. Yara's face stays empty.

She answers.

She lies.

I know because she points to a false rise, one that floods at high tide and could hide nothing heavier than crabs. The captain watches her finger. He says something. She answers again. Her hand moves closer to the true path, not on it, not away. A hunter's almost.

My stomach knots.

She is protecting me.

She is measuring me.

She is protecting herself by giving enough truth to keep the captain from hearing the whole lie.

All three can live in one mouth.

The captain lets her finish. Then he takes the bowl from her and drinks as if they have discussed only weather. When she turns away, his eyes go to the trees.

Not the false rise.

Higher.

Nearer.

He has learned from the shape of her lie.

Yara knows it too. I see it in the tightness of her shoulders as she walks back to the cages. She does not look at my hiding place. That mercy is almost cruel.

At the mud line that night, she teaches me the word for trap.

Then she teaches it again.

Then she points to herself.

Trap.

No. Not only that.

Used.

I cannot tell which meaning she wants me to keep.

The camp sickens by small degrees.

A man who laughed at swarms on the first day now scratches his neck until blood runs. Two owned men take turns vomiting behind the water barrels. A guard limps on a foot gone red around a thorn cut. The captain has ordered shade built over the Mure, boiled water for the marked men, smoke pots for the insects, dry sleeping racks above the mud.

He reads faster than the rest.

Not fast enough.

The island is already inside them.

One evening Yara brings a new word and does not point to anything.

She touches her chest.

Says the sound she uses for herself.

Then she points toward the place where I hide.

Waits.

My name rises so hard it hurts.

My name.

The one possession no hand took because no hand found it. My mother's gift, held behind broken teeth through hold, shore, green, fever, blood, crown.

Yara waits with the patience of someone offering, not taking.

Marrow's hand closes on my ankle from the shadow behind me.

Not hard.

Enough.

I say nothing.

Yara lowers her hand. The disappointment is small and quick, gone almost before I see it. She nods as if silence is also an answer, then points to me again and gives a different word.

Hidden.

Maybe.

Or ghost.

Or beast.

Or the one who sees.

I keep it anyway because she gave it to the dark without chains on it.

Then she points at herself and gives her own sound again.

Not Yara.

The true sound, maybe, or the camp sound, or one she can afford to spend.

I cannot give mine. The refusal tastes like old blood.

So I push another pale stone from the dark and set it beside the shell.

Path marker. Warning. Gift.

She looks at it, then at the shadow where I lie. Her face softens in a way that hurts more than disappointment.

She understands enough.

The next lesson, she brings the stone back.

For one cold breath I think it is rejection. She sets it where I can see it, then places three sticks beside it. One stick points to the camp. One to the mud line. One to the high green. She moves the stone along the wrong stick first and shakes her head. Then the camp stick and shakes her head harder. Then the high green. Her hand hovers there.

Home.

Her word for it is not the Vorth sound. It does not carry Kala's chest warmth or Marrow's shoulder at my knee. It is smaller, human, made of roof and fire and remembered doorways. Still, when she says it over the stick that leads uphill, the word cuts.

She knows I have somewhere to go.

That should comfort me.

Instead it makes me afraid.

Because if she knows, the captain may know soon.

Yara sees the fear. Her fingers move quickly.

Hide home.

Hide self.

Hide mark.

Then she touches the bruise on her cheek, old yellow now under new purple, and gives a small broken smile.

Bad at hiding, that smile says.

It is the first almost-joke she gives me.

It startles a breath from my nose.

Not laughter. Close enough that Marrow, from the roots behind me, goes rigid with betrayal.

Yara hears the breath. Her eyes widen. For a heartbeat the mud line holds a warmth so thin it would tear under a finger.

Then a watchman coughs behind her, and the warmth is gone.

She leaves the stone.

After that, the lessons change.

She teaches verbs. Come. Go. Hide. Eat. Hurt. See. Know. The world grows joints. Before, I had stones. Now the stones begin to move. I can understand pieces when the camp shouts. Not enough to be safe. Enough to know danger sooner.

One owner tells another the green is cursed.

One collared man whispers that the captain will not leave until he has what he came for.

One guard calls the Mure worthless, and the captain has him struck for damaging cargo with bad water.

Cargo.

That word I understand before Yara teaches it.

It is a word men use when they want ownership to sound clean.

Rain comes early on the seventh lesson.

The camp blurs behind gray curtains. Watches hunch under hides. Smoke runs low. Yara comes anyway, hair slick against her face, bowl in hand as her excuse. She crouches closer than before because rain covers small sounds.

Her cheek is bruised fresh.

I move before I mean to.

Only a handspan. Enough for a leaf to shift wrong. Enough for her eyes to snap to me, not afraid, sharp with warning. Stay hidden.

I stop.

She touches the bruise, then points toward the captain's tent, then shakes her head once as if forbidding my anger before I can spend it.

I hate that she is right.

I hate more that she knows I am angry.

She speaks quickly then. Words I half know, half guess.

Captain.

Looking.

Mark.

Green.

I reach for the disc under my shirt without thinking.

The cord catches on wet cloth. The eye-and-thorn slips free.

Yara sees it.

Everything changes.

Her breath catches. The still mask vanishes. For one heartbeat she is only a girl in rain staring at a ghost from a story she hoped was not true. Her hand rises, not toward me, toward the mark. She draws it in the air: the open eye, the thorn crossing through.

Then she names it.

The word is low, old-sounding, and careful.

The word is closed to me.

I know the weight she puts on it.

She knows the mark.

The need in me surges so violently I nearly crawl into the open. I want to seize the word from her mouth and crack it apart. Ask why dead men carried it. Ask why the captain burns it onto cages. Ask why the seer bowed to it on my chest. Ask whether it is a name for me or a price or a curse or a key.

But I have no safe question.

And Yara is already afraid.

Not of me.

For me.

She wipes the mark she drew in the mud. Too late. Rain helps, but not enough. Her eyes dart once toward camp, then back to me.

She points at the disc.

Then the camp.

Then closes her fist slowly.

The hand is coming.

I understand.

She adds another word before Marrow pulls me back.

Not captain.

A name.

Two sounds. Hard first, soft second. The sound men make when they need the captain's attention and fear getting it.

Drevan.

Then another.

Sool.

She says them like a door she should not open.

The name enters me with the mark's old word still ringing behind it. A named danger is worse than an unnamed one. It can be followed. Remembered. Hated in a cleaner shape.

Yara points toward the awning.

Drevan Sool.

Then the mark.

Then me.

The hand is coming, yes.

Now it has a name.

Marrow pulls at my ankle. This time hard.

I slide back into the roots, heart hammering.

The next day Yara does not come.

I watch the camp from a higher blind and learn what absence means. She stays near the owner fire, not by choice. The captain speaks to her twice. Once he lifts his hand and does not strike.

That is worse than striking.

A blow spends itself. A held blow owns the air.

Near dusk, he walks to the wet line himself.

Not alone. Men with hooks keep distance behind him, trying to look bored. The captain carries no net. No bow. Only a walking staff cut from a branch the camp stripped smooth. He plants it in the mud where Yara crouches for lessons and studies the roots above it.

I am not there. I am higher, where wind takes my scent.

Still, my skin tightens.

He looks at the pale stone I left beside the shell. He does not touch it at first. He circles it. Reads the mud. Reads the roots. Reads the height a watching body would need. Then he picks it up with two fingers and turns it once in his palm.

He smiles.

Small.

Certain.

The stone was a gift. In his hand it becomes a measure.

She has told him.

Or he has read her.

Or someone saw rain move where rain should not move.

It does not matter. The camp knows something in the green wears the mark.

When Yara comes again, a watcher follows at a distance.

She knows.

Her body is tight with it, but she comes anyway. She crouches at my line and speaks ordinary words loudly enough for the watcher.

Shell.

Rain.

Tide.

Root.

Her low hand draws where only the mangrove shadow can see.

Eye.

Thorn.

Broken apart.

Then she points back toward camp and closes her fist.

The hand is coming.

Again.

This time she adds another sign. Two fingers walking. Then many fingers closing around them.

Not just one hand.

Men.

Nets.

Soon.

Yara looks straight into the place where I hide. Not at a spirit. Not at the island. At me.

She knows I am a person.

The knowledge lands gentler than Kala's hand and more dangerous than the captain's mark.

The watcher calls.

Yara rises at once. The mask returns. She walks back to camp without looking over her shoulder.

I lie in the roots after she is gone, holding too much behind my teeth.

My hidden name.

The brand-sound from the hold.

The road of words Yara is laying stone by stone.

The mark's old name, closed to me.

Her warning.

The seer saw the mark and bowed. The captain sees it and reaches. Both recognize value. Neither gives me an answer I can hold.

That night I return late to the high stones.

Kala is waiting. So is Marrow. The seer stands near the dead shelf with both hands on his staff, his blind eye turned toward the coast as if listening to a storm still beyond the horizon.

Kala smells the camp smoke on me.

She smells Yara too, maybe. Human fear. Rain. Rope. The strange new road opening under my skin.

This time she holds me, but not as before. Her arm comes around me with care, and the care has a question inside it.

Where do you go?

Marrow crouches on my other side, angry enough not to touch me.

I have words now. Shell. Fire. Cage. Captain. Pain. Hand. Hidden.

Not enough for this.

So I say nothing.

For the first time since fever brought me into her arms, there is distance inside Kala's embrace.

# Chapter 22: The Sigil

The captain stops searching the dark.

He starts feeding it.

The first gift appears just before dusk.

Two owned men carry it down to the mud line while an owner watches from the rise behind them. Cooked meat rests on a strip of bark. Bright water fills a gourd. A twist of salt sits beside both, white and impossible, richer than bone.

They set everything where Yara usually crouches.

Then they leave.

No watcher stays in the open.

That makes it worse.

Bait does not always need teeth showing.

I know bait. A noose under leaves. A crying animal tied near a thicket. Easy meat on ground too clean. The lesson is simple: when the world gives freely, it means to close around the taking hand.

I lie in the roots until full dark.

The meat cools. The smell does not. Smoke, fat, salt, human fire. My belly pulls toward it. That hunger I understand and can master. Hunger is honest. It wants, and if denied it curses, weakens, waits.

This is not only belly-hunger.

Marrow knows it too.

He will not look at the food. He stands uphill with his body turned toward home, high shoulder sharp in the dark, and waits for me to follow. When I stay where I am, he comes down, picks up a stone, and throws it into the mud beside the bark.

Not at the food.

Near it.

Enough to say what he thinks of gifts left by men.

I almost smile.

Almost.

There is a smallness in his anger that makes it sharper. He cannot forbid me as Kala can. He cannot shame me as the seer can. He cannot stand before the tribe and make the old males listen. He has only a stone, a crooked hand, and the certainty that I am being foolish.

He spends all three.

That should be enough to turn me back.

Instead I watch the place where the meat waits, and hate how badly I want the world to mean gift when it has always meant trap.

The next dusk, more comes.

This time the owned men bring roasted fish, a little clay cup of sweet pulp, and water clear enough to see the moon in before night has fully risen. The owner behind them does not watch the jungle. He watches the ground as if that proves he is not watching.

I leave it.

The third dusk, Yara comes with them.

Her face is empty in the camp way, but her hand gives warning low against her thigh.

No.

Then another sign.

Drug.

I know the second from the caged Mure, from the slack-eyed Skell, from the bitter smell around the bowls given to creatures men want quiet but not dead.

The owner speaks. Yara repeats his words louder toward the trees.

I know enough now to catch pieces.

Gift.

Friend.

Marked one.

No harm.

Friend is the ugliest word in the sentence.

They leave the food and take Yara back with them. She does not look over her shoulder. She does not need to. Her warning remains in the mud where her fingers drew it and then smoothed it away.

Marrow presses a strip of dried Vorth meat into my hand that night when we return to the high stones. His own portion, I think. He gives it angrily, as if kindness disgusts him but not as much as letting me hunger after camp scraps.

I take it.

That should be enough.

It is not.

The captain has read me.

Not my paths through the green. Not yet. Not my blood. Not fully. He has read the emptiness I thought I had hidden even from myself. Force will not draw me out. Nets thrown blind will lose men and teach me caution. Fire would drive the island against him as much as me.

So he makes the trap look like welcome.

That is sharper than iron.

He is not only reading me.

He is reading his own camp.

The sickness has made the owners quarrel. I hear it in the night, voices cutting through canvas, a man with rank speaking too loudly because he feels rank slipping under his feet. The cages are not full enough. The island takes men and gives back fever, broken hooks, animals that bite through bars, and rumors of a marked wild boy who eats poison and vanishes.

Drevan cannot show doubt to them.

So he makes patience look like command. He feeds the dark in front of every watcher. He turns failure into a plan the others can see.

That is a kind of strength.

I hate him more for having it.

One afternoon I watch him spend that strength on his own men.

The camp is wet with heat. Rain has fallen and gone, leaving the canvas roofs sagging and the paths between them churned into brown paste. Men move slowly. Even the owners walk as if the air has weight. Two collared rowers carry a barrel of boiled water between them, and every free man they pass looks at the barrel with the same thirst and contempt.

A young owner knocks the barrel with his hip.

Not hard. Hard enough.

Water spills into the mud.

The rower on the left drops to one knee and tries to cup it back with both hands before sense catches him. The owner laughs because the shape of power is sometimes no larger than making another person reach for what cannot be saved.

Drevan sees.

The camp sees Drevan seeing.

For three breaths no one moves. Then the captain walks down from his black awning. The fever has put a shine on him by then. His scar is red at the edges, his left hand trembles once before he closes it around the spear shaft. Still, the camp parts for him as if his sickness has only made him more dangerous.

He asks one question.

I miss most of the words. I know tone. I know the owner answers badly because he is frightened and ashamed of being frightened before collared men.

Drevan strikes him across the mouth with the butt of the spear.

The sound is flat and ugly. Not a fight. Not rage. Order.

The camp holds its breath.

Drevan points to the mud, then to the barrel, then to the cook fires. The young owner goes pale. He drops to his knees beside the collared rower and begins scraping water from the mud into a cracked bowl, knowing every man sees him do it. Knowing the collared men see it too.

Mercy, some might call it.

Justice, if they were hungry for clean names.

I watch Drevan's face and see neither. He has lost water. He has lost order. He chooses the cheaper loss to mend the dearer one. Shame one owner in public, and the collared men carry barrels tomorrow without flinching. Let the owner laugh, and water becomes another war inside the camp.

The rower does not smile.

He is too wise for that.

He keeps his eyes on the mud while a free man learns humiliation beside him, and the whole camp remembers who allowed the lesson.

This is how sickness makes new ladders. The strong are still above. The weak are still beneath. But fever saws at the rungs, and Drevan spends each break before anyone else can climb through it.

Yara stands near the cage line with a bundle of wet cloth in her arms. She watches too. Her face gives nothing to the men around her, but when Drevan turns away she looks at the rower, then at the shamed owner, then at the trees where I hide.

Her eyes say what her hands cannot.

See him whole.

Not good.

Not simple.

Whole.

Later, when the camp thins toward sleep, I see why she wants me to see it.

Another man has power there. Not captain. Not marked. A narrow man with a shaved head and a ledger board wrapped in oiled cloth. He moves through the camp counting what Drevan spends: water, rope, dried grain, hooks, men still strong enough to row. The owners make space for him though he wears no fine coat. Even Drevan listens when the narrow man speaks.

Quartermaster, Yara called him once, shaping the word slowly because I had no place to put it.

The quartermaster wants the boats gone.

I understand that without knowing all his words. He points to the sick tents. To the low water barrels. To the cages with more empty space than cargo. His finger cuts toward the sea again and again. Leave with what we have. Sell the Skell. Sell the Mure. Sell the marked boy if he comes easy, but do not let a story eat the ship before coin can be made from it.

Drevan wants the mark.

The quartermaster wants survival.

The owners want proof that their dead were not spent on a rumor.

The collared men want food, water, and one more dawn.

Yara wants me warned. Yara wants Drevan satisfied enough that he does not put a hook through her warnings. Yara wants a road out of the camp and knows every road here is made from someone else's ribs.

No one wants one clean answer.

That is what makes the camp more frightening than a Hesh. A Hesh hungers in one direction. Men hunger in many, and call the crossing of hungers order.

On the fifth dusk, I go down.

Marrow follows until the last root and then blocks my path. Teeth bared. Curled hand shaking. Small body set against mine as if he can hold back every boat on the water by standing there.

"Stay," I whisper in Vorth.

His face changes when I use Kala's word.

I hate myself for that.

I step around him anyway.

The offering waits on the bark. Meat, water, salt. No Yara. No visible watcher. A better trap than the first because it lets me pretend no one sees.

I crouch over the food.

Not eating. Reading.

Knife cuts on bark. Smoke from their fire. Salt rubbed into the meat. Fat cooled white at the edge. The water smells clean at first, then bitter underneath. A sleeping draught, or something like it. Enough to slow limbs, blur fear, make rope easier.

Drugged.

I should leave it.

Instead I eat.

The drug comes into me as a soft weight behind the eyes. For three breaths the world blurs at the edges. Then the warmth under my ribs turns, quiet and deep. My blood takes the poison, breaks it, throws it aside as it has thrown aside fever, venom, rot-water, and every sickness the island has tried to plant in me.

My sight clears.

My hands stay steady.

The captain thinks he is making me heavy.

He is wrong about my blood.

He is right about the hunger.

I eat every offering after that.

I tell myself it is strategy. Let him think the drug works slowly. Let him waste patience. Let him feed me while I learn his watches, his paths, his sickness, his number of nets and hooks. Let him believe the marked creature grows tame at the mud line.

Those reasons are true.

They are not the whole truth.

The whole truth is uglier.

I like the shape of welcome.

Warm meat set where I can reach it. Clean water. Salt. Once a strip of dried fruit so sweet it hurts my jaw. No blow. No command. No hand on the neck. No one saying mine.

Only a gift.

Only the shape of welcome.

The hook is there. I smell it. I see the line. Some starving part of me leans anyway.

Each night I return later to the high stones.

The tribe notices.

Voss-Marr notices first because hatred is a kind of attention. He gathers his young strong ones on the far side of the fire and watches me eat with human smoke in my hair. His lip curls when I pass. Once he makes the sign for rotten meat. Another male answers with a low laugh.

I could silence them.

That is the new danger of the lip. Not that I lack power. That I have enough to spend badly.

I let the insult pass.

It does not pass. It gathers.

The discarded ones stay near me. Torn-Ear. Stub. Grist. The scarred female whose name I still cannot shape. They know what it is to be half outside the circle. They do not ask why I go down. Perhaps they do not want to hear an answer that sounds too much like their own old hunger.

The center watches them watching me.

That is how a tribe cracks. Not all at once. A glance withheld. A haunch turned away from the shared fire. A child pulled closer when the human king comes near with camp smoke in his hair. Voss-Marr does not need to strike. He only needs to let every doubt find the right place to stand.

He is cleverer than I wanted him to be.

Kala asks without words.

She waits awake when I climb back from the coast. Her hand comes to my shoulder, my hair, my chest where the mark rests. Then she smells my mouth. Salt. Cooked meat. Camp smoke. Human kindness made poisonous by human intent.

Her eyes darken.

She does not push me away.

That would be easier.

She draws me close and lets the question stay between us.

The seer grows less gentle.

On the ninth dusk he bars my way before I leave. Old bones, clouded eye, staff planted across the trail. He touches the eye-and-thorn on my chest, then points down toward the coast, then drags one finger across his own throat.

Danger.

Death.

No farther.

I know enough Vorth to answer badly.

"Need," I say.

The seer goes very still.

It is one of the first words Kala taught me after hunger and pain. Need can mean food. Water. Shelter. A young one crying for its mother. A wound asking for moss.

It can also mean the wound beneath all those hungers.

The seer touches my chest again. Not the mark this time. The breastbone.

Then he points to the high stones under my feet.

Here.

His meaning is plain.

What you need is here.

I want that to be true.

I go down anyway.

Yara comes less often now. Always watched. Always careful. Her mouth gives the camp's words while her fingers give her own.

Drug.

Do not eat.

Captain waits.

Mark wants you.

Once, when the watcher turns to spit, she signs a word I have not learned and points to me. Then to the disc. Then to the captain's black banner.

Same.

No.

Not same.

The mark on me and the mark on them are linked, but not equal. That is all I can gather. It is enough to keep the question burning.

Once she hesitates before smoothing the mud.

The watcher has turned away. She has half a breath. Enough to give another warning. Enough to give me a word. Instead she looks at the bark, at the meat, at the place where I will crouch after she leaves.

Her face hardens.

She smooths away the mark and says the camp's friendly words more clearly.

I understand then that warning me is not the only work she is doing. She is also keeping herself alive. Perhaps she believes both can be done. Perhaps she has been made to choose so often that choice has become a wound she walks on.

Forgiving her would be easy.

Condemning her would be easier.

Reading the fear in her hand is harder than both.

The captain does not appear at the mud line until the eleventh dusk.

He comes alone.

Not truly alone. Men wait behind him in the cut brush. I hear one shift weight too heavily. Smell another's oiled rope. But the captain walks the last stretch by himself and sets the bark down with his own hand.

Then he steps back.

He speaks into the trees.

His voice is low, calm, almost kind.

I understand only fragments.

Marked one.

Hunger.

Answer.

Come.

Behind the words sits the old shape: welcome, welcome, welcome.

I stay hidden.

The captain smiles as if that too is an answer.

Then he coughs.

He turns his head aside before it can bend him, but the sound comes deep and wet. When he straightens, his hand goes to the scar I gave him. Not from pain alone. From fear.

For the first time, I see the man under the method.

Not softer.

Never that.

Only mortal.

He knows the island is in him. He knows his men are watching. He knows the marked creature in the trees may be watching too. His back stays straight because command is another kind of cage, and he is locked inside it as surely as the Mure drying under shade.

He says another word.

Home.

I know it from Yara. Not fully. Enough.

He points to himself. Then the boats. Then the sea beyond them.

Home.

For one hateful moment I understand the bait inside him too. He has somewhere he means to return. Someone, maybe. A city. A child. A debt. A patron with cleaner hands and sharper teeth. He is not cruelty born from mud. He is a man who has chosen cruelty as a road to something else.

That does not forgive him.

It makes him more dangerous.

Pure monsters are simple.

Men with reasons build ships.

He sets one more object on the bark.

Not food.

A little carved figure of dark wood, no longer than my thumb. A child might hold it. A woman maybe. The face is worn smooth by fingers. He places it beside the meat and speaks again.

Home.

Then he leaves it there and walks back toward the camp without looking behind him.

I leave the figure where it is.

I think about it until dawn.

He touches the eye-and-thorn on his chest, then points to the food, then to the place where he knows I am not quite lying.

The gesture is simple enough for any creature.

We are the same mark.

Come eat from my hand.

When he leaves, Marrow makes a sound behind me that is almost a whimper and almost a curse.

I wait until the camp is quiet to eat.

That is what I tell myself, as if waiting changes the shape of the act.

At dawn I return to the high stones with salt on my tongue and poison broken in my blood.

Kala is not asleep.

The seer is not asleep.

Voss-Marr watches from the far fire, eyes bright with the first clean hope I have seen in him since I took the lip.

The human king is taking human bait.

Let him.

The thought moves through the tribe like wind through grass.

I feel it and cannot deny it.

The captain has found the one poison my blood cannot throw off.

A gift.

# Chapter 23: The Baited Hunger

There are two roads in me now.

The up road leads to the high stones: Kala's arm, Marrow's quick breath, the little ones tangled in sleep, the dead faced to morning, the lip where I sit and the tribe turns when danger starts.

The down road leads to the camp: smoke, iron, Yara's words, Drevan Sool's mark, warm meat left at the edge of the green.

I walk both.

Each day they pull farther apart.

In the morning I hunt with Marrow and the scarred ones. We take river-pig from the shallows and burrow-runners from the soft flats. I judge quarrels over meat. I watch Voss-Marr test the air around my choices, looking for a place to push. I sit beside Kala while she combs burrs from a young one's hair with two careful claws.

By dusk I am listening for human feet.

Marrow walks the down road with me and hates every step.

At first he waits above the wet line. Then he refuses that too. He follows all the way to the roots and crouches beside me while I take the bait. He watches the camp, the trees, my hands, anything but the food. His whole body has become one long warning.

Come up.

Come away.

Not yours.

He is right.

Rightness does not move me.

The offerings change. Fish one night. Swine fat another. A gourd of watered wine that burns my nose and makes the drug beneath it hard to smell. Once, a folded scrap of cloth with the eye-and-thorn stamped in black, set beside the meat like an answer waiting to be unfolded.

I leave the cloth where it lies.

I eat the meat.

Each time the poison fails. Each time I tell myself I have learned something useful: how long the watchers wait, which man shifts too soon in the brush, how Drevan changes bait when the last one does not work, how much he knows of me and how much he guesses.

The worst truth is that I am learning him too.

He is patient, but not calm. The difference matters. Calm has no need under it. Drevan's patience is need held on a tight rein. The island is killing his men. The cages are filling slowly. The mark in the green has become the largest prize because it keeps refusing to become cargo.

That should please me.

It does.

It also draws me.

Need makes men honest in the wrong places.

Drevan starts cutting rations for the collared men first. I see it in the way they watch the cooking pots, not with hunger only, but with the careful blankness of those who know complaint has a price. Then he cuts the owners' wine. That makes them louder. Then he gives one sick owner his own cup and stands beside him until the man drinks.

The camp sees mercy.

I see command.

He spends kindness the way he spends rope. Measured. Public. Tightened around the neck that needs it most.

Yara still comes when she can. Watched now. Measured. Her ordinary words are for the men behind her. Her fingers speak lower in the mud.

The hand is coming.

The mark has called it.

Do not trust the gift.

Sometimes she teaches while warning. Two words hidden inside one meeting. Rope. Fever. Ship. Wait. Sell. Pit.

Pit is the word that stays.

She draws a circle in the mud, then points down.

Men around it.

Beasts inside.

Blood.

I have no word to ask why men would build a hole to watch blood. I understand anyway. The hold was a hole for owning. The pit is a hole for proving ownership to a crowd.

Yara sees me understand and looks away.

The camp is sickening.

I smell it before the men accept it. Fever in the shelters. Bad water in the gut. Rot creeping into cuts. Men who laughed at swarms now sleep under blankets in heat that should cook them. Owners step around owners fallen near the cage line. Collared men carry bodies to the shore at dawn and return without meeting anyone's eyes.

The green is eating them from inside while Drevan sets food out for me.

He is dying slower than he is hunting.

The scar I gave him has gone dark at one edge. Not enough to stop him. Enough to make him touch his face when he thinks no one sees. Enough to put a drag in his step by evening. He hides both badly from me and well from his men.

That should make me patient.

Instead it makes the ache worse.

The camp changes when sickness enters its bones.

Owned men become less invisible because owners need them too badly to pretend they are furniture. A collared cook tells a marked guard which water to boil and the guard obeys before remembering to scowl. A fevered owner calls for his mother in a tongue Yara does not translate. Two rowers steal salt from a barrel and share it with a boy who can barely stand.

Cruelty remains.

So does need.

That is the trouble with people. They do not stay one shape long enough for hatred to rest.

The boy with the salt is called Nerin.

I learn the sound because Yara says it when she thinks no watcher cares. He is younger than most of the collared men, old enough to carry buckets and young enough that sleep still changes his face into a child's. His collar has rubbed his neck raw. He hides the sore under a strip of cloth, but the cloth is never dry.

He sees me once.

Not clearly. I am only dark among roots, a place where eyes should not shine. Still, he pauses with his bucket hanging from both hands. His mouth opens. Not to shout. To breathe.

Then Yara says his name.

Nerin.

Soft. Warning. Kind.

He lowers his eyes and keeps walking.

The next day he is put to the fever tent.

That is not mercy either. The healthy free men will not carry fevered bodies if a collared boy can do it. Nerin brings water, changes cloths, holds bowls while men vomit black bile and curse him for seeing them weak. By dusk his own steps have begun to drift sideways. By night he is coughing into his shoulder, trying to hide the sound.

I want to hate him for being human.

Then I watch him tear his own salt cloth in half and tie the cleaner strip around the wrist of a sick rower who cannot stop shaking.

The lie speaks again.

Save him.

As if I can step from the green and lay hands on fever like a king lays hands on law. As if my wrong blood is answer enough for every wound men make and every sickness the island gives back.

I stay hidden.

That is the truth beneath every noble thought.

Nerin dies on the third morning.

Not loudly. The camp has no space left for every death to have its own sound. He folds beside the water rack with two empty gourds against his chest, and for a while men step around him because he is still useful as an obstacle to be avoided. Yara reaches him first. She kneels, touches his throat, and goes still.

The rower with the salt takes one step toward her.

A guard lifts a hook.

The rower stops.

Drevan comes when the camp has gathered enough to matter. He looks down at the boy. Fever has hollowed the captain's cheeks, and sweat runs under the edge of his black collar. For one breath I think he will order the body carried away as he orders crates moved, because that is the cheaper shape.

Instead he crouches.

Slowly. Carefully. As if the bend costs him more than he wants anyone to know.

He takes the two empty gourds from Nerin's arms and gives one to Yara, one to the rower who stopped when the hook lifted. Then he says words I only half know.

Work.

Water.

Remember.

He does not say free.

He does not say sorry.

He gives the dead boy a use, because use is the only holiness this camp understands.

Then he points to the fever tent and orders the rowers back to work.

Yara stays kneeling one breath too long.

Only one.

A guard notices. Drevan notices the guard noticing. Before the guard can speak, Drevan says Yara's camp name and tells her to carry the body to the shore.

It sounds like punishment.

It is also permission to grieve where the owners will not watch closely.

That is the knife of him. The part that cuts cleanest. He can make mercy wear the face of command so thoroughly that no one can tell which edge drew blood.

Yara lifts Nerin under the arms. He is light. Too light for a boy who carried water for men twice his size. The rower takes his feet when Drevan looks away at exactly the right time.

I follow through the trees until they reach the shore.

There, with surf worrying the mud, Yara presses Nerin's forehead once and says his name without the camp listening.

Nerin.

Then the rower and she push him into the tide.

No stone. No morning-facing shelf. No old hands keeping account. Only water taking a body because the camp cannot spare wood.

I think of the dead shelf at home and all the small bodies Kala keeps turned toward dawn.

The down road loses its shine.

Only for a while.

One evening I watch Yara carry broth to the caged Skell with the broken ribs. She waits until the nearest guard turns away, then slips a strip of clean cloth under the bars. The Skell snaps at her hand. She does not flinch until after the jaws close on air.

Then she laughs once, very quietly.

Not because it is funny.

Because the alternative is crying in front of men who would count the tears.

The Skell takes the cloth.

I see Marrow in that. The leavings helping leavings. The weak making small roads under the eyes of the strong.

The sight should send me back to the high stones with a cleaner heart.

Instead it knots the roads tighter.

If I wait, the green may take Yara. It may take the Mure who still lifts its head when she clicks softly through the bars. It may take the collared men who look like me and not like me. It may take the words before I learn enough to ask what I am.

This is the lie the hunger tells best:

Go down now, or lose the answer forever.

There is another lie under that one, softer and more dangerous.

Save them.

Not only Yara. Not only the caged Skell. The fevered boy who carries water. The rower with salt hidden in his palm. The owner calling for his mother in a voice made young by fear.

As if wanting to save a person means I know how.

As if the hand that keeps me from choosing home can call itself mercy and be clean.

At the high stones, the tribe senses the split.

Voss-Marr does more than sense it. He uses it.

He starts with distance. His young strong ones sleep farther from my fire. Then sound. A low note when I pass, almost laughter, almost warning. Then signs left where I will see them: rotten meat near the lip, a strip of human cloth taken from some old wreck and laid across a stone, a child's cage made from twigs and tied with vine.

Marrow finds that one before I do.

He crushes it under his foot until the vines are pulp.

Voss-Marr watches from across the shelf with his head tilted. Not challenging. Not yet. He wants me angry in the wrong direction. He wants the tribe to see me spend strength on shame.

I refuse him that.

But the center of the tribe leans away.

The discarded ones still hold near me. Torn-Ear. Stub. Grist. The scarred female. The ones who know that belonging can always be made conditional by those born nearer the center of the fire. They stay because I made room for them and because my fall would teach the tribe their room was never real.

Marrow stays for simpler reasons.

He is mine and I am his, though neither of us has a word clean enough for it.

Kala reads the worst of it.

One night, after I return with camp smoke in my hair and salt on my tongue, she takes me away from the others. Not far. Just to the edge of the dead shelf, where the small bodies face morning and the old stones remember every name the tribe can give them.

She sits heavily. The movement costs her. I see it now because I have learned her strength well enough to read its limits. Old wounds in the hip. Stiffness in the left hand when rain comes. Breath that catches after a hard climb and then smooths before anyone else notices.

She puts her hand on my chest.

The mark.

Then my face.

Then she points down toward the coast.

Where?

I know the sound. I know the shape of the question. I even have pieces of answer now.

Camp.

Cage.

Girl.

Mark.

Captain.

Hand.

Need.

None of them fit into the pain in her eye.

I put my hand over hers.

No answer comes.

The silence hurts her. I see it land. That is worse than any blow because it is pain I make while trying not to make pain.

She draws me against her side anyway.

Forgiveness can cut deeper than anger.

The seer comes after she leaves.
