He moves slowly across the dead shelf, staff tapping once, then twice, then once again. He does not touch the mark at first. He touches my forehead. My mouth. The hollow at my throat. Last, the breastbone.

Then he gives the meaning-sound.

Not for the mark.

For me.

I go still.

He points to the coast. Gives the danger-sound. Points to my chest. Then to the high stones. Then he lays one old finger against my heart and presses until it hurts.

Here.

No farther.

He is telling me the mark is a road. He is telling me the road goes toward a hand. He is telling me I am mistaking direction for destiny.

I know.

Still the down road pulls.

That is the war.

Not tribe against men. Not green against iron. Not wild against camp.

The war is inside the breastbone.

One hunger wants the home that saved me. One wants the human road that may explain me. One wants Kala's arm. One wants Yara's word for the mark. One wants Marrow's shoulder at my side. One wants Drevan's welcome to be false in a way I can master.

Both roads are real.

Both roads are mine.

That is what makes choosing feel like cutting off a living part.

Near dawn, I wake in Kala's arm with my name behind my teeth.

My name.

Still hidden.

Still whole.

Still useless if I never give it to anyone who can answer.

Marrow is awake on my other side. He watches me with the flat yellow eye and does not pretend sleep. The little twist of dried moss he gave me days ago is tied around his curled wrist now, a reminder returned to himself.

"I know," I whisper.

He does not soften.

Good.

Someone should refuse to make this easy.

Then he reaches over and steals the last strip of meat from my hand.

He chews it slowly while staring at me.

It is so rude, so perfectly Marrow, that a laugh nearly climbs out of the wound in my chest. I stop it before the sound wakes Kala, but he sees it in my face. His high shoulder drops a little.

Not forgiveness.

Enough to remind me that the world still has room for teeth shown without threat.

At first light, before the tribe fully wakes, I go down again.

Marrow follows.

Of course he does.

# Chapter 24: The Two Hungers at War

I tell myself I go down for answers.

The mark. Drevan Sool. Yara's words. The dead expedition. The men sickening in the camp. The old name she spoke when she saw the eye-and-thorn at my chest.

All true.

Truth can still be a lie if it hides the larger truth behind it.

I go down because I want to.

Because the food is warm.

Because Yara looks into the dark and knows a person is there.

Because human voices move through the camp like a river I was born beside and lost before I learned its name.

Because some part of me still believes a hand from my own kind might stay unclosed.

That part is a fool.

It is also me.

The camp is quiet when we reach the roots. Too quiet. No low argument near the water barrels. No owner's laugh. No caged Skell scraping at the wood. Even the swarms seem to hold farther back from the fires, black knots circling without landing.

Marrow stops before I do.

His head turns once toward camp, once toward me.

Trap.

I know.

I go lower in the mud.

Dusk thickens. The wet flats turn from bronze to gray. Behind us the high stones are hidden by green and slope and all the choices I have already failed to make.

No meat comes.

No water.

Yara comes instead.

She walks alone, which means she is not alone at all.

Her hands are empty. Her face is pale under the grime. She keeps her eyes lowered until she reaches the mud line. Then she looks straight into the roots where I lie and begins to speak.

Not the teaching voice.

Not the camp voice.

Something between warning and plea.

I know more words now. Enough to catch pieces.

Danger.

Mark.

You.

Captain.

Come.

That last one lands badly. Not command. Not exactly. A question with rope under it. A plea sharpened into a hook. Maybe she has been ordered to say it. Maybe she means it. Maybe both are true, and she has been given a knife with no clean handle.

Her hand, low in the mud, draws the closing fist again.

Then two lines.

Camp.

Green.

Between them, the eye-and-thorn, split by one finger.

I understand pieces. Drevan will come. The mark has made me too valuable to leave in the dark. If I stay hidden, he will stop feeding the line and start cutting toward it.

Yara's fingers move faster.

Men.

Nets.

Fire.

High.

I miss the next word and hate myself for missing it.

High what? High stones? High path? High watchers?

Behind me, Marrow makes a sound I have never heard from him.

Small. Hurt. Angry.

He knows this is the true bait.

So do I.

Yara looks into the roots and whispers another word.

Name?

The world narrows to the space behind my teeth.

My name waits there. Warm. Hidden. The one possession never taken because I never gave the world a chance. My mother's gift, carried through hold and green and fever and blood, through Kala's arms and Gull-Marr's lip, through every place that wanted to rename me as owned, beast, omen, cargo.

I could give it to her.

She is shaped to catch it. She could say it back. She could make it real in air, not just in the dark of my own mouth.

The need is so strong my lips part.

Marrow's hand closes around my ankle.

Not hard.

Enough.

I stay silent.

Yara sees the almost-answer. Her face folds inward, not with surprise. With grief, maybe. Or relief. She knows what it costs to keep a name out of reach. She nods once, as if I answered after all.

Then she does something beyond my understanding.

She touches her own chest and gives her true name.

Not the camp name I have heard owners use. Not the sound I made for her in my head. A smaller word. Older. Hers.

She gives it to the dark quickly, before fear can take it back.

The name is not beautiful because of its sound.

It is beautiful because it is hers and she is afraid when she gives it.

I have seen men throw meat to earn a step. I have seen Drevan lay bait and call it welcome. This is not that. Or it is, and it is also braver than that, and the two truths cut across each other until I cannot separate wound from gift.

Then she turns and walks toward camp.

The watchers move too late for anyone but me to see: two shapes in the cut brush, one near the water, one behind thornwood. A third farther back, patient enough not to shift until she passes.

Drevan has set her out and watched the line.

He did not catch me.

He learned where my silence lives.

That is almost as bad.

I leave the offering place untouched after she goes. There is nothing there. That was the point. The captain has changed the food again. Warm meat. Salt. Words. Name.

Now choice.

I go back to the high stones before full dark for the first time in many days.

The tribe is waiting.

Not gathered by accident. Gathered the way bodies gather before a challenge, before judgment, before a pressure everyone has felt finally takes shape. Kala stands near the dead shelf. The seer waits before my lip. Voss-Marr stands near the far fire with his young strong ones, his head high and his mouth almost gentle.

That gentleness is the danger.

He does not need to rage. I have brought him better weapons.

Camp smoke in my hair.

Human salt on my tongue.

The mark cold under my shirt.

The seer touches the eye-and-thorn on my chest. Points toward the coast. Points toward the dead shelf. Then lifts both hands and lets them fall open.

Meaning.

Danger.

Death.

No metaphor. No omen fog. A warning as plain as old hands can make it.

Voss-Marr gives a short hard sound from across the fire.

Some of the tribe answer.

I know enough Vorth now to hear the shape.

Let him go.

Human trouble.

Human king.

The words hit differently because I understand them. Ignorance is a kind of hide. Language takes it off.

The first answer comes from one of Voss-Marr's young strong ones, a broad male with a split brow and new scars laid proudly across his chest. He makes the sound for coast and spits into the ash. Another answers with ship. Another with iron. They are not only insulting me now. They are building a path the tribe can follow.

Coast brings ship.

Ship brings iron.

Iron brings dead.

Then the broad male points at the disturbed edge of the shelf where the youngest ones sleep and makes the sign for danger carried in fur.

Me.

The discarded ones bristle. Stub lumbers halfway to his feet with a little one tucked under each arm. Torn-Ear bares the broken side of his mouth. Grist makes a rasping sound deep in the chest, the sound old bark makes when fire has found it.

Voss-Marr lets them answer.

That is clever too.

If he speaks alone, it is jealousy. If the young strong ones speak and the center listens, it becomes concern. A tribe can forgive concern almost anything.

The scarred female steps forward from the edge of my fire.

She does not speak often. Her throat was torn long before I came, and many of her sounds scrape raw. But when she points to me, then to the Hesh skull kept above the challenge stones, then to the little ones under Stub's arms, everyone understands.

He saved.

Voss-Marr's eyes move to her.

Not cruelly.

Worse. Patiently.

He touches the Hesh scar on his own shoulder, where my trap saved him from dying under the apex's mouth. Then he touches the dead shelf. Then the coast.

Saved once.

Doomed now.

A murmur runs through the bodies around us. Not agreement. Not refusal. The awful middle where fear has enough room to grow roots.

I want to hate him cleanly.

I cannot.

He is using the truth.

Every trip down has pulled human attention closer. Every offering eaten has taught Drevan where to lay the next one. Every word Yara gave me has given the camp a road back into my wanting. Voss-Marr twists those truths toward his own hunger for the lip, but the truths remain sharp.

That is what makes him dangerous.

Not lies.

Truth without love.

Marrow lunges before I can move. He goes for Voss-Marr with teeth bared and crooked hand hooked like a claw. Two young males block him. He is small, but fury gives him speed. For a breath I see the whole shelf tilt toward violence.

"Marrow."

My voice stops him.

That hurts too.

He freezes with his teeth a handspan from a young male's arm. The whole tribe sees him obey me when every part of him wants blood.

Voss-Marr sees it best.

His almost-gentle look deepens.

He has found the clean place to put his anger. Not in a blow, where I can answer. Not in a challenge, where the tribe would remember the lip and the Hesh and the day Gull-Marr stepped aside. He puts it in the tribe's oldest fear.

Human trouble belongs with humans.

A king who leaves the fire leaves the right to warm his hands at it.

There is enough truth in both that the tribe can stand near them without shame.

Kala rises.

The shelf quiets.

She does not threaten Voss-Marr. She does not need to. She crosses to me, each step heavy with age and authority and the grief I have put into her bones. She touches the eye-and-thorn. Then she presses her hand over my hidden heart.

Her sound is low, torn with more than age.

Stay.

That word I know.

Stay.

The two roads stand open inside me.

Behind one: Kala, Marrow, the dead shelf, the tribe I won and am losing, the small ones sleeping under a safety I helped make.

Behind the other: Yara, the mark's name, human speech, the captain's closing hand, the answer that might finally tell me why my blood lives wrong.

The right choice is obvious.

So obvious it becomes useless.

I take Kala's hand and put it against my cheek.

For a moment I let myself be the fevered boy she carried from the mud. The kept one. The one who had not yet learned that home creates new ways to be afraid.

Her thumb moves once along the scar at my jaw.

I could stay.

The words open inside me with such force that I almost believe them.

Then I remember Yara's sign.

Men. Nets. Fire. High.

High.

High stones?

If Drevan has read the line all the way back to home, staying may only mean waiting for the hand to close around everyone instead of me.

That thought might be truth.

It might be the hunger wearing truth's face.

I cannot tell anymore.

I step back.

Kala makes no sound.

Marrow does.

It breaks out of him like something torn open.

I cannot look at him.

If I do, I will stay.

I go down from the high stones while the tribe watches. Not running. Not sneaking. A king walking away from his lip toward the one hand everyone can see closing.

I tell myself I am choosing before Drevan chooses for me.

Maybe that is true.

Maybe bait becomes choice if you walk into it with open eyes.

Or maybe that is what every trapped animal tells itself in the last breath before the noose pulls tight.

At the first bend, Marrow catches up.

Of course he does.

His face is wet. Rain, maybe.

Not rain.

He puts himself in front of me and bares his teeth.

No.

One small body against the whole down road.

I touch his crooked shoulder. He shakes under my hand.

"Stay," I tell him, using Kala's word.

He spits at my feet.

Then he strikes me.

Not hard enough to wound. Hard enough to turn my face. His curled hand catches my cheek with the awkward strength of something never made for gentleness and giving it anyway in the only shape it has left.

I let the sting sit there.

He points uphill.

Kala.

Home.

Then he points to me.

Fool.

That word he does not have, but his body speaks it perfectly.

I almost laugh. If I do, it will become something else, so I swallow it.

"I know," I say.

He hates that answer.

So do I.

For a long moment neither of us moves. The green breathes around us. Far below, the camp smoke rises through leaves. Above, home waits with its fires and its wounded silence.

Marrow steps aside.

Not because he agrees.

Because he knows I am already gone.

I leave him there and walk toward the coast with my name locked behind my teeth, the mark cold on my chest, and both hungers tearing at the same heart.

I have not gone twenty paces when Marrow begins following again.

I keep my face toward the coast.

This time I let him.

We walk without speaking.

After a while his shoulder brushes my leg once. Hard enough to be an accusation. Soft enough to be company.

That is Marrow's mercy.

It does not absolve.

It stays.

# Chapter 25: The Bait

I am halfway down the coast road when the high stones scream.

Not one voice.

Many.

Vorth voices breaking against metal. Young ones shrieking. A male's challenge cut short. Stone struck by iron. Nets snapping open with the wet slap of weighted rope.

For one breath I stand between roads.

Below: the camp, its fires, Yara's words, the mark's answer, the hand I thought I was choosing before it chose me.

Above: home, suddenly full of human sound.

Then I run uphill.

Marrow is behind me. Of course he is. I told him to stay and he obeyed only long enough to hate me for asking. His crooked gait cuts through the dark at my heel, faster than fear, faster than sense.

We leave the path before the first bend. The path will be watched. I take the rot gully, the old boar cut, the root ladder that tore my palms raw in my first season and now takes my weight like a remembered hand. Marrow follows every turn. Once he slips in black moss and catches himself with the curled hand, making no sound though I know it hurts.

The high stones scream again.

Then a low Vorth roar shudders through the trees.

Kala.

My speed breaks into something worse.

We reach the last shelf and crawl under leaning thornwood.

The home I left is gone.

The fire is scattered. Burning brands lie in three places, hissing where water has been thrown on them. The sleeping hides are kicked apart. The dead shelf is trampled. Gifts have been crushed under boots that do not know what a dead child means. Small bodies, once faced carefully toward morning, lie crooked now, their stones knocked aside.

That is the first wound.

Not the blood.

The disrespect.

Slavers stand on the high stones.

Eight above. More below the road. Two at the dead shelf. Three near the lip. Others hidden by smoke and broken brush. Nets hang open between them, stone-weighted and tarred dark. Hooked irons wait in their hands. One man holds a coil of rope threaded through a sliding neck loop. Another carries a jar of pitch with a burning rag stuffed in its mouth.

Fire if I run into green.

Nets if I break for stone.

Hooks for anything too strong to net cleanly.

Drevan did not come to kill the tribe.

He came to make the tribe into a snare.

Vorth bodies litter the shelf. Not many dead. He was careful there too. A young male pinned through the thigh by a hook. Torn-Ear bleeding from scalp and mouth, alive but senseless. Stub trapped under a net with two little ones clutched beneath him. The scarred female crouched over a child with her teeth bared while a slaver keeps a spear point just far enough from her throat to make the threat last.

Voss-Marr is nowhere on the open stone.

I mark the absence and hate that I mark it.

Then I see his sign.

Not him. The trace of him. A broken young slaver near the west run, throat opened by teeth too large for any knife. Blood dragged toward the old Voss-Marr path and then stopped. A Vorth handprint in ash beside it, broad and clean, pressed once into the stone.

He fought.

That truth does not absolve him.

It makes the rest harder.

He fought, and then he chose where to stop.

Kala is down.

She lies on the clean shelf among the dead, netted in stone-weighted mesh. One hook is sunk in her shoulder. Another in her haunch. Blood darkens the gray of her hide and runs between the morning stones. Her great hand claws weakly at the rock, not to free herself.

To reach the nearest small body and turn it right again.

My breath leaves.

They have not killed her.

That is how I know.

If they wanted Kala dead, she would be dead. Iron in the throat. Spear through the eye. Fire in the fur. They have left her living because pain is useful while someone who loves the wounded body remains free.

The slavers are not looking at Kala.

They are looking into the green.

At me.

I understand the trap whole.

Drevan did not wait below for my answer. He used the waiting itself. Yara at the mud line. The empty offering. The false choice. While I stood between hunger and home, men climbed another road. Not the one I used. A harder one, maybe shown by a map from the dead expedition, maybe found by patient scouting, maybe bought with sickness and bodies. He followed my absence back to what I could not leave.

He also used the tribe against itself.

The old paths are not all mine. Hunters know them. Rivals know them. Voss-Marr's young strong ones know cuts through the upper green I have never trusted because heavy bodies made them. Men could have found one by chance. Men could have found one with patience.

Or a frightened Vorth could have retreated along it and taught them where home had a weak rib.

There is no answer in me.

That is the cruelty of battle after it breaks open. The mind runs to blame because blame is a shape easier to hold than loss.

The slavers have not taken the shelf by courage.

They have taken it by work.

I see the work once my first terror stops blinding me. Small stakes hammered into cracks where feet might turn. Pitch daubed on the root curtain to close the north path with fire if the tribe bolts that way. Nets laid under ash so the first heavy body rushing to help another pulls rope around its own knees. Hooks set not for hearts, but joints. Shoulders. Hips. Thighs. Places where strength remains alive and becomes a handle.

The owner near the dead shelf wears a strip of blue cloth around his arm. So does the man by the lip. So does a third lower on the road. Signals, then. Not a mob. Crews. One for bait, one for fire, one for dragging bodies down to the boats if the catch changes.

Drevan has made a hunt from men who do not know the island.

He learned enough from watching me. From Yara's words. From dead expedition skins, perhaps. From every failed net and poisoned meat and night of waiting while fever ate his camp. He paid for his knowledge and has come to spend it all at once.

I hate the care in it.

Care should belong to hands that heal, not hands that bind.

I think of Voss-Marr then and hate myself for how quickly the thought comes.

He knows upper paths I never learned. His young strong ones move through the high green with the sure-footed pride of bodies born to those stones. He wants me gone. He wants the lip. He wants the tribe to remember the order that existed before a human climbed into it with tricks and wrong blood.

All true.

But wanting a door open is not the same as opening it.

Maybe he showed them nothing. Maybe he fought until the choice narrowed around him and he chose the tribe left behind over the king already walking down to men. Maybe I am making him into a simple enemy because simple enemies hurt less than broken brothers.

The shelf gives me no answer.

Only blood.

Then, through smoke, I see a shape move above the west run.

Voss-Marr.

Not in the open. Not hiding either. He is half behind a leaning stone, one young strong one bleeding against his side, another crouched low with a hooked iron caught through the meat of his forearm. Voss-Marr has gathered three of his own there, maybe four, a knot of strength pulled out of the snare before it closed.

He sees the shelf.

He sees Kala.

He sees Stub under the net with the little ones.

He sees me in the green.

For a breath all the world narrows to the line between his eyes and mine.

He can charge down.

He can take his knot of strong bodies into the men by the dead shelf. He may break the hook line. He may free Stub. He may reach Kala before the second net tightens. He may die in three breaths and leave no one strong enough to pull the young from fire if the pitch jars break.

Or he can hold the west run.

Keep a path open. Keep his own alive. Wait for the humans to spend their trap on me, because I am the prize, because I brought this hunger down from the coast, because a king who has walked away may still be used as shield by those who stayed.

I watch him choose.

His jaw works once.

He pulls the wounded young male back into shadow.

I hate him then.

I also understand him.

That understanding is a poison no blood can burn clean.

The young male beside him tries to go anyway.

I see the movement. A half-rise. A hand closing around a stone. Blood running from the hook wound in his forearm while his eyes fix on Stub and the little ones under the net. He is young enough to believe courage is a straight path. Young enough to think dying toward a loved body must be better than living away from it.

Voss-Marr catches him by the scruff and drags him back.

Not gently.

The young one twists, snarling, and for one breath Voss-Marr bares his teeth in his face with all the old authority he wanted from the lip. Stay, the snarl says. Hold. Live ugly if living is the only road left.

The young one obeys.

I hate Voss-Marr more for saving him.

It would be easier if his choice were only fear. Easier if he ran, if he crouched low and shook while others bled. Instead he keeps three wounded bodies from throwing themselves into hooks that would make their bravery useful to men. He preserves strength while Kala lies in a net. He lets me become the answer to a trap he did not make but does not refuse.

There are betrayals made of cowardice.

There are betrayals made of arithmetic.

The second kind lasts longer.

The bait is not meat now.

Not water.

Not Yara's voice.

Not the mark's answer.

The bait is home.

Marrow shifts beside me. His breath comes fast and wet. He has seen the net around Kala. The disturbed dead. The little ones under Stub. He makes no sound. That frightens me more than a cry would.

Below, a slaver turns too quickly toward our hiding place.

I catch Marrow before he lunges. My hand closes over his mouth, and for a breath he fights me with everything in him. Teeth against my palm. Nails in my wrist. I hold until he understands that one small sound will close every net.

Then I let him go.

He is shaking.

So am I.

Drevan steps onto the lip.

He should not be there. That is my place, and the wrongness of his boots on that stone strikes harder than if he had put them on my throat. He carries no raised weapon. A marked leather coat. A knife. A short spear in one hand, point down. The scar I gave him has gone dark along the cheek, fever shining at the edges. He looks ill.

He looks satisfied.

Yara stands behind him between two owners.

Her hands are bound.

That cuts through my anger in a new direction. She did not come freely. Or she did and paid for it. Or she told him too much and learned too late how he would spend it. I cannot read enough from here, and that uncertainty is one more barb in the trap.

Her eyes search the green.

When they pass over my shadow, something in her face breaks.

Not surprise.

Apology.

One of the owners grips her arm hard enough to whiten the fingers. She does not look at him. She looks at Kala.

There is horror on her face.

Not surprise.

That matters. Some part of her knew a net was coming. Some part did not know it would be laid through an old mother's shoulder and a child's turned stone. Drevan has used her road farther than she meant it to run.

Or that is what I need to believe so I can keep from hating her cleanly.

Marrow sees it too. His body goes tight with hatred, but not only for her. For all of it. For every road that brought men here.

Drevan speaks in the human tongue. I know enough pieces now.

Marked one.

Come.

No more hurt.

He touches the eye-and-thorn on his chest and then points to Kala.

The meaning needs no translation.

Come, or I spend what you love.

Kala hears enough.

Her head lifts from the stone. Blood runs from her mouth. Through net and pain, through age and iron, her eye finds the shadow where I hide.

She sees me.

Of course she does.

The sound she makes is not come.

It is go.

Not loud. Not a command for the tribe. A breath shaped for me.

Leave me.

Take Marrow.

Live.

That is what the sound means. All of it. The whole last gift she has strength to offer.

The clever part of me wakes cold and bright.

I could obey.

The route is there. Not the path. The thorn crack behind us, then the upper ridge, then fever fog and broken stone where men with nets cannot move quickly. Take Marrow. Free the little ones if a gap opens, or leave them if it does not. Let the green kill the camp slowly. Let Drevan have a wounded old Vorth and a trampled shelf and nothing else.

The thought is clean.

Useful.

True.

Kala's eye stays on me.

Still telling me to go.

That breaks the last clever part.

Some hooks you see and take anyway.

I touch Marrow's shoulder.

He looks at me once.

No, his whole face says.

I step out of the green.

Every slaver on the stone turns toward me.

Drevan smiles like a man hearing a lock open.

# Chapter 26: He Goes Anyway

I move before the nearest slaver can shout.

Low across the stone. Not at Drevan. That is what he wants. The captain stands on the lip like bait carved into a man, and every road to him is netted twice.

I go left.

The first slaver turns too slowly. I throw a stone into his knee with all the force the green taught my shoulder. Bone gives. He drops. I take his spear before his mouth has shaped pain.

For two breaths, the trap is only men.

And men can bleed.

I drive the spear through a thigh and rip it free. Hook the butt into another man's ankle and send him down hard enough to split his chin on stone. A third grabs for my hair. I bite the meat below his thumb until blood floods my mouth and he screams.

The taste opens the door.

Red heat lifts in me.

Not anger. Anger has a face. This has teeth.

It rises from under the breastbone, under hunger, under fear, old and eager. The hand on the neck. The hold. The brand-sound. Kala bleeding in a net. Every command ever laid on me burns clean into one desire.

Hurt them.

The world becomes simple, and that is the sweetness.

No roads. No names. No mark with old meanings hidden under skin. No Yara with warning in one hand and betrayal in the other. No Voss-Marr choosing shadow. No tribe watching me walk away. No child reaching for Kala's dead body because it has not learned that wanting cannot call back the gone.

Only weight.

Grip.

Throat.

Heat under skin.

The Red takes all the tangled pain and makes one clean line from my hand to the next body. It feels like mercy because thought hurts more. It feels like truth because it asks no question I cannot answer with teeth.

That is why it frightens me.

Not because it makes me monstrous.

Because it makes monstrosity restful.

A slaver swings a hook at my head. I slip under it and drive my shoulder into his belly. His breath leaves in a wet grunt. The hook falls. I catch it before it hits stone and for one breath the iron feels made for my hand. Curved point. Good weight. A tool with no lie in it.

The Red loves it.

Hook meat. Pull. Open.

My arm begins to obey before the rest of me has chosen.

Then a small body flashes past my left.

Marrow goes at a slaver like a thrown knife.

Small. Crooked. Vicious. He slips under a hook, opens the man's calf with a shard of stone, then climbs him while he falls. His curled hand rakes at the face. His teeth find an ear. The man shrieks and thrashes, and for a moment the whole trap forgets the runt.

Only for a moment.

Drevan counted him.

The first net drops.

Not on me. On Marrow.

Weighted rope snaps around his high shoulder and drags him sideways. He twists, almost out. Of course he almost gets out. He has spent his whole life escaping hands larger than his body. But another man was waiting where an escape would go. That is the cruelty of a well-laid trap. It does not catch where you are. It catches where you are clever enough to be next.

Marrow screams then.

Not fear.

Fury.

Even caught, he tries to make the net regret him.

He bites a knot. Kicks a hand. Twists toward the smallest gap with a speed that would be funny if there were room in the world for laughter. One slaver yelps and jerks back missing skin from two fingers.

Good, I think wildly.

Bite him again.

I turn toward him, and the second net takes me from above.

Weighted edges wrap my shoulders. Tarred rope bites my mouth when I tear at it. I cut one strand with the stolen spear before the shaft is wrenched away. Bite another. The taste of tar and blood fills my mouth, and the hold rises inside me, black and whole.

The Red answers.

It does not speak in words.

It says more.

My wrist tears through the mesh. Skin opens. I feel none of it. A slaver leans close with a rope loop and I catch his forearm in both hands. For one breath I want to break him. Not to escape. Not to protect Kala. Not even to win.

To feel the warmth leave.

That is the first true shape of the Red.

Not strength.

Appetite.

I bite down before I choose to. Blood fills my mouth, hot and living. The slaver howls. Something in me howls back without sound.

More.

The word is not a word.

It is worse.

A hook catches the net and rips me backward before I can follow the hunger. The spear point meant for my thigh skids off stone and opens my calf instead. Another net comes from the side. Men shout. Boots hammer. Someone calls for the neck loop.

I hear Kala roar.

The sound shakes the high stones.

She rises under her net.

For one impossible moment, age and iron and blood mean nothing. Her great body heaves up from the clean shelf. Hooks tear wider through her shoulder and haunch. A slaver loses his grip and goes sprawling. Another backs away with the pure animal terror men feel when the world reminds them size can still matter.

"No," Drevan says.

I know that word now.

Not shouted. Ordered.

Three hooks drive into Kala at once.

Not the killing places.

The hurting ones.

They drag her down in pieces. Her hand claws at the stone, finds one of the small dead bodies knocked crooked, and turns it toward morning before the net tightens again.

The sight breaks something open in me.

The Red floods through.

I surge so hard the neck loop misses. The men holding the net stumble. One comes too near and I slam my forehead into his nose. Cartilage breaks. He falls back, blood everywhere, and the Red sings at the smell.

Marrow goes still.

Through the mesh around him, his yellow eye finds mine.

Not done, his body says.

Not like this.

That stillness reaches me where shouting cannot.

If I give the Red all of me, I may kill three men. Five. Maybe more before the ropes finish their work.

Then I will be empty when the hand closes.

I force one breath into the next.

The Red claws at my ribs.

I hold.

A rope loop drops over my throat.

This time it catches.

The more I fight, the more my own strength steals air. That is old craft. Hold craft. Let the captive strangle himself and call it restraint. My knees hit stone. Hands close on my wrists. Ankles. Hair. The back of my neck.

A palm presses me down.

The hold returns.

Not memory.

Return.

I scream into the rock.

The sound tears loose before I can bank it. Men flinch. Good. Let them have that much. Let them hear what they have put their hands on.

Then I bite the scream off.

Drevan steps into view at last.

He walks carefully. The drag in his leg is worse. Fever shines on his skin, and the scar from eye to jaw has gone swollen and wet at the edges. My island is killing him. Every breath costs him more than it did yesterday.

He is smiling anyway.

He has paid in men, blood, sickness, and time, and now his hand is on the catch.

Yara stands behind him, still bound. Her face is white under the grime. I cannot tell whether she is looking at me or at the blood on Kala's net.

Maybe there is no difference.

Drevan crouches beside me.

He studies my torn wrist already pulling itself toward closure. My eyes clear of drug. The blood on my mouth that is not all mine. The mark against my chest.

His expression changes.

Not surprise.

Confirmation.

That is worse. He did not come hoping for a monster from the green. He came expecting one, and I have just made him right.

The men nearest him see it too.

One makes a sign against evil. Another whispers a prayer with his lips barely moving. The young owner I saw scrape spilled water from mud stares at my wrist as if the healing cut has opened under his own skin. Even with blood on his mouth from Drevan's public lesson, even with shame still fresh enough to guide his eyes downward, he looks hungry now.

Afraid.

Hungry.

Men can be both.

Fear wants distance. Greed wants a closer look.

Drevan knows which desire will win if he gives it room. He shifts his body between me and them, subtle as a door closing. The catch is his. The story is his. The price will be his if fever does not take him first.

Yara sees that movement.

I see her see it.

For the first time, her fear changes shape. Until now she feared what Drevan would do with me. Now she understands what other men may do if Drevan falls before selling his prize. A wounded captain can still hold one road. A dead captain opens many.

That is the camp's ugliest truth.

Cruel order may be all that keeps worse hunger in line.

I hate the thought.

It remains true enough to hurt.

One of the owners says a word I know from the cage line.

Share.

Drevan looks at him.

No anger. No raised voice. Only the flat attention that made a free man kneel in mud.

The owner lowers his eyes.

There. Another chain. Not on my wrists. On theirs. The captain owns by making everyone need the shape of him, even men who hate his hand.

If I kill him, the camp will not become clean.

It will become many hands reaching at once.

That is how he wins twice.

If I break, he owns a body.

If I rage, he owns a story.

Men above pits and men behind clean tables will pay more for a story than a body. The wild boy. The blood-mark. The island creature that eats poison and smiles with red teeth. Drevan sees the shape of it already, and I see him seeing.

The Red wants to answer by making the story true enough to drown him in.

I keep my mouth shut.

He says the brand-word.

Mine.

The old sound. The hand-sound. The hold-sound.

It falls off my face.

My name stays behind my teeth.

Kala's eye finds me across the stone. Still open. Still keeping.

Marrow is under his net, breathing hard, counting.

I hold all of it.

The Red. The name. The shame. The captain's word lying useless on the stone between us.

Drevan thinks the quiet means breaking.

Let him.

# Chapter 27: Property Again

They kill Kala after the net has done its work.

That is the cruelty.

Not the killing.

The order of it.

She was never the cargo. She was the scream tied to a stake. Once my wrists are bound, once the loop at my throat has taught my body the cost of fighting, once Marrow is netted and hooked and dragged where I can see him, Drevan gives a small nod.

No speech.

No anger.

Only permission.

A slaver drives iron behind Kala's shoulder into the heart.

Her body shudders.

The high stones go silent.

Even the men seem to feel some law has been crossed, though they crossed it by order and will collect pay for the crossing.

Kala's eye stays on me.

No fear for herself. No accusation. Only the keeping look she gave me in fever, when she folded her arm over a burning boy and taught his skin that not every hand meant ownership.

I pull against the ropes once.

The neck loop tightens.

Kala sees that too.

Her eye softens.

Still teaching.

Even now.

Do not spend yourself on the hand.

The light leaves her while she is still looking at me.

I make no sound.

The scream has gone somewhere lower than sound.

For a breath I cannot find her in the body.

The shape is there. The gray-silver muzzle. The torn shoulder. The hand still curved toward the small dead she had turned right. But the keeping has gone out of it, and my mind refuses that emptiness the way a hand refuses fire after it has already burned.

Kala is not in the body.

Kala is in the arm that folded over me. In the sound that made stillness gentle. In Marrow's throat when he gives back the word she gave us. In the way my own hand moves, even bound, trying to reach the crooked small body beside me.

That is worse.

Death does not take all at once.

It leaves pieces everywhere and makes the living carry them.

Men prefer noise. Noise tells them where the wound is. It gives them proof of power. They wait for me to break open, to thrash, to give Drevan the victory he arranged with nets and bait and an old mother's blood.

I give them nothing.

That frightens some of them.

Good.

Let them wonder where the scream went.

Drevan turns my face toward the light with hard fingers. He studies the split in my lip drawing itself closed, the cuts under rope already knitting, the eyes that should be fevered by now and are not. The island's sickness shines on his own skin. Sweat beads at his temple. His wound smells sweet with rot.

He knows I am not only an animal.

He does not know what I am.

That ignorance is why he crossed the water.

He says the brand-word again. Low. Certain.

Mine.

The word cannot reach where my name lives.

My name remains behind the broken teeth, whole and hidden. My mother's gift. The last unowned room.

The slavers begin their leaving.

They move fast now. Not because they fear the tribe. Because they fear the island. The raid has cost them more than they wanted. Two men bleed badly. One coughs red into his hand and hides it until Drevan sees. Another cannot stop scratching at the black swelling under his jaw. The green is still taking payment.

They drag the wounded Vorth away from the path and cut the nets only when they are sure hooks and spear points can hold them. They do not take the young. Not mercy. Calculation. Too much trouble. Too little price. The caged road has its own arithmetic, and I am learning to hate numbers.

Marrow is hauled beside me.

He fights until a hook kisses the skin under his jaw. Then he goes still.

Counting.

Not broken.

His yellow eye finds mine once. There is blood on his teeth. One ear hangs torn. His high shoulder is wrapped wrong by the net, and pain trembles through him in small waves he refuses to show the men.

I give him the still-sound with no voice.

Be held.

He sees it.

The slavers pull us down from the high stones.

Home slides past in pieces.

The fire scattered. The dead shelf disturbed. Kala gray and vast among the morning stones, one hand still near the small body she turned right before the end. Stub crouched over the young ones, shaking. Torn-Ear on one knee, blood running down his neck. The scarred female watching me with a grief too hard for tears.

Smell follows me.

Burnt hide. Wet ash. Blood opened in old fur. The bitter sap from crushed thornwood. Kala's milk-warm scent under all of it, already leaving the air and becoming memory before I am ready to let memory hold her.

Sound follows too.

Not wailing. The Vorth do not spend grief the way humans do. Their mourning is a low work, almost below hearing, a chest-note passed from one body to the next until stone seems to carry it. Stub makes it first, broken and rough. Torn-Ear answers through blood. The scarred female lowers her head and gives the note back. Even one of Voss-Marr's young strong ones takes it up from the west run, half-hidden, ashamed to be heard and unable to stop.

Kala had carried half of them at one time or another. Fed them. Struck them. Turned their faces from foolishness with one heavy hand. She was not soft. She was not gentle in the way human songs might want mothers gentle. She was weather and law and warm dark, and now the tribe has to breathe around the space where she stood.

The note reaches me.

It enters under the neck loop, under rope, under rage.

For one breath I am back against her side in rain while her hand covers my head and the island pounds around us. Kept. That was the word her body taught before my mouth knew any tongue well enough to say it.

Then a slaver jerks the pole, and the note tears away.

A little one crawls from beneath Stub's arm.

Too young to understand trap. Old enough to understand absence. It takes three steps toward Kala, hands open, making the small asking sound young ones make when food is near or sleep has gone wrong.

Stub catches it with one shaking arm and pulls it back against his chest.

The little one fights him.

Not hard. Not with fury. With need.

That is almost what breaks me.

Not iron. Not Drevan's word. Not the neck loop.

A child wanting the dead to answer.

My bound hands move before thought. The pole between them jerks. A slaver curses and drives the butt of his spear into my ribs. Air leaves me. The neck loop tightens until black flecks swim across the world.

I make myself still.

Kala's last lesson again.

Do not spend yourself on the hand.

The little one keeps reaching until Stub turns its face against his chest and hides the sight.

Good, I think.

Hide it.

Then I hate the thought, because Kala never hid the world from me. She only stood between me and the part of it that wanted ownership.

There is no one standing now.

Voss-Marr stands above the path.

He did not come down.

No net holds him. No hook. No rope. He watches from the edge of the shelf where shadow cuts his face in two.

Blood darkens his muzzle.

Not his own, or not only his own. One of Drevan's men lies behind him in the broken brush, face turned the wrong way. Voss-Marr has killed today. He has paid something. That matters because easy cowardice is too clean a story, and none of us have earned clean stories.

His chest heaves.

His hands are open.

He could still come.

The path between us is not empty, but it exists. Two slavers with hooks. One net-man limping. Drevan's attention on me, on his cargo, on the fever eating his own blood. Voss-Marr is strong enough to make that path ugly. Maybe not enough to win. Enough to choose.

For one breath, our eyes meet.

I see the choice happen.

Not from lack of courage.

From arithmetic.

If he comes, he may die and the broken tribe loses another strong body. If he stays, I am taken, the human trouble leaves, and the lip becomes his by absence. He can name that duty. He can name it survival. He can name it the tribe needing him.

All those names will have truth in them.

None will be the whole truth.

The lip is his now.

Not the way he wanted. Not won. Not given by challenge. Left behind by human hands and cowardice and a choice he will spend the rest of his life trying to rename.

I see it settle into his shoulders as I pass.

Shame.

Relief.

Hate.

Opportunity.

All mixed. All dangerous.

He will remember that he let them take me.

I will remember too.

Marrow remembers with his whole body.

When he sees Voss-Marr above the path, he stops fighting the hook for one breath. Just one. His yellow eye narrows. His teeth show, not in challenge but in promise.

Voss-Marr sees that too.

Good.

Let the island keep more than one count.

At the lower bend, Drevan pauses.

Not for breath, though he needs it. To make me look back.

I do.

The high stones stand above the green, smoke-stained and wounded. For one season of my life, they were the whole shape of safety. I know now that safety can be entered by men with enough patience, enough rope, and enough understanding of what a boy loves.

That lesson has a price.

Kala paid most of it.

At the shore, the boats wait in brown water.

The camp is already half-broken behind them. Cages loaded. Crates dragged through mud. Sick men helped by men nearly as sick. The black banner comes down last. When it falls, the eye-and-thorn folds in on itself like a closing lid.

They throw Marrow into a cage with a Mure and a Skell, as if he is refuse.

He lands badly. The Skell snaps. Marrow snaps back harder. Even bound, even hurting, he makes enough violence in the small space that the Skell retreats and the Mure lifts its head to look at him with dull surprise.

That almost makes me smile.

Almost.

They lash me separately because I am worth different rope.

Wrists. Ankles. Throat. A pole through the bindings so two men can hold distance between their skin and mine. Drevan has learned. He will not let me be near one throat, one hand, one careless knife.

He also will not let the others price me.

When one owner argues over the lost men and points at the cage where Marrow crouches, Drevan answers softly. I miss most of the words. I catch enough.

My catch.

My risk.

My sale.

The owner spits into the mud but steps back.

Drevan is sick, wounded, and hated by half the camp.

He is still captain.

That, too, is a cage with teeth.

Yara sits bound near the bow.

Our eyes meet once.

She sees Kala's blood on me. She sees the hand on my neck. She sees Marrow in the cage. She knows some part of this came through the road she opened, and the knowledge wounds her.

I cannot tell whether she betrayed me.

I know Drevan used her.

Both truths can live in the same skin. I have learned that lesson too well.

She mouths a word.

Not my name. I did not give her that.

Her own.

The true one she gave the dark.

I understand then. She is telling me she is still there. Under the camp name. Under the rope. Under whatever Drevan made of her.

I give her the only answer I have.

I stay whole.

The boat pushes off.

Mud sucks at the hull, then lets go.

The island begins to recede.

The mangrove mouth. The bone-spit. The seaward stack. The ridges above the fever fog. The high stones hidden by green but still there, holding Kala's body and the dead faced toward morning.

Home becomes shape.

Then haze.

Then a line my eyes cannot hold.

They have made me property again.

They have not made me theirs.

# Chapter 28: The Hold

I know the dark before they lower me into it.

The hold has been waiting under the world.

Tar. Wet wood. Bodies kept too close. The floor lifting and falling with water under it. A hole made by men to turn breath into property.

They lower me by pole and rope because Drevan will not risk hands near my teeth. The hatch waits below, black and square. Marrow's cage is already there. I smell him before I see him: blood, Vorth musk, swamp mud dried into the fur, pain held tight enough to become another scent.

He makes no sound when they drag him across the planks.

Neither do I.

The hatch takes us.

Dark closes.

The scream rises.

I keep it behind my teeth.

The first hold took my voice because I had no place to put it. This time I have places.

My name.

Kala's eye.

Marrow breathing beyond the bars.

Yara above, still alive.

The brand-words I will one day hand back.

I lay them out inside me like the Vorth laid their dead on clean stone.

Faced.

Ordered.

Kept.

That is how I survive the first night.

The body wants to return to the old shape: small, silent, owned. It remembers before I do. Shoulders folding. Breath thinning. Hands hiding. The old wisdom of captivity, where being noticed was danger and being forgotten was life.

I give it a different silence.

Not obedience.

Banked fire.

I count.

The roll of the floor. Long lift, short fall, shudder in the ribs of the boat. The scrape of rope against my wrists. The wet breath of the Mure in the cage. The Skell's claws ticking once every few moments, not random, not calm. The drip from a seam above my left ear. The coughs on deck. Three men sick. Then four. The drag in Drevan's gait when he crosses over the hatch.

Marrow's breath.

That count matters most.

In.

Hold.

Out.

Angry even sleeping.

There are other breaths too.

The Mure breathes like wet cloth pulled through teeth. Its body is folded badly inside the cage, long limbs tied with ropes that have rubbed pale grooves into scaled skin. In the dark its eyes make two dull moons. It was a bright water hunter once, I think. A creature of river bends and moonlit shallows. Here it has been made into a bundle that smells of old mud and fear.

The Skell breathes in clicks.

Not broken. Waiting. Even chained muzzle to tail, even ribs banded wrong under bruised hide, it keeps one claw against the cage floor and taps whenever the ship rolls. Tap, pause, tap. A measure. A map. A way to tell itself the world still has edges.

Below all that, under plank and cage and rope, human breath.

Not crew.

Cargo.

At first I mistake them for shadows packed near the far bulkhead. Then one coughs words, and another answers with a dry laugh, and I understand Drevan's catch was never only island bodies. Men have been stacked beneath nets there, wrists through a single chain, ankles crossed with rope. Some collared. Some not. A woman with cropped hair and one swollen eye. A boy no older than Nerin was, but harder in the mouth. An old man whose beard has been cut short with a dull knife.

They are not mine.

They are not free.

That should be simple.

It is not.

The woman sees my eyes shining in the dark and whispers a prayer.

The boy tells her to save breath.

The old man laughs once. "Breath is all they haven't priced yet," he says.

I know enough human now to catch the shape if not every edge. Priced. Breath. Save. He hears me listening and goes quiet.

Then he asks what I am.

No one answers him.

Marrow does.

He rattles his bars with one hard kick.

The old man laughs again, softer. "That one knows."

The woman begins to cry without sound. Tears have smell in the dark. Salt, shame, anger swallowed until it turns the stomach sour.

The boy beside her tells her to stop.

She says a name.

Not hers, I think. A child's. Or a husband's. Or a place. Names all sound like wounds in holds.

The boy says the name back badly, mocking at first. Then not mocking. Then he stops speaking.

I add their breaths to the count because I cannot keep them and can count them.

They threw him in as fodder and do not know they carried my right hand across the water.

Sometimes he wakes and forgets where he is.

Only for a breath. His body jerks against the bars, teeth finding iron before his eye opens. Then he remembers. The high shoulder rises. The curled hand folds tight against his chest. He looks for me in the dark and finds the shape of my breathing.

I make the still-sound.

He answers with a click of tooth on bar.

Not gratitude.

An insult, probably.

It steadies me more than gentleness would.

After a while, his cage shifts. A low Skell hiss. Marrow's answering snarl, small and immediate. A thud. Bars rattle. The Mure gives a wet click of protest.

"Still," I whisper in Vorth.

The dark takes the word.

Marrow hears anyway.

The cage goes quiet except for his breathing, which has changed shape.

Listening now.

Yara's voice comes through the planks sometimes, thin and careful. She is made to translate above deck. Even bound, she carries meaning for men who own her. I catch pieces when the wind drops and the water is kind.

Island.

Fever.

Valuable.

Shipment.

Buyer.

Pit.

The last word comes often.

Pit.

Its full shape is still closed to me. I know enough. Yara drew the circle in mud. Men around. Blood inside. Ownership turned into spectacle.

Men do not only want to possess.

They want witnesses.

The thought sits cold in the dark.

The cargo knows the word better.

When Yara's voice thins through the planks and pit comes with it, the old man stops laughing. The woman presses her forehead to her knees. The hard-mouthed boy spits toward the bilge and misses because the ship rolls.

"Not me," he says.

No one answers.

"Not me," he says again, louder, as if volume can shape law.

The old man says a place name. I catch only the first sound. Then he says another word.

Debts.

The woman with the swollen eye says husband.

The boy says sold.

They begin to tell each other how they were taken, each story pushed into the dark as if the dark might judge fairly. A farm note bought twice. A border tax unpaid. A brother accused of stealing grain. A guard who wanted a daughter and took the mother when the daughter ran. None of it sounds like raiding. All of it sounds like paper.

That is another horror men have made.

Rope can take a body.

Ink can take a life before rope ever touches skin.

The old man says the pit pays better for animals than debtors unless the debtor knows how to hold a blade. Then he asks if I know blades.

Marrow answers again with a snarl.

The old man goes quiet.

Smart.

There is order among the chained too.

Not law. Law belongs to those with keys. This is smaller and sadder: who gets the dry corner when the ship rolls, who takes the first cup if a guard lowers water, who is allowed to sleep against the bulkhead and who is kicked awake for leaning too close. Captivity makes ladders even in the dark.

The hard-mouthed boy thinks he should stand higher on that ladder because he still has anger enough to spend. He bares it at the woman when her crying shakes the chain. He bares it at the old man when the old man laughs. He bares it at me until Marrow's cage rattles and his courage remembers its size.

The old man has another kind of place. He knows words the others need. Port names. House marks. Which pit gates pay for blade-work and which pay for beasts. Which collars mean debt, which mean sentence, which mean nobody will ever ask where the body went. Knowledge makes a poor blanket, but the others still huddle near it.

The woman has nothing anyone calls power.

Then the ship pitches hard and the boy's head strikes wood. He curses, sharp and frightened. Blood opens above his brow. The woman reaches with bound hands and presses the torn hem of her sleeve against it before he can refuse.

"Hold still," she says.

He says he does not need her.

She says, "Bleed quieter, then."

The old man laughs. Not cruelly this time.

Even Marrow pauses at that.

The boy lets her press the cloth.

Small kindness has to disguise itself here. If it comes bare-faced, someone will sell it, punish it, or lean on it until it breaks. So it arrives as insult. Accident. Spilled broth. A foot nudging a cup. A woman telling a boy to bleed with better manners.

I count those too.

A slaver comes below with a lamp during the second night.

Light opens like a wound.

He is not one of the marked owners. Younger. Nervous. The lamp shakes in his hand no matter how hard he tries to stop it. He checks the ropes at my wrists, then my ankles, then the throat loop. His eyes linger on cuts that should remain open and do not. On the bruise fading at my jaw. On the blood dried around my mouth.

He says the brand-word.

Mine.

Not with Drevan's certainty. More like a charm against fear.

It falls away.

He does not like my quiet.

Good.

Let him think stillness means damage. Captors grow careless around captives they believe have folded inward.

His lamp passes to Marrow's cage.

There the fear leaves his face. He sees only a small broken Vorth with one high shoulder and a torn ear. Cargo of lesser price. Fodder for the pit. He does not see Marrow's eye following the key ring at his belt. Does not see the curled hand test one bent bar each time the boat rolls.

I see.

Marrow sees me seeing.

The young slaver sees none of it because fear has made him stupid in the way comfort makes men stupid. He has a lamp. A key. A deck above him. A name other men answer when he speaks.

So he believes the dark belongs to him.

The dark has never belonged to men.

The lamp goes back up.

Dark returns.

After him comes the smell of broth.

Not for us.

A collared cook lowers a pot by rope while a guard above holds the other end and complains about the stink. The cook is broad-shouldered, bald, with burns along both forearms and a collar rubbed shiny from old use. He ladles broth into wooden cups and passes them through the far bars to the human cargo first.

The guard above tells him to skip the woman.

The cook does not look up.

His hand slips. Broth spills across the floor near her knee. Not much. Enough. She bends, cups it, drinks from her palm before shame can stop hunger.

The guard curses.

The cook apologizes so flatly the apology has no life in it.

He comes near my ropes last.

His eyes avoid my face. They stop at my wrists instead. At cuts closing too quickly. His mouth tightens.

He does not offer broth. Orders, maybe. Fear, certainly. But when he steps past me, one heel nudges a broken cup within reach of Marrow's cage.

Marrow stares at it as if insulted by charity.

Then he hooks it with the curled hand and drags it in.

The cook sees and does not see.

That is how the owned survive around owners. They make blindness into a craft.

The old man thanks him.

The cook says nothing.

When he reaches the ladder, the guard hauls him up too quickly and the pot bangs against the hatch frame. Broth spills down in a hot stripe across the cook's hand. He makes no sound.

Above, the guard laughs once and then coughs until the laugh breaks apart.

Fever owns him too.

The hard-mouthed boy waits until the hatch closes before he whispers to the woman with the swollen eye.

"Eat next time."

She answers that there may not be a next time.

The boy says there is always a next time until there is not, and until then a person eats.

That sounds like Marrow would say it if he had human words and less pride.

In the cage, Marrow gnaws the broken cup clean and then shoves it toward the Mure with his foot, pretending he meant to kick refuse away.

The Mure drinks the last slick of broth from the crack.

Marrow sees me watching and bares his teeth.

Tell no one, his eye says.

In the dark, among chains and fever, I keep that too.

Above us, men keep dying.

The island came with them. Fever rides in their lungs. Rot climbs through their cuts. One body goes overboard before dawn. I hear the feet gather, the prayer one man starts and another tells him to swallow, the heave, the splash. Later another splash. No prayer that time.

The green is gone from sight, but it has not released them.

It has released me.

That is the question under every count.

Drevan comes to the hatch after the second splash.

He does not open it.

He stands above us for a long while, breathing through his mouth, the way men breathe when fever has filled the nose and left pride no clean path. A drop falls through the plank seam and lands on my cheek.

Sweat.

Or rain.

Or him.

I hope it is him. I hate myself for hoping, and then I keep hoping.

Why?

Why my blood clears what kills them. Why the mark makes old seers bow and slavers pay in bodies. Why the Red wakes when blood touches my mouth. Why warmth rose in the pit of me when Marrow looked through the net and told me not here.

No answer comes.

Only the count.

So I keep counting.

At some point Yara comes to the hatch.

Not down. Drevan will not allow that. Her shadow blocks the seam of light above, and her voice lowers through the crack between planks.

She says the true name she gave the dark.

Then another word.

Sorry.

I know that one now.

It is a poor word for a wound this large.

Still, it is hers to give.

I cannot answer without bringing men to the hatch. I press my forehead to the wet plank below her shadow and breathe once, loud enough that maybe she hears.

Maybe not.

Marrow makes a low sound from the cage.

Not warning.

Witness.

The boat rolls on.

The hold does what holds do. It narrows the world until the mind begins to believe the narrowness is truth. Plank. Rope. Breath. Dark. Pain. Count. Again.

I refuse the lesson.

The hand always opens.

It opened once and dropped me into the sea. The sea made a shore. The shore made a boy who could read mud, hunger, kings, and traps. The high stones made him more than prey. Kala made him kept. Marrow made him answered. Yara gave him roads of speech. Drevan gave him chains and thought chains were the end of learning.

The hand will open again.

When it does, I will not be the same boy it closed on.

# Chapter 29: The Pit Opens

The crossing ends in light and shouting.

They drag me from the hold half-blind. Day goes into my eyes like a spear. I smell the place before I see it: smoke, dung, iron, sweat, old blood. Not jungle. Not sea. A human stink, packed and permanent.

Land waits under my feet, but it is not ground yet.

Ground can be read.

This is noise.

Men crowd the docks. More men than I knew the world could hold in one place. Marked owners in leather. Collared carriers bent under cages. Boys running with water skins. Women selling skewers of meat beside gutters thick with flies. Guards with hook spears. Children perched on rails, pointing at the boats as if the dying green inside them is festival prize.

Humanity is not a shape.

It is a swarm with rules.

The rules show in small violences.

A guard cuffing a water boy without looking down. A woman counting coins before handing over meat gone green at one edge. A collared carrier stepping around a free man's shadow because even shadows have rank here. A child reaching toward a caged Skell and laughing when its muzzle chain snaps short.

No one stops the child.

That teaches me more than the walls.

The slavers have brought pieces of my island to market. A Mure drying under wet cloth. Two Skell chained muzzle to tail. A bird with knife-feet hooded in black. A cage full of small scaled creatures that throw themselves against the bars until their noses bleed. Men walk past and judge them by price, danger, novelty, meat.

One points at me and laughs.

Drevan strikes him with the butt of his spear so quickly the laugh becomes teeth on stone.

Not mercy.

Investment.

The wound in Drevan's face has gone gray at the edges. Fever shines around his eyes. Each step costs him now, though he hides it from everyone but me. My island is still eating him. He left the green, but the green did not leave his blood.

That pleases me.

Not enough.

They march us through the market toward a roar that grows larger with every step.

At first I think it is sea.

Then I understand.

Voices.

Many voices, lifted together, hungry in the same direction.

The pits lie beyond a ring of walls.

Holes cut into the earth, shored with stone, circled by timber stands and iron rails. Men crowd around them in tiers, roaring down at whatever bleeds below. Cages line the far side: Mure drying in the sun, Skell pacing until chains stop them, furred brutes from islands I have never seen, scaled shapes sleeping with eyes open. The slavers have carried wild places here piece by piece and taught men to clap while they die.

Behind the stands, scribes sit under striped shade and scratch marks onto tablets. Coin men weigh silver. Butchers wait near the drains. A healer in a clean apron checks a fighter's teeth the way a buyer checks a horse. Priests or judges or rich fools in red cloth sit above the main gate where the shade is best, faces painted against the stink.

This is not a den of hunger.

It is an order.

That is worse.

The order has levels.

At the bottom are the pit hands with scarred arms and quick feet, men and women who smell of old blood no washing can take from under the nails. They know when to lean away from a cage before the crowd knows anything has moved. They carry hooks, sand buckets, chain keys, buckets of sour water, poles forked at the end for throats and limbs. Their faces are bored until danger comes close. Then they wake like hunters.

Above them are the trainers.

They do not shout as much. They watch teeth. Limp. Breath. The way a beast favors one side after a turn. The way a captive grips a blade, or refuses one. One woman with silver rings through both ears looks at Marrow and frowns, not because she pities him, but because she can tell the pit has been handed a bad description. Her eyes move from his high shoulder to the ground under his feet to the cage latch he tested without seeming to move.

She sees more than the others.

I mark her.

Above the trainers are the coin men, the scribes, the buyers' servants with tablets and dyed cords showing which houses have paid for shade, which houses can demand a private viewing, which house owns a fighter's debt and which only owns a share in the winnings. They argue without raising voices. Soft hands. Sharp eyes. Men who will never drag a net but know how many nets a boy's blood can buy.

Above them sit the patrons.

Painted faces. Clean cloth. Perfumed wrists. Little knives worn for beauty, not use. Their laughter carries differently because no one is allowed to interrupt it. They have the best shade and the farthest distance from blood. That is a kind of power too: to make pain small enough to fit inside entertainment.

The pale-coated man belongs there but stands below, which means he enjoys letting others see him descend.

He is not only buyer.

He is theater.

When he moves, scribes look up. When he speaks, trainers stop pretending not to listen. Drevan straightens because the man has the smell of a gate, not a wall. A gate can open onto silver, pardon, ships, clean surgeons, safer contracts. A gate can also close.

The pale man sees that Drevan knows it.

That pleases him.

The hold was ownership hidden below deck.

The pit is ownership made public.

That is the first lesson of this new place.

They bring me to the largest pit.

The crowd changes when it sees me. Curiosity first. Then disappointment. I am smaller than rumor, maybe. Too boy-shaped. Too thin. Not enough claws. Not enough monster for the price Drevan's men have whispered through the market.

Drevan hears it.

His jaw tightens.

Good.

Let him need me impressive.

An older man in a pale coat meets him by the rail. Not a guard. Not a pit hand. Too clean for both. His fingers never touch Drevan's, but the captain straightens anyway.

Patron, I think.

Or buyer.

Or the mouth of one.

The man looks at me, at the mark, at Drevan's rotting face. He says a few words, calm as a knife laid flat.

Drevan's sickness vanishes from his posture.

Only from his posture.

He answers with numbers.

I know numbers now when I hear them, even if the values slide past me. Losses. Price. Proof. Risk. His voice stays steady, but the old man's eyes move once to the fever shine at his throat and do not move back.

Drevan is selling me before he has finished owning me.

The pale man has a scribe with him, a narrow woman with ink-stained fingers and no expression at all. She looks at me longer than the patron does. Not with hunger. With measurement.

Height.

Weight.

Wounds.

Mark.

Healing.

Her reed scratches across wax. I cannot read the signs, but I know the rhythm of accounting. A body made into lines. A life cut into columns small enough for another person to own without losing sleep.

Drevan points to his own face, then to the mark on my chest. He speaks of the island, I think. Of poison. Of fever. Of men lost. He opens one hand toward Marrow as if the small Vorth is proof of more value, not less.

The pale man glances at Marrow and nearly dismisses him.

The silver-ringed trainer, standing three paces back, clicks her tongue once.

Soft.

The pale man hears.

He looks again.

Marrow chooses that moment to bare his teeth at a pit hand and make a sound so foul in Vorth meaning that even without knowing the words the hand steps back.

The trainer smiles.

Not kindly.

Accurately.

The pale man says a number.

Drevan stiffens.

Too low.

Even I hear that.

He answers with another number and the word blood.

The crowd around us has begun to notice the bargain. Wagers travel faster than speech. A boy with red cords runs along the rail taking calls. Two women in yellow sleeves argue over odds while a priest-faced man with a jeweled cup points at me and makes a slicing motion across his own palm.

They are pricing not only whether I live.

They are pricing how I bleed.

The knowledge should freeze me.

Instead it clarifies.

In the camp, ownership hid behind need. Water. Fever. Order. Here, ownership wears bells.

It wants me to hear it coming.

He leans close before the gate.

"Mine," he says.

The brand-word has followed me across water, through dark, into sunlight, and still it cannot find purchase.

I look at him.

He mistakes that for understanding.

Then they cut the ropes at my ankles, loosen the neck loop just enough to keep me breathing, and shove me over the edge.

The fall is short.

I roll when I hit, shoulder and hip taking the blow. The floor is packed earth soaked black with old blood. It sticks to my palms. Sand has been thrown over the wettest places, but blood always teaches through cover.

The crowd roars.

For a moment the sound is too large to read. It comes down the walls in waves and makes the old dark in me flinch. Men above. Hole below. No path out. Every face wanting something from the body in the pit.

I put one hand on the ground.

Ground can be read.

Packed earth. Slope toward the center where blood drains. Gate left. Gate right. Iron ladder not for fighters. Three chains near the far wall, each with a quick-release hook. Loose sand near the western edge, bad footing. A broken tooth from some previous beast by my knee.

The world returns in pieces I can use.

Something moves at the far wall.

I turn, ready for teeth.

I see a high shoulder.

A curled hand.

Marrow.

They have thrown him in as fodder. The runt, the broken Vorth, a small ugly prize to make the valuable animal perform. The crowd sees a crippled island brute. Drevan sees pressure. The pit-master sees entertainment.

I see the right hand they carried across the water without understanding its worth.

Marrow comes toward me at his crooked run and stops an arm's length away.

Blood mats the fur at his neck. One eye is swollen nearly shut. The torn ear has dried black at the edge. He should look smaller here under all these human throats.

He does not.

He gives the stilling sound.

The one I gave him.

The one Kala gave me.

Low, rough, nearly ruined by his throat, but shaped right.

Be held.

Nothing comes.

Then something does.

The warmth that rises in me is not the Red.

The Red is teeth. The Red is appetite. The Red wants throats and heat leaving bodies. This is deeper. Older. It begins under the breastbone where grief has been howling since Kala died and moves outward through blood, not hot exactly, not power exactly.

Recognition.

A thread pulled tight between two bodies that should have been broken and are not.

For one breath I feel Marrow as if the air between us has become skin. His pain. His fury. His refusal. Not thoughts. Not words. A living answer.

Then it fades when I reach for it.

Of course it does.

No answer gives itself whole.

But it was there.

Marrow saw it too. I know because his eye widens, and for one breath the pit, the crowd, the chains, the dead island behind us, all fall away.

Above, the crowd begins to jeer.

They wanted us to tear at each other.

We stand too still.

A pit-master calls from the rim. Some words are closed to me. I know enough.

Fight.

Blood.

Begin.

Drevan stands behind the rail, fever-bright and angry. Yara is near him, still bound, guarded by a man with a split lip. Her eyes find mine through the bars.

She sees Marrow at my side.

Something like hope crosses her face before fear covers it.

Then the pale-coated man turns toward her.

She lowers her eyes at once.

He says her camp name. Drevan answers before she can. The guard with the split lip tightens his grip on her rope, not cruelly, exactly. Practiced. A reminder.

So she is cargo too.

Or witness.

Or both.

The road she opened has closed around her feet as well.

Then a gate opens.

Chains rattle. Men shout. A smell rolls into the pit: musk, rot, old straw, rage fed badly and kept too long in iron.

The first beast comes low through the gate.

Not from my island. Too much shoulder. No eyes I can find at first, only armored plates over the head and a mouth that opens sideways. Its claws are made for digging, but the pit has starved it into using them for killing. Scars cross its hide where other crowds have taught it pain.

Good.

Now the floor is ground again.

Ground can be read.

Marrow moves to my left without being told.

I take the right.

The crowd wanted a monster and a cripple to tear each other apart. Instead two wrong-made creatures stand shoulder to shoulder in the old blood, reading the charge.

Above, men shout because they do not understand what they are seeing.

Let them shout.

I count the pit wall. The gates. The chains. The loose sand. The quick-release hook. Drevan's bad leg. Yara's bound hands. The beast's blind head and digging claws. Marrow's breath. The brand-words stored like stones inside me. Kala's eye, faced and kept. My name.

My name.

Still hidden.

Not forever.

The beast charges.

I smile with blood in my mouth.

The hand opened.

Not enough to free me.

Enough to put Marrow at my side.

For now, that is a beginning.

The beast is fast in the first three strides and stupid in the fourth.

# Chapter 30: The Hand Opens

Not stupid in the mind. Its mind is closed to me. Stupid in the body, the way every body becomes stupid when pain and hunger have been used on it for too long. It expects the world to answer one way because men have made its world small enough to teach only that answer.

Charge.

Hit.

Tear.

Again.

I move right.

Marrow moves left.

The beast's blind head follows the larger motion, which is mine because men have trained it to hunt the body they throw first. Its digging claws strike packed earth where my leg was. Sand sprays. The side-mouth opens and shuts on empty air.

Marrow is already at the chain.

He has seen what I saw: quick-release hook, rusted pin, a line meant for men above to drag dead meat out when the crowd is done loving it. He leaps, misses, catches the chain with his curled hand, and bites the pin because both his hands are busy and Marrow has never believed a mouth should be saved for eating only.

The beast turns.

Too soon.

I throw the broken tooth.

It hits the armored head and does nothing except give the beast a simpler hatred. It comes for me. I let it. Three steps. Four. Loose sand under my heel. Gate to my left. Chain behind the beast now, Marrow hanging from it with all his crooked weight.

The pin gives.

The chain drops.

Not on the beast.

In front of it.

Its digging claws strike iron where earth should be. One claw catches through a link. Momentum does the rest. The beast's front half stops. The back half keeps coming. It folds into its own weight and hits the ground hard enough to shake old blood loose from the packed floor.

The crowd roars because falling looks like victory to those too high above the ground to read it.

It is not victory.

It is one breath.

I spend it.

I run up the beast's plated shoulder, using ridges for footholds, and drive both thumbs into the soft place behind the head shield where a breathing slit opens and closes. The beast screams. Its side-mouth snaps backward, impossible wide, teeth grazing my forearm.

Pain blooms.

The Red answers under it.

Heat. Teeth. The clean wish to push both hands deeper until the scream becomes silence and the crowd learns the sound a body makes when its monster chooses back.

No.

There is no time for appetite.

I hook one arm over the head plate and shout to Marrow.

"Left!"

The word is human and Vorth and neither. He understands anyway.

Marrow darts in, small under the beast's blind side, and kicks the caught claw free of the chain.

The beast surges up.

Exactly where I want it.

It lunges not at me now, but away from the pain in its breathing slit, toward the open gate it came from. I drop, roll under the side-mouth, and come up with the loose chain in both hands. Marrow takes the other end. Together we pull across the beast's rear legs as it drives for the gate.

Iron bites.

The beast trips again, half in, half out of the gate.

Above, men shout. Pit hands scramble with hooks, suddenly afraid of losing expensive blood into the passage. The crowd's roar changes flavor. Surprise. Delight. Anger. The sweet confusion of people who paid to watch a killing and have been given a problem instead.

Drevan grips the rail so hard his knuckles pale.

Yara is smiling.

Only a little.

Only before she can stop herself.

It is enough.

Marrow sees it and bares his teeth at her like a king receiving tribute.

I nearly laugh.

His chin lifts after, proud enough to be absurd with blood on his ear and old sand stuck to his cheek.

For one breath he is not fodder. Not runt. Not broken prize.

He is Marrow on the high stones after stealing fruit from a sleeping young male, daring the world to notice and punish him quickly enough.

The breath hurts because it is bright.

Then the pit hands drive hooks down and drag the beast clear of the gate before it can tear through. One hook catches my shoulder. I slash the rope with the broken tooth, not deep enough to free myself from the pit, deep enough to make the man above swear and drop the line.

The beast is not dead.

Neither are we.

That is the first victory this place understands poorly.

The crowd begins to chant.

Not my name. They do not have it.

Not Marrow's. They do not know his worth.

A pit-word. Prize. Wild boy. Blood-mark. I catch only pieces. It does not matter. The sound has changed. We were fodder and curiosity when the gate opened. Now the pit is reading us again.

Drevan reads fastest.

His fever-bright eyes move from my healing forearm to Marrow at my side to the chain on the ground.

He understands what the crowd has not.

I did not win by rage.

I made a smaller body matter. I made the ground answer. I made the pit's own tools turn against it.

That frightens him.

Not enough.

But some.

The wild boy is valuable. The monster is valuable. A creature that can turn the pit into a question is another kind of risk. I see him weigh it even through fever: sell quickly and lose the larger price, hold longer and maybe lose the hand.
